Chapter 39

Dynamic Query Structures

Orientation

A leaderboard service precomputes prefix sums so that any range total costs one subtraction. The first score correction arrives, and every stored prefix after that position is wrong. Rebuilding all of them on each correction turns a fast service into a slow one. This chapter teaches classes whose updates and queries share one object. Each lesson asks which summary the object keeps, which call repairs it, and how many calls the workload makes of each kind.

Prerequisites

You should know prefix sums, Fenwick and segment trees, ordered maps, intervals, binary lifting, union-find and shortest paths. Four ideas appear without a full teaching pass. The lessons introduce a modular inverse (the number that undoes a multiplication modulo a prime), lazy propagation (a node stores a pending change for its whole range and applies it to children only when a call needs them) and skip lists (sorted linked lists stacked in levels). They expect you to recall Kruskal's algorithm, which builds a minimum spanning forest by adding the cheapest edge that joins two components, and the Boyer-Moore majority vote, which finds a candidate that may hold more than half the votes. A mutation is a call that changes stored state. A query is a call that reads an answer from that state. The update-to-query ratio is how many mutations the workload makes for each query. Every exercise states that ratio and says whether the calls are known before the first one runs, because both facts decide the design.

What Each Lesson Protects

Each lesson names one fact that every public call must leave true.

  • Mutable Range Queries keeps every tree entry equal to the sum of the range it owns.
  • Interval Unions keeps stored ranges disjoint, ordered and written with one endpoint rule.
  • Ordered Structures keeps keys sorted and every higher-level node also present on level 0.
  • Deferred Transforms keeps pending operations composed in call order.
  • Query Planning keeps a summary that answers every query without rereading the original data.
  • Dynamic Graph Queries keeps every returned distance consistent with all accepted edges.
  • Ordered Interval State joins sorted search with coverage counting over very large coordinate ranges.
  • Preprocessed Query APIs joins ancestor, position and path indexes under a stated workload.

Reading Order

Every lesson opens with a class that passes small tests and then fails on a realistic workload. You predict the failure before the reasoning appears. Two traces follow the state call by call, then Java code and the limits of the idea. The four exercises climb from a basic build to a variation, an edge case and a recognition problem. A false friend is a design that looks like the pattern but breaks its precondition. Solutions check each class against a slow, obviously correct oracle on random inputs.

What You Can Do After This Chapter

You can pick a structure for a class from its call mix instead of from habit. You can state the endpoint rule of an interval API before writing a comparison. You can say when a precomputed index must be rebuilt and when an incremental repair is cheaper. The base trees belong to chapter 31, and the ordered sets belong to chapter 32.

Mutable Range Queries

ProblemSimple approachBottleneckKey ideaStateWalkthroughCodeWhen to use itExercises
Problem

Why Corrections Break Stored Totals

A metrics service keeps one counter per minute for the last 100,000 minutes. Analysts ask for the total between two minutes, and the service answers in constant time because it stores running totals. Then late events arrive. Each one corrects a single minute, and every running total after that minute is now wrong. The service rebuilds all running totals after each correction. With a thousand corrections per second, the rebuild work swamps the work of answering queries.

The stored totals are not buggy. They answer a question about data that never changes, and the data now changes between questions. This lesson asks how to keep range totals correct when single values change, without paying a cost proportional to the whole array for each change. It also asks which structure to choose when the question is not a sum.

Simple approach

Rebuilding Running Totals After Every Change

The direct design keeps an array of running totals, where total[k] is the sum of the first k values. A query subtracts two entries. A change rewrites the value and then recomputes every running total.

final class RebuildingSums {
    private final long[] values;
    private final long[] total;               // total[k] = values[0] + ... + values[k - 1]

    RebuildingSums(int[] start) {
        values = new long[start.length];
        total = new long[start.length + 1];
        for (int i = 0; i < start.length; i++) values[i] = start[i];
        rebuild();
    }

    private void rebuild() {
        for (int k = 0; k < values.length; k++) total[k + 1] = total[k] + values[k];
    }

    void update(int index, int value) {
        values[index] = value;
        rebuild();                             // touches every entry after the change
    }

    long sum(int from, int to) {               // half-open range [from, to)
        return total[to] - total[from];
    }
}

Queries are fast and correct. Every update pays for a full pass over the array.

Bottleneck

Updates Cost As Much As Rebuilding

Predict first: An array holds 100,000 values. The program performs 100,000 updates and 100,000 queries in mixed order. About how many additions does `RebuildingSums` execute?

About ten billion additions. Each of the 100,000 updates rebuilds 100,000 entries, and the queries add almost nothing. The data is small, but the update cost multiplies with the number of updates.

Count the work per call. A query executes one subtraction, which is O(1). An update executes n additions, which is O(n). A workload with u updates and q queries therefore costs u * n + q. When u is close to n, the total is O(n^2), and the cheap queries no longer matter.

The opposite design makes the same mistake in the other direction. Reading the values directly makes an update O(1) and a query O(n), because each query adds the whole range. Both designs put the full cost on one side. The update-to-query ratio is an input to the design, so a workload with many calls of both kinds needs a structure where neither call touches the whole array.

Key idea

Share The Cost Between Updates And Queries

Give Each Entry A Small Range

A Fenwick tree keeps an array of n partial sums, where each entry stores the total of a short block of positions that it covers. Number the entries from 1. Entry i covers the owned range of positions i - lowbit(i) + 1 through i, counted from 1. Here lowbit(i) is the largest power of two that divides i, which the expression i & -i computes. Entry 6 covers positions 5 and 6, entry 4 covers positions 1 through 4, and every odd entry covers only itself.

Walk Up To Update And Down To Query

A prefix query adds the entry at i, jumps to i - lowbit(i), and repeats until the index reaches 0. The owned ranges of the visited entries tile the first i positions with no gap and no overlap. Each jump clears the lowest set bit of the index, so the walk takes at most log2(n) + 1 steps.

An update must reach every entry whose owned range contains the changed position. Those entries are i, then i + lowbit(i), and so on until the index passes n. Each jump moves to the next larger entry that covers the position, and the walk again takes O(log n) steps.

Apply The Delta Instead Of The New Value

The update walk adds a delta to each entry on its path, where delta is the new value minus the old value stored at that position. The structure keeps a plain copy of the values so that it can compute the old value. Adding the new value itself would count the position twice. Setting a position to the value it already holds gives a delta of 0 and changes nothing.

Extend The Same Rule To Two Dimensions

A matrix uses one Fenwick walk nested inside another. The outer walk moves over rows and the inner walk moves over columns, and entry (r, c) covers a rectangle of cells. An update visits O(log m * log n) entries. A rectangle sum combines four prefix queries by inclusion and exclusion. The invariant stays the same: after every call, each entry equals the exact sum over the cells it owns.

The static prefix array p shows the four-term rule that the nested walk keeps under updates. It has one extra row and column of zeros, and p[r][c] sums the cells whose row is below r and whose column is below c.

// p has (m + 1) rows and (n + 1) columns, row 0 and column 0 stay zero
static void fill(long[][] p, int[][] a, int m, int n) {
    for (int r = 1; r <= m; r++)
        for (int c = 1; c <= n; c++)
            p[r][c] = a[r - 1][c - 1] + p[r - 1][c] + p[r][c - 1] - p[r - 1][c - 1];
}

// rectangle rows r1..r2-1, columns c1..c2-1: add, subtract twice, add back
static long rect(long[][] p, int r1, int c1, int r2, int c2) {
    return p[r2][c2] - p[r1][c2] - p[r2][c1] + p[r1][c1];
}

Take the 3 by 3 matrix with rows 1 2 3, 4 5 6 and 7 8 9, and ask for the 2 by 2 block in the lower right corner, so r1 = 1, c1 = 1, r2 = 3 and c2 = 3. The whole matrix sums to 45, the top row to 6, the left column to 12 and the top-left cell to 1. The block sum is 45 - 6 - 12 + 1 = 28, which equals 5 + 6 + 8 + 9. The subtraction removes the top row and the left column, and the top-left cell was removed twice, so it is added back once.

State

Fix The Meaning Of Each Index

Decide the meaning of every index and range before writing a comparison.

  • Position p is a zero-based index into the caller's array, with 0 <= p < n.
  • Entry i is a one-based index into the tree, and i = p + 1 maps a position to its first entry.
  • Prefix end is exclusive, so prefix(end) sums positions 0 through end - 1.
  • Range is half-open [from, to) inside the structure, and sum(from, to) = prefix(to) - prefix(from).
  • Values are long in the tree, because many int values add up past 2^31.

A closed range [left, right] from a caller converts once, at the boundary, to [left, right + 1).

Walkthrough

Update One Position And Query A Range

Take the values [3, 1, 4, 1, 5, 9]. In one-based terms, entries 1 to 6 own the positions 1, 1 to 2, 3, 1 to 4, 5 and 5 to 6. Their stored sums are 3, 4, 4, 9, 5 and 14.

The first trace sets zero-based position 2 from 4 to 10. The delta is 6. The walk starts at entry 3, adds 6 and jumps to entry 4, because 3 + lowbit(3) is 4. Entry 4 gains 6 and becomes 15. The next jump reaches entry 8, which lies past the last entry, so the walk stops. Entries 1, 2, 5 and 6 do not cover position 2 and stay unchanged.

The second trace queries the half-open range [1, 5). It computes prefix(5) - prefix(1). The prefix at 5 adds entry 5, jumps to entry 4, adds it and jumps to entry 0. The prefix at 1 reads entry 1 only. The results are 20 and 3, so the range sum is 17, which equals 1 + 10 + 1 + 5. Each walk touches at most three entries in an array of six.

Code

Implement The Tree With Update And Sum

final class MutableSums {
    private final int n;
    private final long[] tree;       // tree[i] sums the owned range of entry i, one-based
    private final long[] values;     // current value at each zero-based position

    MutableSums(int n) {
        this.n = n;
        tree = new long[n + 1];
        values = new long[n];
    }

    void set(int position, long value) {
        long delta = value - values[position];       // change from the stored value
        values[position] = value;
        for (int i = position + 1; i <= n; i += i & -i) tree[i] += delta;
    }

    private long prefix(int end) {                   // sum of positions [0, end)
        long sum = 0;
        for (int i = end; i > 0; i -= i & -i) sum += tree[i];
        return sum;
    }

    long sum(int from, int to) {                     // half-open range [from, to)
        return prefix(to) - prefix(from);
    }
}

The delta is a long, so subtracting two int-sized values cannot wrap. The tree starts with zeros, which matches an all-zero array. A caller with starting values calls set once per position, for a total of O(n log n) setup time.

When to use it

Check The Workload And The Operation

Match The Structure To The Calls

Reach for this structure when updates and queries interleave and both are frequent. If all updates come before all queries, one prefix array built once is simpler and faster. If calls are known in advance, sorting them offline can also remove the need for a dynamic structure. Each of those choices depends on the update-to-query ratio, so state it before choosing.

Check The Operation Has An Inverse

The invariant of the tree is that each entry equals the exact sum over its owned range after every update. The delta update preserves it only because addition can be undone by subtraction. A range minimum has no such inverse, because removing a value from a stored minimum does not reveal the next smallest one. Treating the Fenwick tree as a general range structure is a false friend. For minimum, maximum and greatest common divisor with arbitrary assignment, use a segment tree that recomputes each node from its two children.

Keep Endpoints And Types Consistent

Convert every caller range to half-open once, at the public method. Store sums as long. A mix of closed and half-open comparisons inside the tree produces off-by-one totals that pass small tests and fail near the array ends.

Exercises

Exercises

Basic

Mutable Range Sum

LeetCode 307

Problem Specification. Design a class over an integer array nums with two operations. update(index, val) assigns val to nums[index]. sumRange(left, right) returns the sum of nums[left] through nums[right], where both endpoints are included. Calls interleave in any order and arrive online, so no later call is known in advance. The update and query counts are about equal.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • nums.length satisfies 1 <= n <= 3 * 10^4.
  • Values are int with -100 <= nums[i], val <= 100.
  • Indices satisfy 0 <= index < n and 0 <= left <= right < n.
  • Calls number at most 3 * 10^4 in total.
  • Return value of sumRange is a long.

Example 1. Input nums = [3, 1, 4, 1, 5, 9] with sumRange(1, 3), update(2, 10), sumRange(1, 3), output 6 and then 12.

Example 2. Input nums = [7] with update(0, -2), sumRange(0, 0), output -2.

Prerequisites. Fenwick tree walks from this lesson.

What This Exercise Changes. Static running totals become a tree of owned ranges, so both calls cost O(log n).

Hint

Which value must the update add to every covering entry: the new value or the difference from the old one? Which index does a closed right endpoint become in a half-open prefix?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The class keeps a Fenwick tree of long sums and a plain copy of the current values.

An update computes the difference between the new value and the stored value, and then adds that difference to every entry whose owned range contains the position. The walk starts at the one-based entry index + 1 and adds the lowest set bit each time. A sum query converts the closed range [left, right] to the half-open range [left, right + 1). It then subtracts one prefix walk from another, where each prefix walk clears the lowest set bit of its index.

The invariant is that every tree entry equals the sum of the copy over the positions it owns, after each call. The update keeps it because only the changed position differs, and the difference reaches exactly the entries that own that position.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(log n) per call, because each walk changes the index by its lowest set bit and visits at most about log2(n) + 1 entries.
  • Space is O(n), because the tree and the value copy each hold one long per position.
import java.util.Random;

public final class MutableRangeSum {
    static final class NumArray {
        private final int n;            // number of positions
        private final long[] tree;      // tree[i] sums the owned range of entry i, one-based
        private final long[] values;    // current value at each zero-based position

        /**
         * Builds the tree from the starting values.
         * Time: O(n log n), because each position is added by one update walk.
         * Space: O(n), because the tree and the copy hold n longs each.
         * Invariant: every tree entry equals the sum of its owned positions.
         */
        NumArray(int[] nums) {
            // Remember the size so every walk knows where to stop.
            n = nums.length;
            // One extra slot because entries are one-based.
            tree = new long[n + 1];
            // The copy lets update compute the old value in O(1).
            values = new long[n];
            // Insert each starting value as an update from zero.
            for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) update(i, nums[i]);
        }

        /**
         * Assigns val to nums[index].
         * Time: O(log n), because the walk moves up by the lowest set bit each step.
         * Space: O(1), because only a few locals are used.
         * Invariant: the difference reaches exactly the entries that own the position.
         */
        void update(int index, int val) {
            // The difference from the old value is what the covering entries must gain.
            long delta = (long) val - values[index];
            // Store the new value so the next update computes its difference correctly.
            values[index] = val;
            // Walk from the one-based entry upward while the entry exists; each step adds the lowest set bit.
            for (int i = index + 1; i <= n; i += i & -i) {
                // Every visited entry owns the position, so it gains the difference.
                tree[i] += delta;
            }
        }

        // Sums positions [0, end) by clearing the lowest set bit of the entry each step; O(log n).
        private long prefix(int end) {
            // Accumulator is a long because sums can exceed the int range.
            long sum = 0;
            // Stop at entry 0; the visited owned ranges tile [0, end) exactly.
            for (int i = end; i > 0; i -= i & -i) {
                // Add the whole owned range of this entry in one read.
                sum += tree[i];
            }
            // Return the prefix total.
            return sum;
        }

        /**
         * Returns the sum of nums[left..right], both included.
         * Time: O(log n), because it runs two prefix walks.
         * Space: O(1), because only locals are used.
         * Invariant: the closed range converts once to the half-open range [left, right + 1).
         */
        long sumRange(int left, int right) {
            // The right endpoint is closed, so the exclusive prefix end is right + 1.
            return prefix(right + 1) - prefix(left);
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Check Example 1: query, update, query again.
        NumArray a = new NumArray(new int[] {3, 1, 4, 1, 5, 9});
        if (a.sumRange(1, 3) != 6) throw new AssertionError("example 1 before");
        a.update(2, 10);
        if (a.sumRange(1, 3) != 12) throw new AssertionError("example 1 after");
        // Check Example 2: a single element with a negative assignment.
        NumArray b = new NumArray(new int[] {7});
        b.update(0, -2);
        if (b.sumRange(0, 0) != -2) throw new AssertionError("example 2");
        // Compare with a brute-force array on random interleaved calls.
        Random rnd = new Random(307);
        for (int round = 0; round < 300; round++) {
            int n = 1 + rnd.nextInt(40);
            int[] ref = new int[n];
            for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) ref[i] = rnd.nextInt(201) - 100;
            NumArray s = new NumArray(ref.clone());
            for (int op = 0; op < 120; op++) {
                if (rnd.nextBoolean()) {
                    int idx = rnd.nextInt(n);
                    int v = rnd.nextInt(201) - 100;
                    ref[idx] = v;
                    s.update(idx, v);
                } else {
                    int l = rnd.nextInt(n);
                    int r = l + rnd.nextInt(n - l);
                    long want = 0;
                    for (int i = l; i <= r; i++) want += ref[i];
                    if (s.sumRange(l, r) != want) throw new AssertionError("random mismatch");
                }
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Variation

Mutable Matrix Region Sum

LeetCode 308

Problem Specification. Design a class over an integer matrix. update(row, col, val) assigns val to one cell. sumRegion(row1, col1, row2, col2) returns the sum of all cells with row1 <= r <= row2 and col1 <= c <= col2, where all four bounds are included. Calls arrive online. Queries outnumber updates by about four to one.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Dimensions satisfy 1 <= m, n <= 200 for m rows and n columns.
  • Values are int with -1000 <= value <= 1000.
  • Indices are in range, with row1 <= row2 and col1 <= col2.
  • Calls number at most 5000 in total.
  • Return value of sumRegion is a long.

Example 1. Input [[1,2,3],[4,5,6],[7,8,9]] with sumRegion(0,0,1,1), update(1,1,0), sumRegion(0,0,1,1), sumRegion(1,1,2,2), output 12, 7 and 23.

Example 2. Input [[5]] with update(0,0,-5), sumRegion(0,0,0,0), output -5.

Prerequisites. The mutable range sum exercise above.

What This Exercise Changes. Ownership extends from intervals to rectangles, so each call costs O(log m * log n).

Hint

Nest the one-dimensional walk. How many prefix rectangles combine into one region, and which signs do they carry?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The class nests a column walk inside a row walk over a table of long entries.

Entry (r, c) owns the rectangle whose rows are owned by r in the row dimension and whose columns are owned by c in the column dimension. An update computes the difference from the stored cell and adds it to every entry reached by the nested upward walks. A prefix query sums every entry reached by the nested downward walks and returns the total of the rectangle from the origin to an exclusive corner.

A region with closed bounds converts to exclusive corners (row2 + 1, col2 + 1) and (row1, col1). Inclusion and exclusion then give four prefix terms, where the overlap is added back once. The invariant is that each entry equals the cell sum over the rectangle it owns after every call.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(log m * log n) per call, because each of the two nested walks visits at most about log2 of its dimension plus one entries.
  • Space is O(m * n), because the table and the cell copy each hold one long per cell.
import java.util.Random;

public final class MutableMatrixSum {
    static final class NumMatrix {
        private final int rows;         // number of rows
        private final int cols;         // number of columns
        private final long[][] tree;    // tree[r][c] sums the rectangle owned by entry (r, c), one-based
        private final long[][] cells;   // current value of each zero-based cell

        /**
         * Builds the table from the starting matrix.
         * Time: O(m * n * log m * log n), because each cell is inserted by one update.
         * Space: O(m * n), because the table and the copy hold one long per cell.
         * Invariant: every entry equals the cell sum over its owned rectangle.
         */
        NumMatrix(int[][] matrix) {
            // Store the dimensions so the walks know their limits.
            rows = matrix.length;
            cols = matrix[0].length;
            // Extra row and column because entries are one-based.
            tree = new long[rows + 1][cols + 1];
            // The copy gives the old value of a cell in O(1).
            cells = new long[rows][cols];
            // Insert every starting cell as an update from zero.
            for (int r = 0; r < rows; r++)
                for (int c = 0; c < cols; c++) update(r, c, matrix[r][c]);
        }

        /**
         * Assigns val to one cell.
         * Time: O(log m * log n), because the outer and inner walks each move up by the lowest set bit.
         * Space: O(1), because only locals are used.
         * Invariant: the difference reaches exactly the entries whose rectangles contain the cell.
         */
        void update(int row, int col, int val) {
            // Difference from the stored value; adding val directly would count the cell twice.
            long delta = (long) val - cells[row][col];
            // Store the new value for future differences.
            cells[row][col] = val;
            // Outer walk over row entries that own this row.
            for (int i = row + 1; i <= rows; i += i & -i) {
                // Inner walk over column entries that own this column.
                for (int j = col + 1; j <= cols; j += j & -j) {
                    // Entry (i, j) owns the cell, so it gains the difference.
                    tree[i][j] += delta;
                }
            }
        }

        // Sums the rectangle of rows [0, endRow) and columns [0, endCol); O(log m * log n).
        private long prefix(int endRow, int endCol) {
            // Accumulator is a long because region sums can pass the int range.
            long sum = 0;
            // Outer walk clears the lowest set bit of the row entry each step.
            for (int i = endRow; i > 0; i -= i & -i) {
                // Inner walk does the same for the column entry.
                for (int j = endCol; j > 0; j -= j & -j) {
                    // The owned rectangles of the visited entries tile the prefix rectangle.
                    sum += tree[i][j];
                }
            }
            // Return the rectangle total.
            return sum;
        }

        /**
         * Returns the sum of all cells in the closed rectangle.
         * Time: O(log m * log n), because it runs four prefix walks.
         * Space: O(1), because only locals are used.
         * Invariant: closed bounds convert once to exclusive corners.
         */
        long sumRegion(int row1, int col1, int row2, int col2) {
            // Inclusion and exclusion: add the big corner, remove the two strips, add the overlap back.
            return prefix(row2 + 1, col2 + 1) - prefix(row1, col2 + 1)
                 - prefix(row2 + 1, col1) + prefix(row1, col1);
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Check Example 1: query, update, then two queries.
        NumMatrix m = new NumMatrix(new int[][] {{1, 2, 3}, {4, 5, 6}, {7, 8, 9}});
        if (m.sumRegion(0, 0, 1, 1) != 12) throw new AssertionError("example 1 before");
        m.update(1, 1, 0);
        if (m.sumRegion(0, 0, 1, 1) != 7) throw new AssertionError("example 1 after");
        if (m.sumRegion(1, 1, 2, 2) != 23) throw new AssertionError("example 1 lower right");
        // Check Example 2: a single cell.
        NumMatrix one = new NumMatrix(new int[][] {{5}});
        one.update(0, 0, -5);
        if (one.sumRegion(0, 0, 0, 0) != -5) throw new AssertionError("example 2");
        // Compare with a brute-force matrix on random interleaved calls.
        Random rnd = new Random(308);
        for (int round = 0; round < 200; round++) {
            int h = 1 + rnd.nextInt(8), w = 1 + rnd.nextInt(8);
            int[][] ref = new int[h][w];
            for (int[] row : ref) for (int c = 0; c < w; c++) row[c] = rnd.nextInt(2001) - 1000;
            int[][] copy = new int[h][];
            for (int r = 0; r < h; r++) copy[r] = ref[r].clone();
            NumMatrix s = new NumMatrix(copy);
            for (int op = 0; op < 80; op++) {
                if (rnd.nextInt(5) == 0) {
                    int r = rnd.nextInt(h), c = rnd.nextInt(w), v = rnd.nextInt(2001) - 1000;
                    ref[r][c] = v;
                    s.update(r, c, v);
                } else {
                    int r1 = rnd.nextInt(h), r2 = r1 + rnd.nextInt(h - r1);
                    int c1 = rnd.nextInt(w), c2 = c1 + rnd.nextInt(w - c1);
                    long want = 0;
                    for (int r = r1; r <= r2; r++) for (int c = c1; c <= c2; c++) want += ref[r][c];
                    if (s.sumRegion(r1, c1, r2, c2) != want) throw new AssertionError("random mismatch");
                }
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Edge Cases

Repeated Assignment

Author exercise

Problem Specification. Design a ledger of n slots that all start at 0. set(slot, value) assigns value to a slot, and a slot may be assigned many times, including with the value it already holds. total(from, to) returns the sum of the slots in the half-open range [from, to). The structure must apply the difference from the previous value on each assignment. Calls arrive online, with about three updates per query.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • n satisfies 1 <= n <= 10^5.
  • Values are int over the full range, so sums need long.
  • Range satisfies 0 <= from <= to <= n, and from == to returns 0.
  • Calls number at most 2 * 10^5 in total.

Example 1. Input n = 4 with set(1,5), set(1,5), set(1,2), total(0,4), output 2, because each assignment replaces the old value.

Example 2. Input n = 2 with set(0, 2000000000), set(1, 2000000000), total(0,2), output 4000000000, which exceeds the int range.

Prerequisites. The two exercises above.

What This Exercise Changes. The same slot changes many times, so each update must be a difference from the stored value and not a fresh addition.

Hint

What does the second set(1,5) add to the tree? What happens if the code adds value directly instead of the difference?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The ledger keeps a Fenwick tree and a copy of the current slot values, and each assignment adds only the difference from the copy.

The first set(1,5) adds 5. The second set(1,5) computes a difference of 0 and changes nothing. The later set(1,2) adds -3, so the total becomes 2. An implementation that adds value directly would report 12 for Example 1, because it counts the slot three times.

The difference between two int values can need 33 bits, so the code subtracts in long. Sums use long as well. The query takes the half-open range [from, to), so from == to yields 0 without a special case. The invariant is that the copy always equals the last assigned value of each slot, and each tree entry equals the sum of the copy over its owned range.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(log n) per call, because each update and each prefix walk visits at most about log2(n) + 1 entries.
  • Space is O(n), because the tree and the copy each hold one long per slot.
import java.util.Random;

public final class RepeatedAssignment {
    static final class Ledger {
        private final int n;            // number of slots
        private final long[] tree;      // tree[i] sums the owned range of entry i, one-based
        private final long[] current;   // last assigned value of each slot

        /**
         * Creates a ledger of n slots, all zero.
         * Time: O(n), because the arrays are allocated and zero-filled.
         * Space: O(n), because the tree and the copy hold n longs each.
         * Invariant: the empty tree matches the all-zero copy.
         */
        Ledger(int n) {
            // Keep the size for the walk limits.
            this.n = n;
            // Extra slot because entries are one-based.
            tree = new long[n + 1];
            // Zero-filled copy: every slot starts at 0.
            current = new long[n];
        }

        /**
         * Assigns value to a slot, as many times as the caller likes.
         * Time: O(log n), because the walk moves up by the lowest set bit.
         * Space: O(1), because only locals are used.
         * Invariant: the tree changes by value minus the last assigned value, never by value itself.
         */
        void set(int slot, int value) {
            // Subtract in long: the difference of two ints can need 33 bits.
            long delta = (long) value - current[slot];
            // Record the assignment so the next set sees this value as the old one.
            current[slot] = value;
            // Walk upward through every entry that owns the slot; a delta of 0 adds nothing.
            for (int i = slot + 1; i <= n; i += i & -i) {
                // Each owning entry gains only the difference.
                tree[i] += delta;
            }
        }

        // Sums slots [0, end) by clearing the lowest set bit each step; O(log n).
        private long prefix(int end) {
            // Long accumulator, since four billion does not fit in an int.
            long sum = 0;
            // Stop at entry 0.
            for (int i = end; i > 0; i -= i & -i) {
                // Add the whole owned range of the entry.
                sum += tree[i];
            }
            // Return the prefix total.
            return sum;
        }

        /**
         * Returns the sum of slots in the half-open range [from, to).
         * Time: O(log n), because it runs two prefix walks.
         * Space: O(1), because only locals are used.
         * Invariant: an empty range gives prefix(from) - prefix(from) = 0.
         */
        long total(int from, int to) {
            // Half-open endpoints need no adjustment.
            return prefix(to) - prefix(from);
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Check Example 1: repeated and changed assignments to one slot.
        Ledger a = new Ledger(4);
        a.set(1, 5);
        a.set(1, 5);
        if (a.total(0, 4) != 5) throw new AssertionError("repeat of the same value adds nothing");
        a.set(1, 2);
        if (a.total(0, 4) != 2) throw new AssertionError("example 1");
        // Show that adding the value directly would give 12, not 2.
        if (5 + 5 + 2 != 12) throw new AssertionError("direct addition counts the slot three times");
        // Check Example 2: the total passes the int range.
        Ledger b = new Ledger(2);
        b.set(0, 2_000_000_000);
        b.set(1, 2_000_000_000);
        if (b.total(0, 2) != 4_000_000_000L) throw new AssertionError("example 2");
        // Check that the int-sized difference of the extreme values overflows int but not long.
        if ((Integer.MAX_VALUE - Integer.MIN_VALUE) != -1) throw new AssertionError("int subtraction wraps");
        Ledger c = new Ledger(1);
        c.set(0, Integer.MIN_VALUE);
        c.set(0, Integer.MAX_VALUE);
        if (c.total(0, 1) != Integer.MAX_VALUE) throw new AssertionError("extreme reassignment");
        // Check that an empty range returns 0.
        if (a.total(2, 2) != 0) throw new AssertionError("empty range");
        // Compare with a brute-force array on random calls with many repeated slots.
        Random rnd = new Random(2024);
        for (int round = 0; round < 300; round++) {
            int n = 1 + rnd.nextInt(12);
            long[] ref = new long[n];
            Ledger s = new Ledger(n);
            for (int op = 0; op < 150; op++) {
                if (rnd.nextInt(3) != 0) {
                    int slot = rnd.nextInt(Math.min(n, 3));
                    int v = rnd.nextBoolean() ? rnd.nextInt(5) : rnd.nextInt();
                    ref[slot] = v;
                    s.set(slot, v);
                } else {
                    int from = rnd.nextInt(n + 1), to = from + rnd.nextInt(n + 1 - from);
                    long want = 0;
                    for (int i = from; i < to; i++) want += ref[i];
                    if (s.total(from, to) != want) throw new AssertionError("random mismatch");
                }
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Pattern Recognition

Mutable Range Minimum

Author exercise

Problem Specification. Design a class over an integer array with set(index, value) and min(from, to), which returns the smallest value in the half-open range [from, to). Calls arrive online, with updates and queries about equal. Choose between a Fenwick tree and a segment tree, implement the choice, and show on a concrete input why the other choice returns a wrong answer after a value increases.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • n satisfies 1 <= n <= 10^5.
  • Values are int over the full range, and comparisons never subtract.
  • Range satisfies 0 <= from < to <= n, so a range is never empty.
  • Calls number at most 2 * 10^5 in total.

Example 1. Input [5, 2, 8, 6] with min(0,4), set(1, 9), min(0,4), output 2 and then 5.

Example 2. Input [4] with set(0, -3), min(0,1), output -3.

Prerequisites. All three exercises above.

What This Exercise Changes. The operation has no inverse, so ownership stays but each node is recomputed from its children and not patched by a delta.

Hint

Can a stored minimum be corrected by subtracting the old value? Which node must change when a leaf rises?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The choice is a segment tree, because a Fenwick tree cannot undo a stored minimum.

A Fenwick entry for addition is patched by a difference, since subtracting the old value restores the sum without it. A stored minimum has no such repair. When a leaf that equals the stored minimum rises, the entry needs the next smallest value in its owned range, and the entry does not contain it. A Fenwick variant that only lowers entries with min therefore keeps stale small values. The harness shows this on [5, 2, 8, 6], where it still reports 2 after position 1 changes to 9.

The segment tree stores the minimum of each node over a block of leaves. An update writes the leaf and then recomputes each ancestor as the minimum of its two children, so a rise propagates correctly. A query on [from, to) combines the O(log n) nodes that tile the range. The invariant is that each node equals the minimum of its leaves after every call.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(log n) per call, because an update recomputes one node per level and a query visits at most two nodes per level.
  • Space is O(n), because the tree holds 2n int values.
import java.util.Random;

public final class MutableRangeMinimum {
    static final class MinTree {
        private final int n;            // number of leaves
        private final int[] node;       // node[n + i] is leaf i; node[p] = min(node[2p], node[2p + 1])

        /**
         * Builds the tree bottom-up from the starting values.
         * Time: O(n), because each internal node is computed once.
         * Space: O(n), because 2n ints are stored.
         * Invariant: every internal node equals the minimum of its two children.
         */
        MinTree(int[] values) {
            // Remember the leaf count.
            n = values.length;
            // Leaves occupy the upper half; internal nodes occupy the lower half.
            node = new int[2 * n];
            // Copy the values into the leaf positions.
            System.arraycopy(values, 0, node, n, n);
            // Fill internal nodes from the last to the first so children exist before parents.
            for (int p = n - 1; p >= 1; p--) node[p] = Math.min(node[2 * p], node[2 * p + 1]);
        }

        /**
         * Assigns value to one leaf and recomputes its ancestors.
         * Time: O(log n), because the walk halves the node index each step.
         * Space: O(1), because only locals are used.
         * Invariant: every ancestor is recomputed from its children, so a rise is never hidden.
         */
        void set(int index, int value) {
            // Start at the leaf position.
            int p = index + n;
            // Write the new value.
            node[p] = value;
            // Move to the parent until the root; each parent is rebuilt from both children.
            while (p > 1) {
                // Parent index is p / 2.
                p >>= 1;
                // Recompute rather than patch, since min has no inverse.
                node[p] = Math.min(node[2 * p], node[2 * p + 1]);
            }
        }

        /**
         * Returns the minimum over the half-open range [from, to).
         * Time: O(log n), because each level contributes at most two nodes.
         * Space: O(1), because only locals are used.
         * Invariant: the visited nodes tile [from, to) exactly.
         */
        int min(int from, int to) {
            // Identity of min, so the first real value replaces it.
            int best = Integer.MAX_VALUE;
            // Shift both ends to leaf positions; the loop ends when the ends meet.
            for (int l = from + n, r = to + n; l < r; l >>= 1, r >>= 1) {
                // An odd left end is a right child, so take it alone and move right.
                if ((l & 1) == 1) best = Math.min(best, node[l++]);
                // An odd right end excludes its right sibling, so take the node before it.
                if ((r & 1) == 1) best = Math.min(best, node[--r]);
            }
            // Return the range minimum.
            return best;
        }
    }

    // A Fenwick tree that only lowers entries; shown to fail once a value rises.
    static final class BrokenFenwickMin {
        private final int n;
        private final int[] tree;
        BrokenFenwickMin(int[] values) {
            // Initialize every entry as the identity of min.
            n = values.length;
            tree = new int[n + 1];
            java.util.Arrays.fill(tree, Integer.MAX_VALUE);
            for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) set(i, values[i]);
        }
        void set(int index, int value) {
            // Lower each covering entry; nothing can raise an entry again.
            for (int i = index + 1; i <= n; i += i & -i) tree[i] = Math.min(tree[i], value);
        }
        int prefixMin(int end) {
            // Minimum of positions [0, end) by the usual downward walk.
            int best = Integer.MAX_VALUE;
            for (int i = end; i > 0; i -= i & -i) best = Math.min(best, tree[i]);
            return best;
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Check Example 1: the minimum rises after the smallest value is replaced.
        MinTree t = new MinTree(new int[] {5, 2, 8, 6});
        if (t.min(0, 4) != 2) throw new AssertionError("example 1 before");
        t.set(1, 9);
        if (t.min(0, 4) != 5) throw new AssertionError("example 1 after");
        // Check Example 2: a single leaf.
        MinTree one = new MinTree(new int[] {4});
        one.set(0, -3);
        if (one.min(0, 1) != -3) throw new AssertionError("example 2");
        // Show that the lowering-only Fenwick variant keeps the stale minimum 2.
        BrokenFenwickMin bad = new BrokenFenwickMin(new int[] {5, 2, 8, 6});
        bad.set(1, 9);
        if (bad.prefixMin(4) != 2) throw new AssertionError("the Fenwick variant keeps the stale minimum");
        // Compare with a brute-force scan on random interleaved calls, including extreme values.
        Random rnd = new Random(2025);
        for (int round = 0; round < 300; round++) {
            int n = 1 + rnd.nextInt(30);
            int[] ref = new int[n];
            for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) ref[i] = rnd.nextInt();
            MinTree s = new MinTree(ref.clone());
            for (int op = 0; op < 120; op++) {
                if (rnd.nextBoolean()) {
                    int idx = rnd.nextInt(n), v = rnd.nextInt();
                    ref[idx] = v;
                    s.set(idx, v);
                } else {
                    int from = rnd.nextInt(n), to = from + 1 + rnd.nextInt(n - from);
                    int want = Integer.MAX_VALUE;
                    for (int i = from; i < to; i++) want = Math.min(want, ref[i]);
                    if (s.min(from, to) != want) throw new AssertionError("random mismatch");
                }
            }
        }
    }
}verified

Interval Unions

ProblemSimple approachBottleneckKey ideaStateWalkthroughCodeWhen to use itExercises
Problem

Answering Coverage Questions After Every Update

A firewall service receives rules all day. Some rules open a block of port numbers, and some close part of a block that an earlier rule opened. Between rules, callers ask whether ports 8000 through 8099 are all open, or how many ports are open in total. The service cannot sort the rules once and finish, because the next rule changes the answer to every later question.

The same shape appears in calendar booking, in disk block allocation and in a stream that reports which values it has seen so far. Each public method either adds a range, removes a range or asks about coverage. The question is how to store the ranges so that each call stays fast after thousands of earlier calls, without rereading the whole history.

Simple approach

Keeping Every Added Range In A List

The direct design stores each call exactly as it arrives. An add appends a pair to a list. A query scans the list and checks the pairs.

final class ListCoverage {
    private final List<long[]> added = new ArrayList<>();

    void add(long l, long r) {
        added.add(new long[] {l, r});
    }

    boolean covers(long l, long r) {
        // Walk the coordinates one by one and test each against every stored pair.
        for (long x = l; x < r; x++) {
            boolean hit = false;
            for (long[] p : added) if (p[0] <= x && x < p[1]) hit = true;
            if (!hit) return false;
        }
        return true;
    }
}

Adding is cheap, and the code is easy to trust. Removal does not fit at all, because a removal must cancel parts of earlier pairs that the list keeps as separate records.

Bottleneck

Each Query Rereads The Whole History

Predict first: A caller adds 100,000 ranges and then asks whether one range of width 1,000 is covered. Roughly how many pair checks does `covers` run?

Up to about 100 million checks, because each of the 1,000 coordinates is compared with all 100,000 stored pairs. Wider query ranges and longer histories multiply the cost further.

The list grows with every call, and nothing inside it ever shrinks. With k stored pairs and a query of width w, covers costs O(w * k) time. Port numbers or timestamps reach 10^9, so a wide query makes the width factor unusable. Even a smarter scan that checks pairs and not coordinates still costs O(k) per query.

Counting the covered coordinates is worse. Two stored pairs may overlap, so adding their lengths counts some coordinates twice. The service must find the union of all pairs before it can report a total. Redoing that union on every call turns a long workload of m calls into O(m^2) work or worse. The list stores the history of calls, while the questions ask about the current coverage. The coverage itself needs a smaller representation that updates in place.

Key idea

Store Merged Ranges In A Sorted Map

Merge Everything Into Disjoint Pieces

Coverage is a set of coordinates, and a set of coordinates that forms runs needs only one record per run. Two stored ranges that overlap or touch describe one run, so the structure merges them at insertion time. After every call, the stored ranges are disjoint, which means no coordinate belongs to two of them, and they stay sorted by start. Sorted and disjoint ranges also have sorted ends, so a single binary search by start finds the only range that can contain a given coordinate.

Fix One Half-Open Convention

A half-open interval [start, end) contains start and every coordinate up to but excluding end. Its length is end - start, and an empty range has start == end. Two ranges [1,3) and [3,5) touch when one end equals the other start. The test is end >= start, with no + 1 or - 1. Closed pairs force those adjustments into every comparison. A closed API such as "add the single value v" translates once at the boundary into [v, v + 1). The output translates back by printing end - 1.

Name The Stored Form And Its Lookup

The ranges live in a TreeMap from start to end. Because the ranges are disjoint and merged, this map has exactly one canonical form for each coverage set. The same covered coordinates always produce the same entries, whatever order the calls arrive in. Equal coverage sets give equal maps, which makes the structure easy to test against a brute-force oracle.

The lookup tool is the floor entry, the entry with the greatest start that is at most a given key. TreeMap.floorEntry returns it in O(log n) time. Add uses the floor entry of l to find a range that reaches back into the new one. Then it walks forward over the entries whose starts are at most r, removing each one it absorbs. Remove uses the same floor entry to find a range that must be cut, and query reads one floor entry and compares its end.

Pay For Merges Only Once

Each add creates at most one entry, and the structure deletes each absorbed entry once. The total number of deletions never exceeds the total number of creations. The walk over absorbed entries absorbs O(1) entries per call on average, so each call costs amortized O(log n) including its tree lookups. Remove can split one range into two, which creates one extra entry and keeps the same argument valid.

State

Fields The Structure Keeps

The structure keeps one map and one running sum. The sum follows from the map.

  • ends is a TreeMap<Long, Long> that maps each stored start to its end under the [start, end) convention.
  • total is a long that always equals the sum of end - start over all entries.
  • l and r are the call arguments, with l < r for a non-empty range, and every comparison uses these half-open meanings.

The values stay long because a coordinate near 10^9 plus one, or a sum of many lengths, can exceed the int range.

Walkthrough

Tracing Merges And Splits

A Stream Of Single Points

The first trace feeds the values 1, 3, 2, 7, 6, 5 and 4 to a stream that stores closed points. Each value v becomes the range [v, v + 1), so the pointer l marks v and the pointer r marks v + 1. The variable stored lists the map after the call.

The first two values create separate entries [1,2) and [3,4). The value 2 arrives between them. The floor entry [1,2) ends exactly at 2, and the entry [3,4) starts exactly at 3, so one call fuses all three into [1,4). The values 7, 6 and 5 then grow a second run backward, because each new range ends where the next entry starts. The last value 4 touches both runs and joins them into [1,8). No call scans more than the entries it absorbs.

Adds Removes And Queries

The second trace uses the half-open calls of a range module over coordinates 0 to 11. The pointers l and r mark the call arguments, and result appears on queries only.

Adding [2,6) and [8,11) creates two entries, and adding [6,8) bridges the gap between them. The map holds a single entry [2,11). Removing [4,7) finds that entry as the floor entry, cuts it out and puts back the two remaining pieces [2,4) and [7,11). The query [3,5) reads the floor entry [2,4), whose end 4 falls short of 5, so the answer is false. The query [7,10) succeeds because the entry starting at 7 ends at 11. The query [2,4) succeeds because the end 4 equals the query end, which half-open ranges allow.

Code

Writing Add, Remove And Query Methods

final class IntervalSet {
    // Half-open ranges [start, end), keyed by start, always disjoint and merged.
    private final TreeMap<Long, Long> ends = new TreeMap<>();
    private long total;

    void add(long l, long r) {
        if (l >= r) return;
        Map.Entry<Long, Long> left = ends.floorEntry(l);
        if (left != null && left.getValue() >= l) {
            drop(left);
            l = left.getKey();
            r = Math.max(r, left.getValue());
        }
        Map.Entry<Long, Long> next;
        while ((next = ends.ceilingEntry(l)) != null && next.getKey() <= r) {
            drop(next);
            r = Math.max(r, next.getValue());
        }
        ends.put(l, r);
        total += r - l;
    }

    void remove(long l, long r) {
        if (l >= r) return;
        Map.Entry<Long, Long> left = ends.floorEntry(l);
        if (left != null && left.getValue() > l) cut(left, l, r);
        Map.Entry<Long, Long> next;
        while ((next = ends.ceilingEntry(l)) != null && next.getKey() < r) cut(next, l, r);
    }

    boolean covers(long l, long r) {
        if (l >= r) return true; // an empty range holds no integer, so every integer in it is covered
        Map.Entry<Long, Long> e = ends.floorEntry(l);
        return e != null && e.getValue() >= r;
    }

    long covered() {
        return total;
    }

    private void drop(Map.Entry<Long, Long> e) {
        ends.remove(e.getKey());
        total -= e.getValue() - e.getKey();
    }

    private void cut(Map.Entry<Long, Long> e, long l, long r) {
        drop(e);
        if (e.getKey() < l) { ends.put(e.getKey(), l); total += l - e.getKey(); }
        if (e.getValue() > r) { ends.put(r, e.getValue()); total += e.getValue() - r; }
    }
}

Add absorbs everything that overlaps or touches [l, r), and remove cuts everything that overlaps it. The helper drop keeps total in step with the map, and cut reinserts the pieces that survive on each side. A query on an empty range returns true before any lookup, because it holds no integer. A query on a non-empty range needs only the floor entry of l, because disjoint sorted entries leave no other candidate.

When to use it

Check The Endpoint Convention First

Spot The Pattern In A Problem Statement

Reach for a sorted map of merged ranges when public methods add ranges, remove ranges or ask about coverage over time. The coordinate space must be too large for an array. The invariant to protect is that stored ranges stay disjoint, sorted and merged under one endpoint convention after every call. Every method may assume the invariant on entry and must restore it before it returns.

The workload decides the cost. With many updates and few queries, the amortized merge work dominates and stays cheap. With a small coordinate domain such as 0 to 10^5, a plain boolean array or a segment tree with lazy assignment also works. Before choosing, read the update and query counts, and check whether calls arrive online or are known in advance.

Avoid The Closed Interval Trap

Closed pairs look simpler, yet they are a false friend here. A closed range [1,3] and a closed range [4,6] touch even though 3 < 4. Every comparison then needs a plus one or minus one, which is easy to forget on one side. Translate once at the boundary to half-open ranges and keep every internal comparison free of adjustments.

Know Where The Pattern Stops

Touching ranges merge in a range module, but a booking calendar often must keep them apart because two back-to-back reservations are different bookings. Decide whether touching ranges merge before writing the first comparison. If callers need each original range back, this structure loses that information. The same holds when ranges carry different labels. Those designs need a different record.

Exercises

Exercises

Basic

Data Stream as Disjoint Intervals

LeetCode 352

Problem Specification. Design a class SummaryRanges with two methods. addNum(v) records the integer v as seen, and recording a value twice has no further effect. getIntervals() returns the seen values as the shortest list of closed pairs [a, b], sorted by a. Each pair stands for all integers from a to b inclusive. Two pairs [a, b] and [c, d] stay separate only when c > b + 1. Store each value v internally as the half-open range [v, v + 1) and convert an end back by subtracting 1.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • v is an int with 0 <= v <= 10^9.
  • Calls number at most 2 * 10^5, and they arrive online, so the sequence is not known in advance.
  • Update/query ratio is about 10 to 1, because getIntervals is rare compared with addNum.
  • Return value of getIntervals is a new int[][], and the caller may modify it.
  • Empty stream returns an empty array.

Example 1. Input addNum(1), addNum(3), addNum(7), then getIntervals(), output [[1,1],[3,3],[7,7]]. After addNum(2), the next getIntervals() output is [[1,3],[7,7]].

Example 2. Input addNum(5), addNum(5), addNum(4), then getIntervals(), output [[4,5]], so the repeated value changes nothing.

Prerequisites. The sorted map of merged half-open ranges from this lesson.

What This Exercise Changes. Baseline case: inputs are single points, so a new value has at most one left neighbor and one right neighbor to merge.

Hint

Which stored entries can a new point touch? How many neighbors does a single point have in a sorted map?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The class stores every run of seen values as a half-open entry [start, end) in a TreeMap, keyed by start. The invariant is that entries are disjoint, sorted and never touch, so each run has exactly one entry.

A new value v first checks the floor entry of v. If that entry already reaches past v, the value is a duplicate and the call stops. Otherwise v can join the entry on its left when that entry ends exactly at v, and it can join the entry on its right when one starts exactly at v + 1. The four combinations of those two tests create a fresh entry, extend one entry, or fuse two entries into one. Only one point enters per call, so at most two entries change. getIntervals walks the map and prints each end minus 1, which converts the half-open end back to the closed endpoint.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(log n) per addNum for the floor lookup and at most three map writes, and O(n) per getIntervals for the output size, where n is the number of stored runs.
  • Space is O(n) for the entries, with one entry per run of consecutive seen values.
import java.util.*;

public final class StreamIntervals {
    /** Stores runs of seen values as half-open entries [start, end) keyed by start. */
    static final class SummaryRanges {
        // Invariant: entries are disjoint, sorted by start, and no two entries touch.
        private final TreeMap<Long, Long> ends = new TreeMap<>();

        /**
         * Records v by translating the closed point v into the range [v, v + 1).
         * Time: O(log n), because one floor lookup and at most three map writes run.
         * Space: O(1) extra, because at most one entry is added per call.
         */
        void addNum(int v) {
            // Work in long so that v + 1 never overflows near the int limit.
            long s = v, e = (long) v + 1;
            // The floor entry is the only stored range that can contain or touch v from the left.
            Map.Entry<Long, Long> left = ends.floorEntry(s);
            // A floor entry reaching past v means v is already seen, so nothing changes.
            if (left != null && left.getValue() > s) return;
            // A floor entry that ends exactly at v touches the new range, so absorb it.
            if (left != null && left.getValue() == s) { s = left.getKey(); ends.remove(s); }
            // An entry that starts exactly at v + 1 touches from the right, so absorb it too.
            Long rightEnd = ends.get(e);
            if (rightEnd != null) { ends.remove(e); e = rightEnd; }
            // Store the merged run, which restores the disjoint and untouching invariant.
            ends.put(s, e);
        }

        /**
         * Returns the runs as closed pairs sorted by start.
         * Time: O(n), because each stored run produces one output pair.
         * Space: O(n), because the answer array has one row per run.
         */
        int[][] getIntervals() {
            // Allocate one row per stored run.
            int[][] out = new int[ends.size()][];
            int i = 0;
            // Iterate in key order, so the pairs come out sorted by start.
            for (Map.Entry<Long, Long> en : ends.entrySet())
                // Convert the half-open end back to the closed endpoint by subtracting 1.
                out[i++] = new int[] {(int) (long) en.getKey(), (int) (en.getValue() - 1)};
            // Return the fresh array, which the caller may modify freely.
            return out;
        }
    }

    /** Brute-force oracle: marks seen values in a boolean array and scans for runs. */
    static int[][] oracle(boolean[] seen) {
        List<int[]> runs = new ArrayList<>();
        // Scan every coordinate once.
        for (int i = 0; i < seen.length; i++) {
            // Skip unseen coordinates.
            if (!seen[i]) continue;
            int j = i;
            // Extend j to the last seen coordinate of the run.
            while (j + 1 < seen.length && seen[j + 1]) j++;
            runs.add(new int[] {i, j});
            i = j;
        }
        return runs.toArray(new int[0][]);
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Checks Example 1: three separate points, then 2 fuses the first run.
        SummaryRanges s = new SummaryRanges();
        s.addNum(1); s.addNum(3); s.addNum(7);
        if (!Arrays.deepEquals(s.getIntervals(), new int[][] {{1, 1}, {3, 3}, {7, 7}})) throw new AssertionError("example 1 before");
        s.addNum(2);
        if (!Arrays.deepEquals(s.getIntervals(), new int[][] {{1, 3}, {7, 7}})) throw new AssertionError("example 1 after");
        // Checks Example 2: a repeated value changes nothing.
        SummaryRanges d = new SummaryRanges();
        d.addNum(5); d.addNum(5); d.addNum(4);
        if (!Arrays.deepEquals(d.getIntervals(), new int[][] {{4, 5}})) throw new AssertionError("example 2");
        // Checks the empty stream and the largest allowed value.
        if (new SummaryRanges().getIntervals().length != 0) throw new AssertionError("empty stream");
        SummaryRanges big = new SummaryRanges();
        big.addNum(Integer.MAX_VALUE);
        if (!Arrays.deepEquals(big.getIntervals(), new int[][] {{Integer.MAX_VALUE, Integer.MAX_VALUE}})) throw new AssertionError("max int");
        // Checks the map against the oracle after every call on 3,000 random streams.
        Random rnd = new Random(11);
        for (int t = 0; t < 3000; t++) {
            SummaryRanges sr = new SummaryRanges();
            boolean[] seen = new boolean[30];
            for (int k = 0; k < 25; k++) {
                int v = rnd.nextInt(30);
                sr.addNum(v);
                seen[v] = true;
                if (!Arrays.deepEquals(sr.getIntervals(), oracle(seen))) throw new AssertionError("stream mismatch");
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Variation

Range Module

LeetCode 715

Problem Specification. Design a class RangeModule over the integers, with half-open ranges [left, right). addRange(left, right) makes every integer in the range tracked. removeRange(left, right) makes every integer in the range untracked. queryRange(left, right) returns true exactly when every integer in the range is tracked. Ranges that touch or overlap behave as one tracked run.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • left and right are int values with 1 <= left < right <= 10^9.
  • Calls number at most 10^5, and they arrive online.
  • Update/query ratio is about 1 to 1, so adds, removes and queries all appear in volume.
  • Return value is true or false for a query, and the other two methods return nothing.

Example 1. Input addRange(10,20), removeRange(14,16), then three queries. Output: queryRange(10,14) is true, queryRange(13,15) is false and queryRange(16,17) is true.

Example 2. Input addRange(5,8), addRange(8,10), queryRange(5,10), output true, because the touching ranges merge. After input removeRange(8,9), the same query has output false.

Prerequisites. The stream exercise above.

What This Exercise Changes. Removal is added, so an entry can split in two and the structure must reinsert surviving pieces.

Hint

A removal may split one stored entry into a left piece and a right piece. Which entries need cutting, and which need deleting?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The module keeps the same invariant as the stream: disjoint, sorted half-open entries in a TreeMap, where touching entries are merged. Every method runs a window over the map instead of a single lookup.

For addRange, the window starts at the floor entry of left when that entry reaches left, and ends at the last start that is at most right. A sub-map view holds exactly the entries to absorb. The new end is the maximum of right and all absorbed ends, then the view is cleared and one entry is inserted. For removeRange, the only entry that can stick out on the left is the one with the greatest start below left, and it is trimmed to end at left. The entries with starts inside [left, right) all vanish, and the largest end seen among the trimmed and vanished entries becomes a surviving tail entry [right, end) when it exceeds right. A query reads the floor entry of left and compares its end with right.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(log n) plus the number of absorbed entries per call, which is amortized O(log n) because each entry is created once and deleted at most once.
  • Space is O(n) for the entries, where n is the number of stored runs.
import java.util.*;

public final class RangeModuleSolution {
    /** Tracks integers as disjoint half-open ranges [start, end) keyed by start. */
    static final class RangeModule {
        // Invariant: entries are disjoint, sorted by start, and touching entries are merged.
        private final TreeMap<Long, Long> ends = new TreeMap<>();

        /**
         * Tracks every integer of [left, right).
         * Time: O(log n) plus absorbed entries, amortized O(log n) per call.
         * Space: O(1) extra, because one entry is inserted per call.
         */
        void addRange(long left, long right) {
            // An empty range tracks nothing.
            if (left >= right) return;
            long lo = left, hi = right;
            // A floor entry that reaches left (touching counts) extends the new range backward.
            Map.Entry<Long, Long> f = ends.floorEntry(left);
            if (f != null && f.getValue() >= left) lo = f.getKey();
            // The view holds every entry that starts in [lo, right], all of which overlap or touch.
            NavigableMap<Long, Long> hit = ends.subMap(lo, true, right, true);
            // The merged end is the largest end among the new range and the absorbed entries.
            for (long e : hit.values()) hi = Math.max(hi, e);
            // Clearing the view deletes the absorbed entries from the backing map.
            hit.clear();
            // One merged entry restores the invariant.
            ends.put(lo, hi);
        }

        /**
         * Untracks every integer of [left, right).
         * Time: O(log n) plus removed entries, amortized O(log n) per call.
         * Space: O(1) extra, because at most one tail entry is inserted.
         */
        void removeRange(long left, long right) {
            // An empty range untracks nothing.
            if (left >= right) return;
            long tail = -1;
            // Only the entry with the greatest start below left can stick out to the left.
            Map.Entry<Long, Long> f = ends.lowerEntry(left);
            if (f != null && f.getValue() > left) {
                // Remember its old end, because the part past right must survive.
                tail = f.getValue();
                // Trim it so that it ends at left.
                ends.put(f.getKey(), left);
            }
            // Entries starting in [left, right) lie fully or partly inside the removed range.
            NavigableMap<Long, Long> mid = ends.subMap(left, true, right, false);
            for (long e : mid.values()) tail = Math.max(tail, e);
            mid.clear();
            // A tail past right survives as one entry, because the last cut entry covers [right, tail).
            if (tail > right) ends.put(right, tail);
        }

        /**
         * Returns true when every integer of [left, right) is tracked.
         * Time: O(log n), because one floor lookup runs.
         * Space: O(1), because nothing is allocated.
         */
        boolean queryRange(long left, long right) {
            // An empty or reversed range holds no integer, so it is trivially tracked.
            if (left >= right) return true;
            // Disjoint merged entries leave the floor entry of left as the only candidate.
            Map.Entry<Long, Long> f = ends.floorEntry(left);
            // The range is tracked only when that entry reaches right (end is exclusive).
            return f != null && f.getValue() >= right;
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Checks Example 1: a removal splits the added range.
        RangeModule m = new RangeModule();
        m.addRange(10, 20); m.removeRange(14, 16);
        if (!m.queryRange(10, 14) || m.queryRange(13, 15) || !m.queryRange(16, 17)) throw new AssertionError("example 1");
        // Checks Example 2: touching ranges merge and a removal breaks them.
        RangeModule t = new RangeModule();
        t.addRange(5, 8); t.addRange(8, 10);
        if (!t.queryRange(5, 10)) throw new AssertionError("touching ranges merge");
        t.removeRange(8, 9);
        if (t.queryRange(5, 10)) throw new AssertionError("removal breaks the run");
        // Checks that an empty range is covered even on an empty module.
        if (!new RangeModule().queryRange(5, 5) || !m.queryRange(7, 7) || !m.queryRange(9, 3)) throw new AssertionError("empty range");
        // Checks coordinates near 10^9, which stay in long arithmetic.
        RangeModule b = new RangeModule();
        b.addRange(1, 1_000_000_000);
        if (!b.queryRange(5, 1_000_000_000) || b.queryRange(5, 1_000_000_001)) throw new AssertionError("large coordinates");
        // Checks every query against a boolean-array oracle after every call on random calls.
        Random rnd = new Random(3);
        for (int trial = 0; trial < 3000; trial++) {
            RangeModule rm = new RangeModule();
            boolean[] cov = new boolean[40];
            for (int k = 0; k < 30; k++) {
                int l = rnd.nextInt(39), r = l + 1 + rnd.nextInt(40 - l);
                int op = rnd.nextInt(3);
                if (op == 0) { rm.addRange(l, r); for (int x = l; x < r; x++) cov[x] = true; }
                else if (op == 1) { rm.removeRange(l, r); for (int x = l; x < r; x++) cov[x] = false; }
                else {
                    // The oracle answers by testing each coordinate of the query.
                    boolean want = true;
                    for (int x = l; x < r; x++) if (!cov[x]) want = false;
                    if (rm.queryRange(l, r) != want) throw new AssertionError("query mismatch");
                }
            }
            // After the calls, every unit query agrees with the oracle.
            for (int x = 0; x < 40; x++) if (rm.queryRange(x, x + 1) != cov[x]) throw new AssertionError("final unit query");
        }
    }
}verified
Edge Cases

Touching And Empty Ranges

Author exercise

Problem Specification. A coverage set starts empty. A list of operations arrives, each with a type (add or remove) and a half-open range [l, r) with l <= r. An add makes the integers of the range covered, and a remove makes them uncovered. A range with l == r contains no integers and changes nothing. After each operation, report the number of maximal runs of consecutive covered integers. Two runs that touch, where one ends at the coordinate that the next starts at, count as one run.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • l and r are long values with 0 <= l <= r <= 10^12.
  • Operations number at most 2 * 10^5, and the whole list is known in advance.
  • Update/query ratio is 1 to 1, because every operation also reports a count.
  • Empty range has l == r and must never create or split an entry.
  • Return value is a long[] with one run count per operation.

Example 1. Input operations add [1,3), add [5,7), add [3,5), remove [2,2), remove [3,5), output run counts [1,2,1,1,2].

Example 2. Input operations add [0,4), remove [0,4), add [2,2), remove [1,3), output run counts [1,0,0,0].

Prerequisites. The range module exercise above.

What This Exercise Changes. Empty ranges and touching ends appear together, so add merges on equality while remove does not, and the empty range exits early.

Hint

What should the touching test compare for an add, and what should it compare for a remove? Why do these two tests differ by an equality?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The structure is the same disjoint map of half-open entries, but each operation first extracts every entry that it affects and then reinserts the result. Extraction walks downward from the greatest start that can matter and stops at the first entry that is out of reach, which is valid because disjoint sorted entries also have sorted ends.

The touching test is the only difference between the two operations. An add extracts entries with start <= r and end >= l, so equal endpoints merge. A remove extracts entries with start < r and end > l, so an entry that merely touches stays untouched. Extraction returns the union bounds lo and hi of the removed entries and the operation range. An add reinserts [lo, hi). A remove reinserts [lo, l) when lo < l and [r, hi) when hi > r. An operation with l == r returns before extraction, which keeps empty ranges from splitting or creating entries. The answer after each operation is the map size.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(m log m) for m operations, because each operation does one lookup and one deletion per absorbed entry, and absorbed entries are amortized against creations.
  • Space is O(m) for the entries in the worst case, plus O(m) for the answer array.
import java.util.*;

public final class TouchingEmpty {
    /** Applies add and remove operations on half-open ranges and reports the run count after each. */
    static final class Coverage {
        // Invariant: entries [start, end) are disjoint, sorted and never touch.
        private final TreeMap<Long, Long> ends = new TreeMap<>();

        /**
         * Removes every entry that intersects [l, r) (or touches it when touching is true)
         * and returns the bounds {lo, hi} of the union of those entries with [l, r).
         * Time: O(log n) plus removed entries, amortized O(log n) per call.
         * Space: O(1) extra.
         */
        private long[] extract(long l, long r, boolean touching) {
            long lo = l, hi = r;
            while (true) {
                // The greatest start that may reach the range: at most r when touching, below r otherwise.
                Map.Entry<Long, Long> e = ends.floorEntry(touching ? r : r - 1);
                // No candidate remains, so extraction ends.
                if (e == null) break;
                // Touching ends count as a hit for add (end >= l), and not for remove (end > l).
                boolean hit = touching ? e.getValue() >= l : e.getValue() > l;
                // Ends are sorted like starts, so the first miss ends the walk.
                if (!hit) break;
                // Grow the union bounds to include the extracted entry.
                lo = Math.min(lo, e.getKey());
                hi = Math.max(hi, e.getValue());
                ends.remove(e.getKey());
            }
            return new long[] {lo, hi};
        }

        /**
         * Applies one operation and restores the invariant.
         * Time: O(log n) amortized. Space: O(1) extra.
         */
        void apply(boolean add, long l, long r) {
            // An empty range contains no integers, so the coverage cannot change.
            if (l == r) return;
            if (add) {
                // Merge on equal endpoints and store the union as one entry.
                long[] b = extract(l, r, true);
                ends.put(b[0], b[1]);
            } else {
                // Cut without merging on equal endpoints, then reinsert the surviving sides.
                long[] b = extract(l, r, false);
                if (b[0] < l) ends.put(b[0], l);
                if (b[1] > r) ends.put(r, b[1]);
            }
        }

        int runs() {
            return ends.size();
        }
    }

    /**
     * Returns the run count after each operation; each op is {type, l, r} with type 1 for add.
     * Time: O(m log m) overall. Space: O(m).
     */
    static long[] runCounts(long[][] ops) {
        Coverage c = new Coverage();
        long[] out = new long[ops.length];
        // One pass applies each operation, then records the run count (the update/query ratio is 1 to 1).
        for (int i = 0; i < ops.length; i++) {
            c.apply(ops[i][0] == 1, ops[i][1], ops[i][2]);
            out[i] = c.runs();
        }
        return out;
    }

    /** Brute-force oracle using a boolean array and a run scan after each operation. */
    static long[] oracle(long[][] ops, int n) {
        boolean[] cov = new boolean[n];
        long[] out = new long[ops.length];
        for (int i = 0; i < ops.length; i++) {
            // Set or clear each coordinate of the half-open range.
            for (int x = (int) ops[i][1]; x < (int) ops[i][2]; x++) cov[x] = ops[i][0] == 1;
            // Count the positions where a covered coordinate follows an uncovered one.
            long runs = 0;
            for (int x = 0; x < n; x++) if (cov[x] && (x == 0 || !cov[x - 1])) runs++;
            out[i] = runs;
        }
        return out;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Checks Example 1: touching adds merge, and the empty remove changes nothing.
        long[][] e1 = {{1, 1, 3}, {1, 5, 7}, {1, 3, 5}, {0, 2, 2}, {0, 3, 5}};
        if (!Arrays.equals(runCounts(e1), new long[] {1, 2, 1, 1, 2})) throw new AssertionError("example 1");
        // Checks Example 2: removing everything and empty operations leave no runs.
        long[][] e2 = {{1, 0, 4}, {0, 0, 4}, {1, 2, 2}, {0, 1, 3}};
        if (!Arrays.equals(runCounts(e2), new long[] {1, 0, 0, 0})) throw new AssertionError("example 2");
        // Checks that a remove touching an entry from outside leaves it whole.
        if (!Arrays.equals(runCounts(new long[][] {{1, 5, 9}, {0, 9, 12}, {0, 0, 5}}), new long[] {1, 1, 1})) throw new AssertionError("remove touching");
        // Checks the largest allowed coordinate in long arithmetic.
        if (!Arrays.equals(runCounts(new long[][] {{1, 0, 1_000_000_000_000L}, {0, 1, 1_000_000_000_000L}}), new long[] {1, 1})) throw new AssertionError("large coordinates");
        // Compares with the oracle on 5,000 random operation lists, including empty ranges.
        Random rnd = new Random(5);
        for (int t = 0; t < 5000; t++) {
            long[][] ops = new long[25][];
            for (int i = 0; i < ops.length; i++) {
                int l = rnd.nextInt(21), r = l + rnd.nextInt(21 - l);
                ops[i] = new long[] {rnd.nextInt(2), l, r};
            }
            if (!Arrays.equals(runCounts(ops), oracle(ops, 20))) throw new AssertionError("oracle mismatch");
        }
    }
}verified
Pattern Recognition

Count Integers in Intervals

LeetCode 2276

Problem Specification. Design a class CountIntervals. add(left, right) marks every integer from left to right inclusive as covered, where the pair is closed. count() returns how many distinct integers are covered by all calls so far. Marking an integer twice counts it once. Convert each closed pair into a half-open range internally and keep a running total, because the domain is too large for an array.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • left and right are int values with 1 <= left <= right <= 10^9.
  • Calls number at most 10^5, mixed between add and count, and they arrive online.
  • Update/query ratio is about 1 to 1.
  • Return value of count is a long.
  • Coordinate domain is far too large for a boolean array.
