Example 1. Input add(2,3), add(7,10), count(), output 6. After input add(5,8), the covered integers are 2, 3 and 5 through 10, so the count() output is 8.

Example 2. Input add(1,1000000000), count(), output 1,000,000,000. After input add(1,3), which lies inside the covered range, the count() output is still 1,000,000,000.

Prerequisites. The three exercises above.

What This Exercise Changes. The query asks for total covered length, so a running sum is kept and every merge adjusts it. The domain size rules out an array.

Hint

Can count read a stored field instead of walking the map? What must each merge do to that field?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The domain reaches 10^9, so an array is too large, and a segment tree over that domain needs dynamic nodes. A sorted map of merged half-open entries is smaller because it stores one record per run, and the workload only adds and counts. The closed pair [left, right] converts to [left, right + 1).

The class keeps total, the sum of all entry lengths, and updates it during each add. An add finds the start of the merge window from the floor entry of left and then iterates the map forward from that start. Each visited entry whose start is at most the growing end is removed, its length is subtracted from total, and its end extends the window. The merged entry is inserted and its length is added. count returns the field in O(1). The invariant is that total equals the sum of end - start over the entries.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(log n) amortized per add, because each entry is inserted once and removed at most once, and O(1) per count.
  • Space is O(n) for the entries, where n is the number of stored runs, and never more than the number of add calls.
import java.util.*;

public final class CountIntervalsSolution {
    /** Counts covered integers with merged half-open ranges and a running total. */
    static final class CountIntervals {
        // Invariant: entries are disjoint and merged, and total is the sum of end - start.
        private final TreeMap<Long, Long> ends = new TreeMap<>();
        private long total;

        /**
         * Marks the closed pair [left, right] as covered.
         * Time: O(log n) amortized, because merged entries are deleted once.
         * Space: O(1) extra, because one entry is inserted.
         */
        void add(long left, long right) {
            // Convert the closed pair to the half-open range [lo, hi).
            long lo = left, hi = right + 1;
            // A floor entry reaching lo (touching counts) moves the window start backward.
            Map.Entry<Long, Long> f = ends.floorEntry(lo);
            long from = (f != null && f.getValue() >= lo) ? f.getKey() : lo;
            lo = Math.min(lo, from);
            // Walk forward from the window start with an iterator that can delete entries.
            Iterator<Map.Entry<Long, Long>> it = ends.tailMap(from, true).entrySet().iterator();
            while (it.hasNext()) {
                Map.Entry<Long, Long> e = it.next();
                // An entry starting beyond the window end is separated by a gap, so the walk stops.
                if (e.getKey() > hi) break;
                // Widen the window and give back the old length of the absorbed entry.
                hi = Math.max(hi, e.getValue());
                total -= e.getValue() - e.getKey();
                it.remove();
            }
            // Store the merged entry and add its full length once.
            ends.put(lo, hi);
            total += hi - lo;
        }

        /**
         * Returns the number of covered integers.
         * Time: O(1). Space: O(1).
         */
        long count() {
            // The running total is exact by the invariant, so no walk is needed.
            return total;
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Checks Example 1: a merge across two entries.
        CountIntervals c = new CountIntervals();
        c.add(2, 3); c.add(7, 10);
        if (c.count() != 6) throw new AssertionError("example 1 first count");
        c.add(5, 8);
        if (c.count() != 8) throw new AssertionError("example 1 second count");
        // Checks Example 2: the total uses long and a covered re-add changes nothing.
        CountIntervals d = new CountIntervals();
        d.add(1, 1_000_000_000);
        if (d.count() != 1_000_000_000L) throw new AssertionError("big count");
        d.add(1, 3);
        if (d.count() != 1_000_000_000L) throw new AssertionError("covered re-add");
        // Checks that totals beyond the int range are exact, by adding two disjoint huge pieces.
        CountIntervals h = new CountIntervals();
        h.add(1, 1_000_000_000); h.add(1_500_000_000L, 2_500_000_000L);
        if (h.count() != 2_000_000_001L) throw new AssertionError("long total");
        // Compares every count with a boolean-array oracle on 4,000 random call lists.
        Random rnd = new Random(17);
        for (int t = 0; t < 4000; t++) {
            CountIntervals ci = new CountIntervals();
            boolean[] cov = new boolean[50];
            for (int k = 0; k < 20; k++) {
                int l = rnd.nextInt(49), r = l + rnd.nextInt(49 - l);
                ci.add(l, r);
                for (int x = l; x <= r; x++) cov[x] = true;
                long want = 0;
                for (boolean b : cov) if (b) want++;
                if (ci.count() != want) throw new AssertionError("count mismatch");
            }
        }
    }
}verified

Ordered Structures

ProblemSimple approachBottleneckKey ideaStateWalkthroughCodeWhen to use itExercises
Problem

Find The Nearest Score Below A Limit

A game server keeps player scores and must answer one question many times per second. What is the highest stored score that does not exceed 1500? New scores arrive all the time, and old ones leave. Lesson 02 used a library sorted map. This exercise sets a standalone constraint instead: the runtime offers only arrays, linked nodes and integers, so the sorted structure must be built by hand.

A sorted array answers the question with binary search, but every insertion shifts half of the array. A linked chain inserts in constant time once the position is known. Finding that position means visiting nodes one by one. This lesson asks how to build search that stays fast while both insertions and removals keep happening.

Simple approach

Walk A Sorted Chain Node By Node

The direct approach keeps the scores in a sorted singly linked chain. To answer the question, it starts at the first node and follows links until the next key would exceed the limit.

final class SortedChain {
    static final class Node {
        int key;
        Node next;
        Node(int key, Node next) { this.key = key; this.next = next; }
    }

    Node first; // smallest key, or null for an empty chain

    // Returns the largest key at most limit, or -1 when every key is larger.
    int floor(long limit) {
        int best = -1;
        for (Node cur = first; cur != null && cur.key <= limit; cur = cur.next) {
            best = cur.key;
        }
        return best;
    }
}

Insertion and removal reuse the same walk to find the neighbor node, then relink in constant time. The code is short and correct, and it never shifts any element.

Bottleneck

One Link Per Step Is Too Slow

Predict first: A chain holds 1,000,000 sorted keys and the limit is larger than every key. How many links does floor follow, and does a longer chain change that count?

The loop follows all 1,000,000 links, because the cursor must pass every node before it reaches the end. A chain twice as long needs twice as many steps.

The walk costs O(n) per query, since the cursor moves exactly one node per step and the answer can sit at the far end. With 100,000 calls on 100,000 keys, the work reaches about 10^10 link follows. Insertion and removal inherit the same cost because they must locate the neighbor first.

The slow part is not relinking. The slow part is that the chain offers no way to jump over many nodes. A structure that keeps the sorted order but lets a search skip long stretches would cut the cost without shifting any element.

Key idea

Short Cuts Over A Sorted Chain

Stack Sparser Chains On Top

Keep the full sorted chain as level 0. Build level 1 as a second chain that holds only some of those nodes, again in sorted order. Build level 2 from some nodes of level 1, and continue upward. Every node on a higher level is the same node as on level 0, with one extra forward link per level.

This layered arrangement is a skip list. A search starts at the sparsest level and moves right while the next key still fits. When the next key is too large, the search drops one level and continues from the same node. At level 0 the search stops on the answer.

Choose Which Nodes Move Up

The rule that picks which nodes appear on higher levels is promotion. A fixed rule such as "every second node of a level moves up" gives about log n levels. Each level halves the remaining stretch. For a structure that changes, a fixed rule breaks on insertion. A new node instead flips a coin at insertion time and moves up one more level for each head. Each level then holds about half the nodes of the level below, in expectation.

Return The Predecessor

A search never moves past the limit, so it ends on the predecessor. That node is the last one whose key is below the limit, or at or below it for a closed comparison. Insertion needs exactly this node on every level, because the new node attaches right after it. Erase needs it too, because it must relink the node before the removed one. One descent therefore serves search, insert and erase.

The invariant says that every level is sorted and forms a subsequence of the level below. Insertion and erase only splice links after the predecessors, so the invariant survives each call.

State

Name The Pieces Before Coding

Four quantities describe the structure at any time.

  • Level count is how many chains exist, about the base-2 logarithm of the number of nodes.
  • Node height is the number of levels a node belongs to, and it equals its number of forward links.
  • Head is a sentinel node that belongs to every level and holds no key.
  • Update array stores, for each level, the last node whose key is below the target.

The comparison against the target is strict for insert, erase and exact search, and non-strict only for the floor query.

Walkthrough

Follow One Search And One Erase

Search For A Floor

Take the eight keys 3, 7, 12, 18, 25, 31, 40 and 52 at indexes 0 to 7. Index i occupies one level plus the number of trailing zero bits of i + 1. This rule is a deterministic stand-in for the coin flips, so every run of the trace repeats exactly. The heights are therefore 1, 2, 1, 3, 1, 2, 1 and 4. Only key 52 reaches level 3.

The first trace answers the floor of 31. At level 3 the next key from the head is 52, which fails, so the search drops. At level 2 the cursor moves to key 18, then key 52 fails and the search drops. At level 1 the cursor moves to key 31, and key 52 fails again. At level 0 the next key is 40, which fails, so the cursor rests on index 5. That is six comparisons, against seven for the linear walk over the same chain, so the saving is small here and grows with n.

Erase A Present Key

The second trace erases key 18 with a strict comparison, so it stops before any key equal to 18. Level 3 and level 2 leave the cursor at the head. Level 1 moves to key 7. Level 0 moves to key 12. The predecessor on each level is therefore head, head, index 1 and index 2. The level 0 successor of index 2 is key 18, so the node is present. Erase unlinks it on levels 0, 1 and 2, the three levels it occupies.

Code

Search And Splice With An Update Array

The core method descends once and records the predecessor on every level. Insert and erase both reuse it, and the level source is a parameter so a test can fix every height.

final class Layers {
    static final class Node {
        final int key;
        final Node[] next;
        Node(int key, int levels) { this.key = key; this.next = new Node[levels]; }
    }

    static final int MAX = 16;
    final Node head = new Node(0, MAX);
    int top = 1;

    // Fills update with the last node below target on each used level.
    Node descend(int target, Node[] update) {
        Node cur = head;
        for (int l = top - 1; l >= 0; l--) {
            while (cur.next[l] != null && cur.next[l].key < target) cur = cur.next[l];
            update[l] = cur;
        }
        return cur.next[0];
    }

    // Splices a node of the given height after its predecessors.
    void insert(int key, int height) {
        Node[] update = new Node[MAX];
        java.util.Arrays.fill(update, head);
        descend(key, update);
        Node node = new Node(key, height);
        for (int l = 0; l < height; l++) {
            node.next[l] = update[l].next[l];
            update[l].next[l] = node;
        }
        top = Math.max(top, height);
    }

    // Unlinks one node holding key, or returns false when the key is absent.
    boolean erase(int key) {
        Node[] update = new Node[MAX];
        java.util.Arrays.fill(update, head);
        Node hit = descend(key, update);              // hit is the first node with key >= target
        if (hit == null || hit.key != key) return false;
        for (int l = 0; l < hit.next.length; l++) update[l].next[l] = hit.next[l]; // bypass on each level
        return true;
    }

    // Largest stored key at most limit, or -1 when none; keys are non-negative here.
    int floor(int limit) {
        Node cur = head;
        for (int l = top - 1; l >= 0; l--)
            while (cur.next[l] != null && cur.next[l].key <= limit) cur = cur.next[l]; // closed comparison
        return cur == head ? -1 : cur.key;
    }
}

The array is pre-filled with the head, so levels above the current top need no special case. The height argument comes from a caller-supplied source, which is a seeded random generator in production and a constant in a test. The strict comparison places an equal key before its existing copies, which is a valid and consistent choice. Erase removes the first copy that the descent reaches.

The expected search cost is O(log n), because each level holds about half the nodes of the level below, so a search crosses about two nodes per level over about log n levels. A run of unlucky coin flips costs O(n) time but still returns a correct answer, because the answer comes from comparing keys.

When to use it

Check Order And Reachability

Decide When To Use It

Reach for a layered search when the design needs nearest-key queries with frequent updates and no sorted-set class is available, which is the standalone constraint of this lesson. Typical cases are an interview setting without library collections, an embedded runtime, or a store that must expose its own range scan. Real systems use the same idea, for example the sorted sets of an in-memory database.

Guard The Invariant

The invariant to guard is that every level stays sorted and every node on a higher level also sits on level 0. A bug that links a node on level 2 without linking it on level 0 makes it reachable by some searches and invisible to others. Test with fixed heights, including all heights equal to one and all at the maximum.

Avoid Two False Friends

Binary search over a linked chain looks like the pattern, because jumping to the middle node halves the range. It breaks the precondition of random access. Reaching the middle node costs a walk of n / 2 links, so the search is O(n) again.

A skip list with a fixed promotion rule, such as every second node of a level, also looks like the pattern. The rule needs evenly spaced nodes, and insertions and removals destroy that spacing. After enough updates one level holds long stretches with no shortcut, and searches fall back toward O(n).

Choose a different tool when keys are small integers in a fixed range, since a Fenwick tree then answers count and rank queries without pointers. Also prefer an ordered map from the library when one exists.

Exercises

Exercises

Basic

Layered Linked Search

Author exercise

Problem Specification. Build a class LayeredLinkedSearch from a strictly ascending int[] keys. The key at index i joins numberOfTrailingZeros(i + 1) + 1 linked levels. The method floorIndex(long x) returns the largest index whose key is at most x, or -1 when every key exceeds x. The structure is built once and queried many times, with no updates. Calls arrive online, with no advance knowledge.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Keys number 0 <= n <= 10^5, are distinct and ascend.
  • Queries number at most 10^5, with ratio 0 updates to 10^5 queries.
  • Comparison is closed, so a key equal to x counts as a match.
  • Query value x is any long.

Example 1. Input keys [3, 7, 12, 18, 25, 31, 40, 52] and x = 31, output 5.

Example 2. Input the same keys and x = 2, output -1.

Prerequisites. The level, head and descent steps from this lesson.

What This Exercise Changes. Basic case: search moves right on a sparse level and drops down, with heights fixed by a rule and no updates.

Hint

Which level should the search start on, and what condition makes it drop one level instead of stopping?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The constructor links every key into the levels given by its height rule. Index i reaches numberOfTrailingZeros(i + 1) + 1 levels. About half the nodes stay on level 0 only, and a quarter reach level 1. A last array holds the newest node of each level, so appending in ascending order keeps every level sorted without a second pass.

The query keeps one cursor that starts at the head. On each level from the top down, it moves right while the next key is at most x, then drops. The cursor key stays at most x and the next node on the current level stays above x. After level 0 the cursor sits on the largest key not above x, or on the head, which reports -1. The harness checks the level count against the bit length of n and compares every query with a linear scan. It also asserts at most two comparisons per level.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(log n) per query, because each level costs at most one advance and one failed comparison. Construction is O(n).
  • Space is O(n) for the links, because the heights sum to about 2n. A query uses O(1) extra space.
public final class LayeredLinkedSearch {
    /** One node of the layered structure; next[l] is the successor on level l. */
    private static final class Node {
        final int index;   // position of the key in the sorted array, -1 for the head
        final int key;     // stored key, unused for the head
        final Node[] next; // one forward link per level the node belongs to
        Node(int index, int key, int levels) {
            this.index = index;
            this.key = key;
            this.next = new Node[levels];
        }
    }

    private final Node head;
    private final int levels;
    long comparisons;

    /**
     * Builds the layers over strictly ascending keys; index i joins trailingZeros(i + 1) + 1 levels.
     * Time: O(n), because each node is linked on an average of two levels.
     * Space: O(n), because the links total about 2n.
     * Invariant: on every level the nodes appear in ascending key order.
     */
    LayeredLinkedSearch(int[] keys) {
        // The top level count is the bit length of n, which is 1 + floor(log2 n).
        int top = Math.max(1, 32 - Integer.numberOfLeadingZeros(keys.length));
        levels = top;
        // The head belongs to every level so each search can start at any level.
        head = new Node(-1, 0, top);
        // last[l] is the most recently linked node on level l.
        Node[] last = new Node[top];
        java.util.Arrays.fill(last, head);
        // Visit keys in ascending order so every level list stays sorted.
        for (int i = 0; i < keys.length; i++) {
            // Height rule: odd positions of (i + 1) reach level 0 only, multiples of 2^k reach level k.
            int h = Integer.numberOfTrailingZeros(i + 1) + 1;
            Node node = new Node(i, keys[i], h);
            // Append the node to the tail of each level it belongs to.
            for (int l = 0; l < h; l++) {
                last[l].next[l] = node;
                last[l] = node;
            }
        }
    }

    int levelCount() {
        return levels;
    }

    /**
     * Returns the index of the largest key that is at most x (closed comparison), or -1 if none.
     * Time: O(log n), because each level advances at most once before dropping.
     * Space: O(1), because only one cursor is kept.
     * Invariant: cur.key <= x always holds, and the node after cur on the current level exceeds x.
     */
    int floorIndex(long x) {
        // Start at the head so the cursor is a valid "no key yet" position.
        Node cur = head;
        // Scan from the sparsest level down to the full list.
        for (int l = levels - 1; l >= 0; l--) {
            // Advance while the next key on this level still fits under x.
            while (cur.next[l] != null) {
                comparisons++;
                if (cur.next[l].key > x) break;
                cur = cur.next[l];
            }
        }
        // The cursor rests on the answer, or on the head when every key exceeds x.
        return cur.index;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Fixed example from the lesson: floor of 31 is index 5, floor of 2 is none.
        int[] fixed = {3, 7, 12, 18, 25, 31, 40, 52};
        LayeredLinkedSearch s = new LayeredLinkedSearch(fixed);
        if (s.floorIndex(31) != 5) throw new AssertionError("floor 31");
        if (s.floorIndex(2) != -1) throw new AssertionError("floor 2");
        if (s.floorIndex(1000) != 7) throw new AssertionError("floor 1000");
        if (s.levelCount() != 4) throw new AssertionError("levels of 8 keys");
        // Random inputs against a linear scan oracle.
        java.util.Random rnd = new java.util.Random(7);
        for (int t = 0; t < 300; t++) {
            int n = rnd.nextInt(40);
            java.util.TreeSet<Integer> set = new java.util.TreeSet<>();
            while (set.size() < n) set.add(rnd.nextInt(200) - 100);
            int[] keys = set.stream().mapToInt(Integer::intValue).toArray();
            LayeredLinkedSearch ls = new LayeredLinkedSearch(keys);
            if (ls.levelCount() != Math.max(1, 32 - Integer.numberOfLeadingZeros(n))) throw new AssertionError("level count");
            for (int q = -110; q <= 110; q++) {
                int expect = -1;
                for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) if (keys[i] <= q) expect = i;
                long before = ls.comparisons;
                if (ls.floorIndex(q) != expect) throw new AssertionError("floor " + q);
                // Each level costs at most one advance plus one failed comparison.
                if (ls.comparisons - before > 2L * ls.levelCount()) throw new AssertionError("too many steps");
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Variation

Design Skiplist

LeetCode 1206

Problem Specification. Design a class Skiplist that stores integers and allows duplicates. The method add(num) inserts one more copy. The method search(num) returns whether at least one copy exists. The method erase(num) removes exactly one copy and returns whether a copy existed. The constructor receives an IntSupplier that returns the height of each new node, so tests can fix every height. Updates and queries are mixed in roughly equal numbers, and calls arrive online.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Values are int values in 0 to 20000.
  • Calls number at most 5 * 10^4 in total, split about evenly between updates and queries.
  • Height returned by the supplier is clamped to 1 to 16.
  • Duplicates are stored as separate nodes.

Example 1. Input add(1), add(2), add(3), search(0), add(4), search(1), erase(0), erase(1), search(1), output the five query and erase results false, true, false, true, false in order.

Example 2. Input add(5), add(5), erase(5), search(5), erase(5), search(5), erase(5), output true, true, true, false, false.

Prerequisites. The layered search exercise above and the splice code from this lesson.

What This Exercise Changes. Heights now come from random promotion, and insertion and erase must splice links on every level a node occupies.

Hint

When duplicates have different heights, which copy does erase remove, and does it remove that same node on every level?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The skiplist keeps a head with sixteen links and one node per stored copy. A shared descend method walks from the highest used level and records in an update array the last node whose key is below the target. All three operations use that array. Search checks whether the level 0 successor has the target key. Add splices a new node of the supplied height after the update entries. Erase takes the first copy at level 0 and unlinks it from every level it occupies.

The first copy is the right node on every level, because it is the first node at or above the target everywhere it appears. Duplicates of unequal height are therefore safe: the unlinked node is always the first one, and the others stay in place. The height comes from an injected supplier. The harness runs seeded coin flips, a constant height of one that degenerates into a chain, and an out-of-range height that clamps to sixteen. Each stream is compared with a counting TreeMap, and the node count is compared after every call.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(log n) expected per call with random heights, because each level holds about half the nodes of the one below. A degenerate height source gives O(n).
  • Space is O(n) for the nodes, plus O(1) per call for the update array of sixteen entries.
import java.util.function.IntSupplier;

public final class Skiplist {
    private static final int MAX = 16;

    /** A node holds one key copy; duplicates are separate nodes. */
    private static final class Node {
        final int key;
        final Node[] next;
        Node(int key, int levels) {
            this.key = key;
            this.next = new Node[levels];
        }
    }

    private final Node head = new Node(0, MAX);
    private final IntSupplier levelSource;
    private int top = 1; // number of levels currently in use

    /** The supplier returns how many levels a new node joins; it is clamped to 1..16. */
    Skiplist(IntSupplier levelSource) {
        this.levelSource = levelSource;
    }

    /** Standard source: each extra level has probability one half. */
    static IntSupplier coinFlips(long seed) {
        java.util.Random rnd = new java.util.Random(seed);
        return () -> {
            int h = 1;
            while (h < MAX && rnd.nextBoolean()) h++;
            return h;
        };
    }

    /**
     * Fills update[l] with the last node on level l whose key is below target (strict comparison).
     * Time: O(log n) expected. Space: O(1) besides the array.
     * Invariant: cur.key < target, and the next node on level l has key >= target.
     */
    private Node descend(int target, Node[] update) {
        // Start from the head at the highest level in use.
        Node cur = head;
        for (int l = top - 1; l >= 0; l--) {
            // Move right while the next key is still strictly smaller than target.
            while (cur.next[l] != null && cur.next[l].key < target) cur = cur.next[l];
            // Remember the last node below target for this level.
            update[l] = cur;
        }
        // The level-0 successor is the first key that is >= target.
        return cur.next[0];
    }

    /**
     * Reports whether at least one copy of num exists.
     * Time: O(log n) expected. Space: O(1).
     */
    boolean search(int num) {
        Node found = descend(num, new Node[MAX]);
        // The first node at or above num matches only when the key is equal.
        return found != null && found.key == num;
    }

    /**
     * Inserts one more copy of num.
     * Time: O(log n) expected. Space: O(1) expected for the new node's links.
     * Invariant: every level list stays sorted and each level list is a subsequence of level 0.
     */
    void add(int num) {
        Node[] update = new Node[MAX];
        // Levels above top are served by the head, so fill them before the descent.
        java.util.Arrays.fill(update, head);
        descend(num, update);
        // Ask the level source and clamp its answer to the allowed range.
        int h = Math.max(1, Math.min(MAX, levelSource.getAsInt()));
        Node node = new Node(num, h);
        // Splice the new node after update[l] on each of its levels.
        for (int l = 0; l < h; l++) {
            node.next[l] = update[l].next[l];
            update[l].next[l] = node;
        }
        // Grow the number of levels in use if the node is taller than before.
        top = Math.max(top, h);
    }

    /**
     * Removes exactly one copy of num and reports whether a copy existed.
     * Time: O(log n) expected. Space: O(1).
     */
    boolean erase(int num) {
        Node[] update = new Node[MAX];
        Node target = descend(num, update);
        // Missing key: nothing changes.
        if (target == null || target.key != num) return false;
        // Unlink the first copy on every level it occupies.
        for (int l = 0; l < target.next.length; l++) {
            update[l].next[l] = target.next[l];
        }
        // Lower the used level count while the highest level is empty.
        while (top > 1 && head.next[top - 1] == null) top--;
        return true;
    }

    /** Counts level-0 nodes; used by the harness to compare sizes. */
    int size() {
        int n = 0;
        for (Node c = head.next[0]; c != null; c = c.next[0]) n++;
        return n;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Fixed scenario with duplicates.
        Skiplist s = new Skiplist(coinFlips(1));
        s.add(1); s.add(2); s.add(3);
        if (s.search(0)) throw new AssertionError("0 absent");
        s.add(4);
        if (!s.search(1)) throw new AssertionError("1 present");
        if (s.erase(0)) throw new AssertionError("erase 0");
        if (!s.erase(1)) throw new AssertionError("erase 1");
        if (s.search(1)) throw new AssertionError("1 gone");
        s.add(5); s.add(5);
        if (!s.erase(5) || !s.search(5)) throw new AssertionError("one copy of 5 remains");
        if (!s.erase(5) || s.search(5)) throw new AssertionError("both copies of 5 gone");
        // Second fixed scenario: the final erase of a missing key reports false.
        Skiplist d = new Skiplist(coinFlips(2));
        d.add(5); d.add(5);
        if (!d.erase(5) || !d.search(5) || !d.erase(5) || d.search(5) || d.erase(5)) throw new AssertionError("duplicate scenario");
        // Random operation streams against a counting map, under three level sources.
        for (int mode = 0; mode < 3; mode++) {
            for (int seed = 0; seed < 60; seed++) {
                final int m = mode;
                IntSupplier src = m == 0 ? coinFlips(seed) : m == 1 ? () -> 1 : () -> 99;
                Skiplist sl = new Skiplist(src);
                java.util.TreeMap<Integer, Integer> oracle = new java.util.TreeMap<>();
                java.util.Random rnd = new java.util.Random(seed * 31L + m);
                int total = 0;
                for (int op = 0; op < 300; op++) {
                    int v = rnd.nextInt(15);
                    int kind = rnd.nextInt(3);
                    if (kind == 0) {
                        sl.add(v);
                        oracle.merge(v, 1, Integer::sum);
                        total++;
                    } else if (kind == 1) {
                        boolean exp = oracle.containsKey(v);
                        if (sl.erase(v) != exp) throw new AssertionError("erase " + v);
                        if (exp) {
                            total--;
                            if (oracle.merge(v, -1, Integer::sum) == 0) oracle.remove(v);
                        }
                    } else if (sl.search(v) != oracle.containsKey(v)) {
                        throw new AssertionError("search " + v);
                    }
                    if (sl.size() != total) throw new AssertionError("size");
                }
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Edge Cases

Duplicate Key Policy

Author exercise

Problem Specification. Design CountedSkiplist, a multiset that stores one node per distinct key together with a count. The method add(key, times) adds times copies. The method eraseOne(key) removes one copy and returns whether the key was present. The method eraseAll(key) removes every copy and returns the number removed. The method count(key) returns the multiplicity and size() returns the total over all keys. A node must disappear when its count reaches zero. Updates and queries are mixed evenly, and calls arrive online.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Calls number at most 10^5.
  • Keys are int values in 0 to 10^9.
  • Times is a long in 1 to 3 * 10^9.
  • Counts and size are long values.
  • Absent key gives false for eraseOne and 0 for eraseAll and count.

Example 1. Input add(7,3), add(7,2), count(7), eraseOne(7), count(7), eraseAll(7), count(7), output 5, true, 4, 4, 0.

Example 2. Input add(4,2), add(9,1), eraseOne(9), size(), eraseAll(9), eraseOne(4), size(), output true, 2, 0, true, 1.

Prerequisites. The skiplist exercise above.

What This Exercise Changes. Duplicates become a count on one node, so removal must tell apart lowering a count from unlinking a node.

Hint

What should happen to the node structure when the count reaches zero, and which type holds a total that exceeds the int range?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The policy stores one node per distinct key and a long count in it. Add either raises the count of an existing node or creates a node with the injected height. Remove-one lowers the count and unlinks the node only when the count reaches zero. Remove-all unlinks at once and returns the old count. This keeps the invariant that every node on level 0 holds a positive count, so a search never reaches a key that has no copies.

The boundary cases are the counts and the totals. A single add can contribute three billion copies, so two adds already exceed the int range, which is why times, count and size are long. The harness checks a total of 4,000,000,001, the node removal on the last copy and absent-key results. It also compares random streams with a TreeMap under three height sources.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(log n) expected per call, because each call is one descent plus constant relinking.
  • Space is O(d) for d distinct keys, because duplicates cost no extra nodes.
import java.util.function.IntSupplier;

public final class CountedSkiplist {
    private static final int MAX = 16;

    /** One node per distinct key; count is the multiplicity. */
    private static final class Node {
        final int key;
        long count;
        final Node[] next;
        Node(int key, long count, int levels) {
            this.key = key;
            this.count = count;
            this.next = new Node[levels];
        }
    }

    private final Node head = new Node(0, 0, MAX);
    private final IntSupplier levelSource;
    private int top = 1;
    private long total; // sum of all multiplicities

    CountedSkiplist(IntSupplier levelSource) {
        this.levelSource = levelSource;
    }

    /**
     * Records the last node below key on every level and returns the first node at or above key.
     * Time: O(log n) expected. Space: O(1).
     * Invariant: update[l].key < key (strict) and update[l].next[l] is null or has key >= key.
     */
    private Node find(int key, Node[] update) {
        Node cur = head;
        // Walk from the highest used level down to level 0.
        for (int l = top - 1; l >= 0; l--) {
            // Advance while the next distinct key is smaller than the target.
            while (cur.next[l] != null && cur.next[l].key < key) cur = cur.next[l];
            update[l] = cur;
        }
        return cur.next[0];
    }

    /** Detaches a node from every level it occupies. */
    private void unlink(Node node, Node[] update) {
        // Each update[l] points at this node on the levels the node occupies.
        for (int l = 0; l < node.next.length; l++) update[l].next[l] = node.next[l];
        // Shrink the used level count when the top levels become empty.
        while (top > 1 && head.next[top - 1] == null) top--;
    }

    /**
     * Adds times copies of key (times >= 1).
     * Time: O(log n) expected. Space: O(1) amortized, one node per new distinct key.
     */
    void add(int key, long times) {
        Node[] update = new Node[MAX];
        java.util.Arrays.fill(update, head);
        Node at = find(key, update);
        total += times;
        // Existing key: raise the multiplicity and keep the node.
        if (at != null && at.key == key) {
            at.count += times;
            return;
        }
        // New key: create one node and splice it into its levels.
        int h = Math.max(1, Math.min(MAX, levelSource.getAsInt()));
        Node node = new Node(key, times, h);
        for (int l = 0; l < h; l++) {
            node.next[l] = update[l].next[l];
            update[l].next[l] = node;
        }
        top = Math.max(top, h);
    }

    /** Removes one copy; returns false when the key is absent. Time: O(log n) expected. Space: O(1). */
    boolean eraseOne(int key) {
        Node[] update = new Node[MAX];
        Node at = find(key, update);
        // Absent key: nothing to remove.
        if (at == null || at.key != key) return false;
        total--;
        at.count--;
        // The last copy removes the node itself so the structure holds only present keys.
        if (at.count == 0) unlink(at, update);
        return true;
    }

    /** Removes every copy and returns how many were removed. Time: O(log n) expected. Space: O(1). */
    long eraseAll(int key) {
        Node[] update = new Node[MAX];
        Node at = find(key, update);
        if (at == null || at.key != key) return 0;
        long removed = at.count;
        total -= removed;
        unlink(at, update);
        return removed;
    }

    long count(int key) {
        Node at = find(key, new Node[MAX]);
        return at != null && at.key == key ? at.count : 0;
    }

    long size() {
        return total;
    }

    /** Counts distinct keys by walking level 0; harness only. */
    int distinct() {
        int n = 0;
        for (Node c = head.next[0]; c != null; c = c.next[0]) n++;
        return n;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Fixed example: bulk additions that overflow an int total.
        CountedSkiplist s = new CountedSkiplist(() -> 3);
        s.add(5, 2_000_000_000L);
        s.add(5, 2_000_000_000L);
        s.add(9, 1);
        if (s.size() != 4_000_000_001L) throw new AssertionError("long total");
        if (s.count(5) != 4_000_000_000L) throw new AssertionError("count above int range");
        if (!s.eraseOne(9) || s.count(9) != 0 || s.distinct() != 1) throw new AssertionError("last copy removes node");
        if (s.eraseOne(9) || s.eraseAll(9) != 0) throw new AssertionError("absent key");
        if (s.eraseAll(5) != 4_000_000_000L || s.size() != 0 || s.distinct() != 0) throw new AssertionError("erase all");
        // Examples 1 and 2 of the exercise.
        CountedSkiplist a = new CountedSkiplist(() -> 2);
        a.add(7, 3); a.add(7, 2);
        if (a.count(7) != 5 || !a.eraseOne(7) || a.count(7) != 4 || a.eraseAll(7) != 4 || a.count(7) != 0 || a.size() != 0) throw new AssertionError("example 1");
        a.add(4, 2); a.add(9, 1);
        if (!a.eraseOne(9) || a.size() != 2 || a.eraseAll(9) != 0 || !a.eraseOne(4) || a.size() != 1) throw new AssertionError("example 2");
        // Random streams against a TreeMap oracle under several level sources.
        for (int mode = 0; mode < 3; mode++) {
            for (int seed = 0; seed < 60; seed++) {
                final int m = mode;
                java.util.Random coin = new java.util.Random(seed);
                IntSupplier src = m == 0 ? () -> { int h = 1; while (h < MAX && coin.nextBoolean()) h++; return h; }
                        : m == 1 ? () -> 1 : () -> MAX;
                CountedSkiplist c = new CountedSkiplist(src);
                java.util.TreeMap<Integer, Long> oracle = new java.util.TreeMap<>();
                java.util.Random rnd = new java.util.Random(seed * 17L + m);
                for (int op = 0; op < 300; op++) {
                    int v = rnd.nextInt(12);
                    int kind = rnd.nextInt(4);
                    long have = oracle.getOrDefault(v, 0L);
                    if (kind == 0) {
                        long times = 1 + rnd.nextInt(3) * 1_000_000_000L;
                        c.add(v, times);
                        oracle.put(v, have + times);
                    } else if (kind == 1) {
                        if (c.eraseOne(v) != (have > 0)) throw new AssertionError("eraseOne");
                        if (have > 1) oracle.put(v, have - 1); else oracle.remove(v);
                    } else if (kind == 2) {
                        if (c.eraseAll(v) != have) throw new AssertionError("eraseAll");
                        oracle.remove(v);
                    } else if (c.count(v) != have) {
                        throw new AssertionError("count");
                    }
                    long sum = 0;
                    for (long x : oracle.values()) sum += x;
                    if (c.size() != sum || c.distinct() != oracle.size()) throw new AssertionError("totals");
                }
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Pattern Recognition

Nearest Keys In A Multiset

Author exercise

Problem Specification. Recognize that lower(x) and floor(x) are the same descent with limits x and x + 1, then build OrderedMultiset on that single descent. The structure has a fixed capacity of distinct keys and a caller-supplied height source. The method add(x) adds one copy. The method removeOne(x) removes one copy and returns whether it existed. The method count(x) returns the multiplicity. The method lower(x) returns the largest stored key strictly below x, and floor(x) returns the largest stored key at most x. Both return -1 when no such key exists. Store the nodes in parallel arrays addressed by slot index, so the capacity bound is exact. Queries outnumber updates about three to one, calls arrive online, and no library sorted set is available.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Capacity is at most 10^5 distinct keys at any time.
  • Keys are int values in 0 to 2^31 - 1.
  • Calls number at most 4 * 10^5.
  • Comparison for lower is strict and for floor is closed.
  • Removed slots are reused by later keys.

Example 1. Input add(10), add(10), add(30), floor(10), lower(10), lower(31), count(10), output 10, -1, 30, 2.

Example 2. Input add(5), add(8), removeOne(8), floor(100), lower(5), floor(4), output true, 5, -1, -1.

Prerequisites. The duplicate key policy exercise above.

What This Exercise Changes. The API now needs nearest-key queries on both sides of a key, so one strict descent serves both by shifting the limit.

Hint

Can floor use a strict search with a larger limit, and what happens to that limit for the largest int key?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The recognition step is that both nearest-key queries are the same descent. Lower asks for the last key below x. Floor asks for the last key below x + 1, computed as a long so the largest int key does not overflow. One half-open limit method therefore serves both, and the other operations use it too.

Nodes live in parallel arrays addressed by slot index, with slot 0 as the head and 0 also meaning no successor. Removed slots go onto a free stack, so capacity bounds the distinct keys alive at once and not the total ever inserted. Add raises a count or splices a slot after the update entries. Remove-one lowers a count and recycles the slot on the last copy. The harness compares floor, lower and count with TreeMap results after every call under three height sources. A capacity of twelve keys forces slot reuse, and one check uses the maximum int key.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(log n) expected per call, because every operation is one descent over the levels.
  • Space is O(capacity) for the arrays, which hold sixteen links per slot.
import java.util.function.IntSupplier;

public final class OrderedMultiset {
    private static final int MAX = 16;
    private final int[] key;      // key[i] is the value stored in slot i
    private final int[] count;    // count[i] is its multiplicity
    private final int[] levels;   // levels[i] is how many levels slot i occupies
    private final int[] link;     // link[i * MAX + l] is the next slot on level l, 0 means none
    private final int[] freeStack;
    private int freeTop;
    private int top = 1;
    private final IntSupplier levelSource;

    /** Slot 0 is the head; slots 1..capacity hold distinct keys. */
    OrderedMultiset(int capacity, IntSupplier levelSource) {
        this.levelSource = levelSource;
        key = new int[capacity + 1];
        count = new int[capacity + 1];
        levels = new int[capacity + 1];
        link = new int[(capacity + 1) * MAX];
        freeStack = new int[capacity];
        // Push slots so that slot 1 is handed out first.
        for (int i = 0; i < capacity; i++) freeStack[i] = capacity - i;
        freeTop = capacity;
        levels[0] = MAX;
    }

    /**
     * Returns the last slot whose key is below limit (half-open range of keys [-inf, limit)) and fills update.
     * Time: O(log n) expected. Space: O(1).
     * Invariant: key[cur] < limit, and the next slot on level l is 0 or has key >= limit.
     */
    private int lastBelow(long limit, int[] update) {
        int cur = 0;
        // Descend from the highest used level.
        for (int l = top - 1; l >= 0; l--) {
            // Slot 0 means no successor; any other slot is compared by key.
            while (link[cur * MAX + l] != 0 && key[link[cur * MAX + l]] < limit) cur = link[cur * MAX + l];
            if (update != null) update[l] = cur;
        }
        return cur;
    }

    /** Adds one copy of x. Time: O(log n) expected. Space: O(1). */
    void add(int x) {
        int[] update = new int[MAX]; // all zero, which is the head
        int pred = lastBelow(x, update);
        int nxt = link[pred * MAX];
        // Same key already stored: raise its count.
        if (nxt != 0 && key[nxt] == x) {
            count[nxt]++;
            return;
        }
        // Capacity is the number of distinct keys the caller promised.
        if (freeTop == 0) throw new IllegalStateException("capacity exceeded");
        int slot = freeStack[--freeTop];
        int h = Math.max(1, Math.min(MAX, levelSource.getAsInt()));
        key[slot] = x;
        count[slot] = 1;
        levels[slot] = h;
        // Splice the slot after update[l] on each of its levels.
        for (int l = 0; l < h; l++) {
            link[slot * MAX + l] = link[update[l] * MAX + l];
            link[update[l] * MAX + l] = slot;
        }
        top = Math.max(top, h);
    }

    /** Removes one copy of x; false when absent. Time: O(log n) expected. Space: O(1). */
    boolean removeOne(int x) {
        int[] update = new int[MAX];
        int pred = lastBelow(x, update);
        int slot = link[pred * MAX];
        if (slot == 0 || key[slot] != x) return false;
        // More copies remain: only the count changes.
        if (--count[slot] > 0) return true;
        // Last copy: detach the slot from its levels and recycle it.
        for (int l = 0; l < levels[slot]; l++) link[update[l] * MAX + l] = link[slot * MAX + l];
        freeStack[freeTop++] = slot;
        while (top > 1 && link[top - 1] == 0) top--;
        return true;
    }

    int count(int x) {
        int slot = link[lastBelow(x, null) * MAX];
        return slot != 0 && key[slot] == x ? count[slot] : 0;
    }

    /** Largest stored key strictly below x, or -1. Uses the half-open range [-inf, x). */
    int lower(int x) {
        int s = lastBelow(x, null);
        return s == 0 ? -1 : key[s];
    }

    /** Largest stored key at most x, or -1. Uses the half-open range [-inf, x + 1), with x + 1 computed as long. */
    int floor(int x) {
        int s = lastBelow(x + 1L, null);
        return s == 0 ? -1 : key[s];
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Fixed example: floor includes equal keys, lower excludes them.
        OrderedMultiset m = new OrderedMultiset(8, () -> 2);
        m.add(10); m.add(10); m.add(30);
        if (m.floor(10) != 10 || m.lower(10) != -1) throw new AssertionError("equal key handling");
        if (m.floor(29) != 10 || m.lower(31) != 30) throw new AssertionError("between keys");
        if (m.count(10) != 2) throw new AssertionError("count 10");
        if (!m.removeOne(10) || m.floor(10) != 10) throw new AssertionError("one copy remains");
        if (!m.removeOne(10) || m.floor(10) != -1) throw new AssertionError("key gone");
        if (m.removeOne(10)) throw new AssertionError("absent");
        // Second example of the exercise.
        OrderedMultiset b = new OrderedMultiset(4, () -> 1);
        b.add(5); b.add(8);
        if (!b.removeOne(8) || b.floor(100) != 5 || b.lower(5) != -1 || b.floor(4) != -1) throw new AssertionError("example 2");
        // The largest allowed key must not overflow when floor adds one.
        m.add(Integer.MAX_VALUE);
        if (m.floor(Integer.MAX_VALUE) != Integer.MAX_VALUE) throw new AssertionError("max key");
        // Random streams against TreeMap, with slot recycling under small capacity.
        for (int mode = 0; mode < 3; mode++) {
            for (int seed = 0; seed < 60; seed++) {
                java.util.Random coin = new java.util.Random(seed);
                final int md = mode;
                IntSupplier src = md == 0 ? () -> { int h = 1; while (h < MAX && coin.nextBoolean()) h++; return h; }
                        : md == 1 ? () -> 1 : () -> MAX;
                OrderedMultiset om = new OrderedMultiset(12, src);
                java.util.TreeMap<Integer, Integer> oracle = new java.util.TreeMap<>();
                java.util.Random rnd = new java.util.Random(seed * 13L + md);
                for (int op = 0; op < 400; op++) {
                    int v = rnd.nextInt(12);
                    int kind = rnd.nextInt(4);
                    if (kind == 0) {
                        om.add(v);
                        oracle.merge(v, 1, Integer::sum);
                    } else if (kind == 1) {
                        boolean exp = oracle.containsKey(v);
                        if (om.removeOne(v) != exp) throw new AssertionError("remove");
                        if (exp && oracle.merge(v, -1, Integer::sum) == 0) oracle.remove(v);
                    }
                    Integer f = oracle.floorKey(v), lo = oracle.lowerKey(v);
                    if (om.floor(v) != (f == null ? -1 : f)) throw new AssertionError("floor " + v);
                    if (om.lower(v) != (lo == null ? -1 : lo)) throw new AssertionError("lower " + v);
                    if (om.count(v) != oracle.getOrDefault(v, 0)) throw new AssertionError("count");
                }
            }
        }
    }
}verified

Deferred Transforms

ProblemSimple approachBottleneckKey ideaStateWalkthroughCodeWhen to use itExercises
Problem

Why A Price Change Takes Seconds

A pricing service keeps a list of prices in memory. New prices arrive all day, and an operator sometimes applies a currency correction to every price already stored. The correction adds a fixed amount, or it multiplies every price by a rate. The list holds a million entries, so each correction visits a million slots, and a burst of corrections stalls the service.

The work looks wasteful because the correction is one decision about the whole list, yet the code pays for it once per entry. New prices make it harder. A price that arrives after a correction must not receive that correction, so the list cannot simply hold one global number. This lesson asks how to record a whole-list operation in constant time and still return the correct value of any single entry on demand.

Simple approach

Updating Every Stored Entry

The direct design stores the values in an array list and applies each whole-list operation by looping over every entry. A new value is appended as it is. A read returns the stored value. Every operation keeps the invariant that each stored value is the current true value.

final class NaiveSequence {
    private static final long MOD = 1_000_000_007L;
    private final java.util.ArrayList<Long> values = new java.util.ArrayList<>();

    void append(long v) { values.add(v % MOD); }

    void addAll(long d) {
        for (int i = 0; i < values.size(); i++)
            values.set(i, (values.get(i) + d) % MOD);
    }

    void multAll(long m) {
        for (int i = 0; i < values.size(); i++)
            values.set(i, values.get(i) * m % MOD);
    }

    long get(int i) { return i < values.size() ? values.get(i) : -1; }
}

The design is correct and easy to read. Its cost depends on how many entries exist when a whole-list operation arrives.

Bottleneck

Every Whole List Call Costs Linear Time

Predict first: A client appends 50,000 values and then calls addAll 50,000 times. About how many element updates does the naive class perform?

About 2.5 billion, because every addAll call sees all 50,000 entries. That is 50,000 calls times 50,000 entries.

Count the element updates. When the list holds n entries, each call to addAll or multAll performs n updates, so a single call costs O(n). A workload of q calls on a list of n entries therefore costs O(n * q). With n = q = 100,000 that is ten billion updates, which exceeds any reasonable time limit.

The loop is also redundant. Every entry receives the same operation, so the loop repeats one decision n times. Reads and appends already cost O(1), so the whole-list calls are the only slow part. The workload here is update heavy, the calls arrive one at a time, and a later call may depend on an earlier answer, so no offline reordering is available. The state must absorb a whole-list operation without touching the entries.

Key idea

Store One Transform For All Entries

Keep The Operation Once

Instead of changing each entry, keep the operation in one place and apply it only when an entry is read. A pending operation that waits for a read is a lazy tag. Whole-list calls update only the tag, so each costs O(1). Reads pay the small price of applying the tag to a single entry.

Compose Operations Into A Pair

Adding d and multiplying by m are both functions of the form x * mul + add. A function of this form is an affine pair (mul, add). Applying one more operation to a value already transformed by (mul, add) produces another pair of the same form. Adding d gives (mul, add + d). Multiplying by m gives (mul * m, add * m). The pair never grows, so a history of a million operations still fits in two numbers. The product of two operations is their composition, and its order matters because multiplication does not commute with addition.

Translate New Values Backwards

A value v appended now must read back as exactly v, even though the tag already holds history. The design stores a raw value x such that x * mul + add equals v. Solving gives x = (v - add) / mul. Division modulo a prime M multiplies by the modular inverse of mul, which is the number whose product with mul leaves remainder 1. Fermat's little theorem gives it as mul^(M-2) mod M, computed by fast exponentiation in O(log M). The inverse exists only when mul is not a multiple of M. That condition is the one weak point of the design, and the Boundary exercise returns to it.

State

Name The Stored And Pending Values

Four quantities describe the whole state, and every method reads or writes only these.

  • M is the prime modulus 1,000,000,007, and every stored number lies in [0, M).
  • x[i] is the raw value stored for entry i at the moment it was appended.
  • mul is the pending multiplier in the affine pair, and it starts at 1.
  • add is the pending addend in the affine pair, and it starts at 0.

The invariant is that the true value of entry i equals (x[i] * mul + add) mod M after every call. Indices are zero-based, and an index at or beyond the current size returns -1.

Walkthrough

Follow The Pair Through Updates

Trace The Small Example

Start with an empty sequence, so mul = 1 and add = 0. The first append of 2 stores raw value 2, because the pair is the identity. The call addAll(3) changes only the addend to 3, and no entry is touched. The second append is 7. The design must store x with x * 1 + 3 = 7, so it stores (7 - 3) * 1 = 4. The call multAll(2) doubles both parts and leaves mul = 2 and add = 6.

The first read of index 0 computes 2 * 2 + 6 = 10, which equals (2 + 3) * 2. The read of index 1 computes 4 * 2 + 6 = 14, which equals (7 + 3) * 2. No whole-list call ever visited either entry. The read of index 2 returns -1 because only two entries exist. The hardest step is the second append, because it writes a raw value that differs from the visible value.

Trace The Modulus Wrap

The second run shows the modulus. Six calls to multAll(100) raise mul to 10^12, which wraps to 999993007 modulo M, and the later append of 9 needs a true inverse of that residue. The first entry reads 999965007 and the second reads 9, the same values that the naive loop produces.

Code

Implement The Pair With Fermat Inverse

final class DeferredSequence {
    private static final long M = 1_000_000_007L;
    private final java.util.ArrayList<Long> raw = new java.util.ArrayList<>();
    private long mul = 1;
    private long add = 0;

    private static long power(long base, long exp) {
        long result = 1;
        base %= M;
        while (exp > 0) {
            if ((exp & 1) == 1) result = result * base % M;
            base = base * base % M;
            exp >>= 1;
        }
        return result;
    }

    void append(long v) {
        long shifted = ((v - add) % M + M) % M;
        raw.add(shifted * power(mul, M - 2) % M);
    }

    void addAll(long d) { add = (add + d) % M; }

    void multAll(long m) {
        mul = mul * m % M;
        add = add * m % M;
    }

    long get(int i) {
        if (i >= raw.size()) return -1;
        return (raw.get(i) * mul + add) % M;
    }
}

The method append first reduces v - add into [0, M), because Java % keeps the sign of its left operand. It then multiplies by the inverse of mul. Every product stays below M * M, which is under 2^63, so long never overflows. The class never loops over the list in addAll or multAll, so those calls cost O(1), while append costs O(log M) and get costs O(1).

When to use it

Check The Cue And The Limits

Spot The Cue

Use this design when new values are appended and some operations affect every value that already exists. The cue is a mix of cheap appends and sweeping updates. The invariant to protect is that each stored raw value and the single pending pair together reproduce the true value of every entry, in the order the operations arrived.

Watch The Order And The False Friend

A plain running total of additions looks like enough, and for addition alone it is. Adding multiplication turns that total into a false friend, because a later multiplier must also scale the additions that came before it. The pair handles this by multiplying the addend as well. Order matters, so composing the same two operations in the other order gives a different pair.

Know When It Stops Working

The design needs a prime modulus and a nonzero multiplier, since division is replaced by the inverse. It also needs every operation to hit all entries. An operation that affects only a range of entries cannot live in one global pair. That case needs a tag per segment, which the next sub-section previews and the last exercise builds.

Preview Lazy Propagation For Ranges

A segment tree node owns a segment and stores its sum. Lazy propagation adds a pending pair (mul, add) to the node. The pair applies to the whole segment, but the node's children have not received it yet. Three rules keep the tree correct.

First, applying (b, c) to a node of length len sets sum = b * sum + c * len, because every one of the len entries is scaled by b and then gains c. Second, the same call composes the pending pair into (b * mul, b * add + c), since the older pair runs first. Third, before a call descends into a node, a push-down hands the node's pair to both children by the first two rules and resets the node to (1, 0).

Take the segment holding values 2 and 3 with sum 5. Applying (2, 1) gives 2 * 5 + 1 * 2 = 12, and the node stores the pair (2, 1). Applying (3, 2) next gives 3 * 12 + 2 * 2 = 40 and the pair (6, 5). A push-down to the leaf holding 2 then gives 6 * 2 + 5 * 1 = 17, which equals 3 * (2 * 2 + 1) + 2. The last exercise is a full build of this tree and not a small change to the global pair.

Exercises

Exercises

Basic

Global Add Sequence

Author exercise

Problem Specification. Design a sequence of long values with three operations. append(v) adds v at the end. addAll(d) adds d to every value currently in the sequence and does not change values appended later. get(i) returns the current value at zero-based index i. Use exact long arithmetic and no modulus. Each call must run in O(1) time.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Calls number at most 10^5 in total, and they arrive online one at a time.
  • Update/query ratio is about 2 to 1, with appends and addAll calls outnumbering get calls.
  • v and d are long values with absolute value at most 10^9.
  • i is any int, and get returns -1 when i < 0 or i >= size(). A stored value of -1 looks the same, so a caller compares i with size() first.
  • Sums stay below 10^15 in absolute value, so long is enough.

Example 1. Input the calls append(5), addAll(3), append(10), get(0), get(1), output 8, 10.

Example 2. Input the calls of Example 1 followed by addAll(-4), get(0), get(1), output 4, 6.

Prerequisites. The affine pair of this lesson, used with mul fixed at 1.

What This Exercise Changes. The loop over entries becomes one stored offset, and new values subtract the offset in force when they arrive.

Hint

What single number records how much was added since the sequence was created? What must a new value subtract so that it reads back unchanged?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The structure keeps one number, offset, that records the sum of every addAll argument so far. Each stored raw value is the visible value minus the offset in force when it was appended. A read adds the current offset back to the raw value.

The invariant is that the true value of entry i equals raw[i] + offset after every call. An addAll(d) raises offset by d, so every existing entry rises by d at once. A value appended later subtracts the offset at its arrival, so it does not receive additions that happened before it existed. The harness compares every read with a brute-force list that applies each addition to each entry, and it asserts that a bad index returns -1.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(1) per call, because each call reads or writes one list slot and one number.
  • Space is O(n) for the n appended raw values plus one offset.
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.Random;

public final class GlobalAddSequence {
    // The pending offset equals the sum of all addAll arguments received so far.
    private long offset = 0;
    // Raw values hold each appended value minus the offset at its arrival.
    private final ArrayList<Long> raw = new ArrayList<>();

    /**
     * Appends v so that it reads back as v right now.
     * Time: O(1) amortized. Space: O(1) extra per call.
     * Invariant: raw.get(i) + offset is the true value of entry i.
     */
    void append(long v) {
        // Subtract the offset so that the later read, which adds it back, returns v.
        raw.add(v - offset);
    }

    /**
     * Adds d to every current entry in constant time.
     * Time: O(1). Space: O(1).
     * Invariant: only offset changes, which shifts every stored entry equally.
     */
    void addAll(long d) {
        // One update replaces a loop over all entries; later appends are unaffected.
        offset += d;
    }

    /**
     * Returns the current value of entry i.
     * Time: O(1). Space: O(1).
     * Invariant: raw.get(i) + offset is the true value of entry i.
     */
    long get(int i) {
        // Return -1 for indices outside the sequence, as the contract requires.
        if (i < 0 || i >= raw.size()) return -1;
        // Add the offset back to the raw value to recover the visible value.
        return raw.get(i) + offset;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Check Example 1 and Example 2 from the exercise.
        GlobalAddSequence s = new GlobalAddSequence();
        s.append(5); s.addAll(3); s.append(10);
        if (s.get(0) != 8 || s.get(1) != 10) throw new AssertionError("example 1");
        s.addAll(-4);
        if (s.get(0) != 4 || s.get(1) != 6) throw new AssertionError("example 2");
        // Check that an invalid index returns -1, since the contract promises it.
        if (s.get(2) != -1 || s.get(-1) != -1) throw new AssertionError("bad index must return -1");
        // Compare against a brute-force list on random call sequences.
        Random rnd = new Random(1);
        for (int round = 0; round < 300; round++) {
            GlobalAddSequence fast = new GlobalAddSequence();
            ArrayList<Long> slow = new ArrayList<>();
            for (int step = 0; step < 60; step++) {
                int kind = rnd.nextInt(3);
                long x = rnd.nextInt(2_000_001) - 1_000_000;
                if (kind == 0) { fast.append(x); slow.add(x); }
                else if (kind == 1) { fast.addAll(x); for (int i = 0; i < slow.size(); i++) slow.set(i, slow.get(i) + x); }
                else if (!slow.isEmpty()) {
                    int i = rnd.nextInt(slow.size());
                    if (fast.get(i) != slow.get(i)) throw new AssertionError("round " + round + " step " + step);
                }
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Variation

Fancy Sequence

LeetCode 1622

Problem Specification. Implement a sequence with append(val), addAll(inc), multAll(m) and getIndex(idx). multAll(m) multiplies every value currently stored by m. getIndex(idx) returns the value at zero-based index idx modulo 1_000_000_007, or -1 when idx is not less than the current size. The three updates apply only to values that exist at the time of the call.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Calls number at most 10^5, they arrive online, and no call is known in advance.
  • Update/query ratio is mixed with no fixed split, so each of the four calls must stay cheap.
  • val and inc are integers in [0, 100].
  • m is an integer in [1, 100], so it is never a multiple of the modulus.
  • Return value is a long in [0, M) or -1.

Example 1. Input the calls append(2), addAll(3), append(7), multAll(2), getIndex(0), getIndex(1), getIndex(2), output 10, 14, -1.

Example 2. Input the calls append(5), multAll(100) six times, append(9), getIndex(0), getIndex(1), output 999965007, 9.

Prerequisites. The Global Add Sequence exercise above and the modular inverse of this lesson.

What This Exercise Changes. Multiplication joins addition, so the stored state grows from one offset to a pair and appends need a modular inverse.

Hint

What pair of numbers composes both operations into one? How does a new value find the raw number that reads back as itself?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The structure keeps a raw value per entry and one pending pair (mul, add). The invariant is that the true value of entry i equals raw[i] * mul + add modulo M after every call. The call addAll(d) adds d to add. The call multAll(m) multiplies both mul and add by m, because the earlier additions must be scaled as well.

An appended value v needs a raw value x with x * mul + add = v. The code computes x = (v - add) * inverse(mul), where the inverse is mul^(M-2) by Fermat's little theorem. The inverse exists because every m lies in [1, 100] and M is a prime larger than 100, so mul never becomes a multiple of M. The harness asserts that % returns a negative remainder for a negative left operand, which is why append adds M before the final reduction. It also checks the inverse identity and compares every read with a naive loop on random calls.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(1) for addAll, multAll and getIndex, and O(log M) for append, because the inverse needs about 30 squarings.
  • Space is O(n) for the raw values plus two numbers for the pair.
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.Random;

public final class FancySequence {
    // M is prime, so every residue except 0 has a modular inverse.
    private static final long M = 1_000_000_007L;
    // Raw values hold each entry as it must be stored, given the pair at its arrival.
    private final ArrayList<Long> raw = new ArrayList<>();
    // Pending multiplier of the affine pair, never a multiple of M in this exercise.
    private long mul = 1;
    // Pending addend of the affine pair.
    private long add = 0;

    /**
     * Computes base^exp mod M by repeated squaring.
     * Time: O(log exp). Space: O(1).
     * Invariant: result * base^exp stays equal to the original power.
     */
    static long power(long base, long exp) {
        // Start with the multiplicative identity.
        long result = 1;
        // Reduce the base once so that products stay below M * M.
        base %= M;
        // Each pass handles one bit of the exponent, so there are about 30 passes.
        while (exp > 0) {
            // A set low bit multiplies the current square into the result.
            if ((exp & 1) == 1) result = result * base % M;
            // Squaring prepares the next bit.
            base = base * base % M;
            // Drop the bit that was just handled.
            exp >>= 1;
        }
        // The result equals base^exp mod M.
        return result;
    }

    /**
     * Appends v so that it reads back as v under the current pair.
     * Time: O(log M). Space: O(1) extra.
     * Invariant: raw * mul + add equals v modulo M for the new entry.
     */
    void append(long v) {
        // Reduce v - add into [0, M), because Java % can return a negative remainder.
        long shifted = ((v - add) % M + M) % M;
        // Multiply by the inverse of mul to undo the pending multiplier.
        raw.add(shifted * power(mul, M - 2) % M);
    }

    /**
     * Adds inc to every existing entry.
     * Time: O(1). Space: O(1).
     * Invariant: only add changes, which shifts every existing entry equally.
     */
    void addAll(long inc) {
        // Compose the new addition with the pending pair.
        add = (add + inc) % M;
    }

    /**
     * Multiplies every existing entry by m.
     * Time: O(1). Space: O(1).
     * Invariant: scaling both parts of the pair scales raw * mul + add.
     */
    void multAll(long m) {
        // The multiplier scales the multiplier part.
        mul = mul * m % M;
        // The addend also scales, because earlier additions are multiplied too.
        add = add * m % M;
    }

    /**
     * Returns the value at idx modulo M, or -1 when idx is out of range.
     * Time: O(1). Space: O(1).
     * Invariant: raw * mul + add is the true value of the entry.
     */
    long getIndex(int idx) {
        // An index at or beyond the size has no entry.
        if (idx >= raw.size()) return -1;
        // Apply the pending pair to the stored raw value.
        return (raw.get(idx) * mul + add) % M;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Check Example 1 and Example 2 from the exercise.
        FancySequence f = new FancySequence();
        f.append(2); f.addAll(3); f.append(7); f.multAll(2);
        if (f.getIndex(0) != 10 || f.getIndex(1) != 14 || f.getIndex(2) != -1) throw new AssertionError("example 1");
        FancySequence g = new FancySequence();
        g.append(5);
        for (int i = 0; i < 6; i++) g.multAll(100);
        g.append(9);
        if (g.getIndex(0) != 999965007L || g.getIndex(1) != 9) throw new AssertionError("example 2");
        // Check the Java claim that % keeps the sign of the left operand.
        if (-7 % 3 != -1) throw new AssertionError("remainder sign");
        // Check the Fermat inverse identity for several residues.
        for (long a : new long[] {1, 2, 100, 999_999_999L}) {
            if (a * power(a, M - 2) % M != 1) throw new AssertionError("inverse of " + a);
        }
        // Compare against a brute-force list on random calls with the exercise limits.
        Random rnd = new Random(2);
        for (int round = 0; round < 300; round++) {
            FancySequence fast = new FancySequence();
            ArrayList<Long> slow = new ArrayList<>();
            for (int step = 0; step < 80; step++) {
                int kind = rnd.nextInt(4);
                if (kind == 0) { long v = rnd.nextInt(101); fast.append(v); slow.add(v); }
                else if (kind == 1) { long d = rnd.nextInt(101); fast.addAll(d); for (int i = 0; i < slow.size(); i++) slow.set(i, (slow.get(i) + d) % M); }
                else if (kind == 2) { long m = 1 + rnd.nextInt(100); fast.multAll(m); for (int i = 0; i < slow.size(); i++) slow.set(i, slow.get(i) * m % M); }
                else {
                    int idx = rnd.nextInt(slow.size() + 2);
                    long want = idx < slow.size() ? slow.get(idx) : -1;
                    if (fast.getIndex(idx) != want) throw new AssertionError("round " + round + " step " + step);
                }
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Edge Cases

Zero Multiplier

Author exercise

Problem Specification. Use the same four calls as Fancy Sequence, but allow multAll(m) with m = 0. After such a call every value currently stored equals 0 before any later addition. A pending multiplier equal to 0 modulo 1_000_000_007 has no modular inverse, so a design that divides by the running multiplier fails on the next append. Design the structure so it never divides by zero, and explain how it resets the pending pair.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Calls number at most 10^5 and arrive online, with no advance knowledge.
  • Update/query ratio is about 1 to 1, and every call must stay cheap.
  • val, inc and m are integers in [0, M - 1], where M = 1_000_000_007.
  • Zero multiplier m = 0 may occur any number of times and anywhere.
  • Return value is a long in [0, M) or -1 for an index at or beyond the size.

Example 1. Input the calls append(4), multAll(0), addAll(6), append(9), getIndex(0), getIndex(1), output 6, 9.

Example 2. Input the calls append(7), append(1), multAll(0), addAll(4), append(3), multAll(5), getIndex(0), getIndex(2), output 20, 15.

Prerequisites. The Fancy Sequence exercise above.

What This Exercise Changes. The nonzero guarantee disappears, so a zero multiplier must reset the pair and mark every earlier entry as zero.

Hint

After a multiplication by zero, what is the value of every earlier entry, whatever it held before? Could you forget their raw values and restart the pair at the identity?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. A multiplier of 0 makes the pending mul equal to 0 modulo the prime M. A zero has no inverse, because no y satisfies 0 * y = 1, so the formula x = (v - add) * inverse(mul) breaks on the next append. The design avoids the division by resetting the pair whenever m = 0 arrives.

On multAll(0) every existing entry becomes 0, whatever it held. The code sets frozen to the current size, resets the pair to (1, 0) and keeps the old raw values untouched. The invariant is that entry i below frozen has true value add, and entry i at or above frozen has true value raw[i] * mul + add. A frozen entry behaves as a raw value of 0 under the fresh pair, so later additions and nonzero multiplications still apply to it correctly. After a reset, mul is a product of nonzero residues of a prime, so it is nonzero and invertible until the next zero call, which resets again. The harness asserts that the power function returns 0 for base 0, so no inverse exists. It then compares every read with a naive loop on random calls that contain many zeros.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(1) for addAll, multAll and getIndex, and O(log M) for append, because the reset replaces any scan of the entries.
  • Space is O(n) for the raw values plus the pair and the frozen boundary.
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.Random;

public final class ZeroMultiplierSequence {
    // M is prime, so a residue has an inverse exactly when it is not 0.
    private static final long M = 1_000_000_007L;
    // Raw values of entries appended since the last reset keep their meaning under the pair.
    private final ArrayList<Long> raw = new ArrayList<>();
    // Entries with index below frozen were zeroed by a multiplier of 0 and read as add.
    private int frozen = 0;
    // Pending multiplier, always a nonzero residue so that it stays invertible.
    private long mul = 1;
    // Pending addend.
    private long add = 0;

    /**
     * Computes base^exp mod M by repeated squaring.
     * Time: O(log exp). Space: O(1).
     * Invariant: result * base^exp stays equal to the original power.
     */
    static long power(long base, long exp) {
        // Start with the multiplicative identity.
        long result = 1;
        // Reduce the base once so that products stay below M * M.
        base %= M;
        // Each pass handles one bit of the exponent.
        while (exp > 0) {
            // A set low bit multiplies the current square into the result.
            if ((exp & 1) == 1) result = result * base % M;
            // Squaring prepares the next bit.
            base = base * base % M;
            // Drop the handled bit.
            exp >>= 1;
        }
        // The result equals base^exp mod M.
        return result;
    }

    /**
     * Appends v so that it reads back as v under the current pair.
     * Time: O(log M). Space: O(1) extra.
     * Invariant: mul is nonzero, so its inverse exists and raw * mul + add equals v.
     */
    void append(long v) {
        // Reduce v - add into [0, M), because Java % can return a negative remainder.
        long shifted = ((v - add) % M + M) % M;
        // Divide by mul using its Fermat inverse, which is safe because mul is nonzero.
        raw.add(shifted * power(mul, M - 2) % M);
    }

    /**
     * Adds inc to every existing entry.
     * Time: O(1). Space: O(1).
     * Invariant: only add changes, which shifts every existing entry equally.
     */
    void addAll(long inc) {
        // Compose the addition with the pending pair.
        add = (add + inc) % M;
    }

    /**
     * Multiplies every existing entry by m, where m may be 0.
     * Time: O(1). Space: O(1).
     * Invariant: after the call, mul is nonzero and frozen entries read as add.
     */
    void multAll(long m) {
        // A zero multiplier cannot be stored in mul, so it triggers a reset.
        if (m == 0) {
            // Every entry that exists now becomes 0 and is marked by the frozen boundary.
            frozen = raw.size();
            // The fresh pair is the identity, so a frozen entry reads as 0 * 1 + 0 = 0.
            mul = 1;
            add = 0;
