            // Nothing else changes, so no scan of the entries is needed.
            return;
        }
        // A nonzero multiplier scales the multiplier part, and a product of nonzero residues stays nonzero.
        mul = mul * m % M;
        // The addend scales too, because earlier additions are multiplied.
        add = add * m % M;
    }

    /**
     * Returns the value at idx modulo M, or -1 when idx is out of range.
     * Time: O(1). Space: O(1).
     * Invariant: frozen entries read as add, others as raw * mul + add.
     */
    long getIndex(int idx) {
        // An index at or beyond the size has no entry.
        if (idx >= raw.size()) return -1;
        // A frozen entry was zeroed, so only the addend since the reset remains.
        if (idx < frozen) return add;
        // Other entries apply the full pair to the stored raw value.
        return (raw.get(idx) * mul + add) % M;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Check Example 1 and Example 2 from the exercise.
        ZeroMultiplierSequence a = new ZeroMultiplierSequence();
        a.append(4); a.multAll(0); a.addAll(6); a.append(9);
        if (a.getIndex(0) != 6 || a.getIndex(1) != 9) throw new AssertionError("example 1");
        ZeroMultiplierSequence b = new ZeroMultiplierSequence();
        b.append(7); b.append(1); b.multAll(0); b.addAll(4); b.append(3); b.multAll(5);
        if (b.getIndex(0) != 20 || b.getIndex(2) != 15) throw new AssertionError("example 2");
        // Check that zero has no inverse: the power function returns 0, and 0 never multiplies to 1.
        if (power(0, M - 2) != 0) throw new AssertionError("zero power");
        if (0L * power(0, M - 2) % M == 1) throw new AssertionError("zero inverse");
        // Compare against a brute-force list on random calls with frequent zero multipliers.
        Random rnd = new Random(3);
        for (int round = 0; round < 400; round++) {
            ZeroMultiplierSequence fast = new ZeroMultiplierSequence();
            ArrayList<Long> slow = new ArrayList<>();
            for (int step = 0; step < 80; step++) {
                int kind = rnd.nextInt(4);
                if (kind == 0) { long v = rnd.nextInt(5) == 0 ? M - 1 : (long) rnd.nextInt(1_000_000_000); fast.append(v); slow.add(v); }
                else if (kind == 1) { long d = rnd.nextInt(1_000_000_000); fast.addAll(d); for (int i = 0; i < slow.size(); i++) slow.set(i, (slow.get(i) + d) % M); }
                else if (kind == 2) {
                    long m = rnd.nextInt(3) == 0 ? 0 : (long) rnd.nextInt(1_000_000_000) + 1;
                    fast.multAll(m);
                    for (int i = 0; i < slow.size(); i++) slow.set(i, slow.get(i) * m % M);
                } else {
                    int idx = rnd.nextInt(slow.size() + 2);
                    long want = idx < slow.size() ? slow.get(idx) : -1;
                    if (fast.getIndex(idx) != want) throw new AssertionError("round " + round + " step " + step);
                }
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Pattern Recognition

Affine Lazy Segment Tree

Author exercise

Problem Specification. Maintain an array a of n integers modulo M = 1_000_000_007. The call apply(l, r, b, c) replaces a[i] by (b * a[i] + c) mod M for every i with l <= i < r. The call sum(l, r) returns the sum of a[i] for l <= i < r, modulo M. Both ranges are half-open, so r is excluded and an empty range with l = r is allowed. State which fact breaks the single global pair of this lesson and which structure replaces it.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • n satisfies 1 <= n <= 10^5, and the initial values lie in [0, M).
  • Calls number at most 10^5, and they arrive online in any order.
  • Update/query ratio is about 1 to 1, so both calls must cost O(log n).
  • b and c are integers in [0, M - 1], and b = 0 is allowed.
  • Ranges satisfy 0 <= l <= r <= n, and sums use long arithmetic.

Example 1. Input a = [1, 2, 3, 4] and the calls apply(1, 3, 2, 1), sum(0, 4), output 17, because the array becomes [1, 5, 7, 4].

Example 2. Input the calls of Example 1 followed by apply(0, 4, 0, 9), sum(1, 3), output 18, because every entry becomes 9.

Prerequisites. The Zero Multiplier exercise above, a segment tree with range sum queries and the lazy propagation preview at the end of this lesson. The exercise is a full build, not a small variation.

What This Exercise Changes. Updates now cover only a range, so one tag per segment replaces the single global pair and tags compose when pushed down.

Hint

A range update leaves some entries unchanged. How can a node store a pending pair for its whole segment and push it down only when a query needs the children?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. A range update touches only some entries, so one global pair cannot describe it. Two entries that differ in range membership need different transforms. The fix is a segment tree. Every node stores the sum of its half-open segment [lo, hi). It also stores a pending pair (mul, add) that applies to the whole segment but not yet to its children.

The invariant is that a node's sum already includes every update that reached it, and its pending pair still has to reach its children. Applying (b, c) to a node of length len sets the sum to b * sum + c * len, because each of the len entries gains c after scaling. It also composes the tag, so the new pair is (b * mul, b * add + c), which applies the older pair first and the new one second. Before descending, a node pushes its pair to both children and resets to the identity (1, 0). The pair b = 0 needs no inverse, because the tree composes and never divides. The harness compares both calls with a naive array on random ranges, including empty ranges and b = 0.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(log n) per apply and per sum, because each call visits at most about four nodes per level and pushes down one pair per visited node.
  • Space is O(n) for the arrays of sums and tags, with 4n slots each.
import java.util.Random;

public final class AffineLazySegmentTree {
    // M is the prime modulus of every stored sum.
    private static final long M = 1_000_000_007L;
    // sum[o] is the sum of the half-open segment of node o, including all updates that reached o.
    private final long[] sum;
    // mulTag[o] and addTag[o] hold the pending pair that children of o have not received yet.
    private final long[] mulTag, addTag;
    // n is the number of array entries.
    private final int n;

    AffineLazySegmentTree(long[] a) {
        // Allocate four slots per entry, which suffices for a recursive tree.
        n = a.length;
        sum = new long[4 * n];
        mulTag = new long[4 * n];
        addTag = new long[4 * n];
        // Build the tree over the half-open range [0, n).
        build(1, 0, n, a);
    }

    /**
     * Builds node o for the half-open segment [lo, hi).
     * Time: O(n) over the whole build. Space: O(log n) recursion.
     * Invariant: sum[o] is the segment sum and the tag is the identity.
     */
    private void build(int o, int lo, int hi, long[] a) {
        // Every node starts with the identity pair.
        mulTag[o] = 1;
        addTag[o] = 0;
        // A leaf holds one entry.
        if (hi - lo == 1) { sum[o] = a[lo] % M; return; }
        // Split the segment at mid, so the children are [lo, mid) and [mid, hi).
        int mid = (lo + hi) >>> 1;
        build(2 * o, lo, mid, a);
        build(2 * o + 1, mid, hi, a);
        // The parent sum is the sum of the two children.
        sum[o] = (sum[2 * o] + sum[2 * o + 1]) % M;
    }

    /**
     * Applies the map x to b * x + c to the whole segment of node o, which has len entries.
     * Time: O(1). Space: O(1).
     * Invariant: sum[o] is updated and the tag records what children still need.
     */
    private void applyNode(int o, int len, long b, long c) {
        // Each of the len entries becomes b * x + c, so the sum becomes b * sum + c * len.
        sum[o] = (b * sum[o] + c * len) % M;
        // The new tag applies the old tag first and the new pair second.
        addTag[o] = (b * addTag[o] + c) % M;
        mulTag[o] = b * mulTag[o] % M;
    }

    /**
     * Moves the pending pair of node o to both children and resets it.
     * Time: O(1). Space: O(1).
     * Invariant: after the call, o has the identity tag and children hold the full history.
     */
    private void push(int o, int lo, int hi) {
        // Skip the work when the tag is already the identity.
        if (mulTag[o] == 1 && addTag[o] == 0) return;
        // Children cover [lo, mid) and [mid, hi).
        int mid = (lo + hi) >>> 1;
        applyNode(2 * o, mid - lo, mulTag[o], addTag[o]);
        applyNode(2 * o + 1, hi - mid, mulTag[o], addTag[o]);
        // The parent no longer owes anything to its children.
        mulTag[o] = 1;
        addTag[o] = 0;
    }

    /**
     * Applies the map x to b * x + c to every entry in the half-open range [l, r).
     * Time: O(log n). Space: O(log n) recursion.
     * Invariant: sums of visited nodes stay equal to the sums of the true entries.
     */
    void apply(int l, int r, long b, long c) {
        // An empty range changes nothing.
        if (l >= r) return;
        update(1, 0, n, l, r, b, c);
    }

    private void update(int o, int lo, int hi, int l, int r, long b, long c) {
        // A segment fully inside the range takes the pair as a tag and stops.
        if (l <= lo && hi <= r) { applyNode(o, hi - lo, b, c); return; }
        // A partly covered segment pushes first so that children see the older history.
        push(o, lo, hi);
        int mid = (lo + hi) >>> 1;
        // Recurse only into children that overlap [l, r).
        if (l < mid) update(2 * o, lo, mid, l, r, b, c);
        if (r > mid) update(2 * o + 1, mid, hi, l, r, b, c);
        // Rebuild the parent sum from the updated children.
        sum[o] = (sum[2 * o] + sum[2 * o + 1]) % M;
    }

    /**
     * Returns the sum of entries in the half-open range [l, r) modulo M.
     * Time: O(log n). Space: O(log n) recursion.
     * Invariant: pushing before descent keeps children exact for the queried part.
     */
    long sum(int l, int r) {
        // An empty range sums to 0.
        if (l >= r) return 0;
        return query(1, 0, n, l, r);
    }

    private long query(int o, int lo, int hi, int l, int r) {
        // A segment fully inside the range contributes its stored sum.
        if (l <= lo && hi <= r) return sum[o];
        // Push the pending pair so that the children are exact.
        push(o, lo, hi);
        int mid = (lo + hi) >>> 1;
        long total = 0;
        // Add the contribution of each overlapping child.
        if (l < mid) total += query(2 * o, lo, mid, l, r);
        if (r > mid) total += query(2 * o + 1, mid, hi, l, r);
        // Each child result is below M, so the total is reduced once.
        return total % M;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Check Example 1 and Example 2 from the exercise.
        AffineLazySegmentTree t = new AffineLazySegmentTree(new long[] {1, 2, 3, 4});
        t.apply(1, 3, 2, 1);
        if (t.sum(0, 4) != 17) throw new AssertionError("example 1");
        t.apply(0, 4, 0, 9);
        if (t.sum(1, 3) != 18) throw new AssertionError("example 2");
        // Compare against a naive array on random ranges, including empty ranges and b = 0.
        Random rnd = new Random(4);
        for (int round = 0; round < 300; round++) {
            int n = 1 + rnd.nextInt(20);
            long[] naive = new long[n];
            for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) naive[i] = rnd.nextInt(1_000_000_000);
            AffineLazySegmentTree fast = new AffineLazySegmentTree(naive.clone());
            for (int step = 0; step < 60; step++) {
                int l = rnd.nextInt(n + 1), r = rnd.nextInt(n + 1);
                if (l > r) { int tmp = l; l = r; r = tmp; }
                if (rnd.nextBoolean()) {
                    long b = rnd.nextInt(4) == 0 ? 0 : (long) rnd.nextInt(1_000_000_000);
                    long c = rnd.nextInt(1_000_000_000);
                    fast.apply(l, r, b, c);
                    for (int i = l; i < r; i++) naive[i] = (b * naive[i] + c) % M;
                } else {
                    long want = 0;
                    for (int i = l; i < r; i++) want = (want + naive[i]) % M;
                    if (fast.sum(l, r) != want) throw new AssertionError("round " + round + " step " + step);
                }
            }
        }
    }
}verified

Query Planning

ProblemSimple approachBottleneckKey ideaStateWalkthroughCodeWhen to use itExercises
Problem

Why Every Query Rescans The Array

A log analysis service loads one million request records into an array, where each entry is an error code. The dashboard then asks questions such as how often code 503 appears between request 40,000 and request 90,000. The first version answers each question with a loop over that range. It passes every test on a small sample. On launch day, the dashboard sends 100,000 questions against the same array, and each one scans up to a million entries. The service burns minutes of processor time on data that never changed between questions.

Nothing is wrong with a single answer, because each loop is correct. The waste comes from repeating the same reading of the array for every question. This lesson asks what the constructor can store once so that each later question needs only a few steps. It also asks how the promises in the API change that choice, such as whether all questions arrive at the start or one at a time.

Simple approach

Scanning The Range For Each Question

The direct design keeps the array and answers each question with a loop over the requested range. The loop compares every entry with the wanted value and counts the matches.

final class ScanFrequency {
    private final int[] arr;

    ScanFrequency(int[] arr) { this.arr = arr; }

    int query(int left, int right, int value) {
        int count = 0;
        for (int i = left; i <= right; i++) {   // visits every slot in the closed range
            if (arr[i] == value) count++;
        }
        return count;
    }
}

The constructor does no work, and each question costs time proportional to the width of its range. For a small array this is the best choice, because nothing can beat a loop that is already short.

Bottleneck

Total Cost Grows With Questions Times Length

Predict first: An array holds 100,000 entries and the service receives 100,000 questions that each span the whole array. About how many entries does the loop read in total?

About ten billion reads, because each of the 100,000 questions reads all 100,000 entries. The array never changes, so almost all of that reading repeats work already done.

With n entries and q questions, each question reads up to n entries, so the total is O(n * q). At n = q = 10^5 that is 10^10 reads. The constructor ran in zero time, yet the service pays for that saving with every question. When questions outnumber constructor calls by a large factor, the total cost sits almost entirely in the question path.

The array also does not change between questions, so every read after the first pass over a region repeats earlier work. A faster loop does not fix this. The fix is to decide, before the first question, which summary of the array answers every possible question. The right summary depends on what the API promises about the questions, and that promise is the next point.

Key idea

Store Positions And Read The Contract

Keep Sorted Positions Per Value

A position index maps each value to the ascending list of array positions where it occurs. The constructor builds it in one pass. The constructor appends positions in index order, so each list stays sorted. A question about value v in the closed range [left, right] now needs only the list of v. The answer is the number of list entries inside the range, and two binary searches find that number. The first search returns how many entries are below left. The second returns how many entries are below right + 1. Their difference is the count, and the half-open end right + 1 keeps both searches identical.

Let The Call Contract Pick The Plan

The summary is only half of the plan. The other half is a fact about the API, namely whether the questions are known in advance. Offline questions all arrive before any answer is due, so the code may reorder them. A union-find is a structure that merges vertices into groups and tells whether two vertices share a group. For example, take edges of weight 2, 4 and 9 and offline questions with limits 5 and 10. The code sorts the questions by limit, merges every edge below the limit into the groups, and then answers each question with one group check. Online questions arrive one at a time, and each answer is due before the next question is visible. Reordering is then impossible, so the structure must answer any question from stored state alone. The update/query ratio matters as well. This lesson has no updates, so any work moved into the constructor pays back across all questions.

Verify What A Summary Only Suggests

Some summaries produce a suspect and not an answer. A majority vote is the clearest case. It scans the values once and holds one candidate with a counter. A value equal to the candidate raises the counter, a different value lowers it, and a counter at zero adopts the current value as the new candidate. The candidate left after the scan is the survivor. It equals the true majority value when one exists, and otherwise it is an arbitrary element. In that case the position index serves a second purpose: it counts the suspect exactly, and the answer is the suspect only when that count meets the requirement.

State

Name The Facts That Choose The Plan

Four facts decide the plan before the first line of code.

  • Updates counts the calls that change the data after construction; here it is zero.
  • Queries is the number of questions, written q, and it sets how much constructor work pays back.
  • Arrival says whether every question is known at the start or each is visible only when asked.
  • Endpoints states whether a range is closed, [left, right], or half-open, [left, right + 1), and the code states which one before any comparison.

A count that can exceed the int range or a sum of lengths uses long. The counts in this lesson stay below the array length, so int is enough.

Walkthrough

Count Entries Between Two Binary Searches

Two Searches Over One List

Take the array 3, 1, 3, 2, 3, 1, 3 and the question for value 3 in the closed range [1, 5]. The index lists value 3 at positions 0, 2, 4 and 6. The two searches below look for the first stored position that is at least 1 and then the first that is at least 6, which is right + 1.

The first search ends at 1, because only position 0 is below 1. The second ends at 3, because positions 0, 2 and 4 are below 6. The count is 3 - 1 = 2, which matches positions 2 and 4. The step to watch is the second search, where the target is 6 and not 5. A search for 5 would give the wrong count whenever position 5 is stored, because that position belongs in the range. The first trace shows both searches step by step.

Second Example With Boundary Hits

The second trace uses value positions 1, 4, 5, 8 and 9 and the closed range [5, 8]. A stored position that equals left must count, so the first search stops at index 2 and does not skip past position 5. A stored position that equals right must count too. The second search targets 9 and stops at index 4, which leaves position 8 inside the count. The result is 4 - 2 = 2.

Code

Build The Index And Answer By Counting

final class PositionIndex {
    private final java.util.HashMap<Integer, int[]> where = new java.util.HashMap<>();

    PositionIndex(int[] arr) {
        java.util.HashMap<Integer, Integer> size = new java.util.HashMap<>();
        for (int x : arr) size.merge(x, 1, Integer::sum);          // count occurrences per value
        java.util.HashMap<Integer, Integer> used = new java.util.HashMap<>();
        for (java.util.Map.Entry<Integer, Integer> e : size.entrySet())
            where.put(e.getKey(), new int[e.getValue()]);           // exact-size list per value
        for (int i = 0; i < arr.length; i++)
            where.get(arr[i])[used.merge(arr[i], 1, Integer::sum) - 1] = i; // ascending by construction
    }

    // First list index whose stored position is at least x; equals p.length when none is.
    private static int firstAtLeast(int[] p, int x) {
        int lo = 0, hi = p.length;
        while (lo < hi) {
            int mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2;
            if (p[mid] < x) lo = mid + 1; else hi = mid;
        }
        return lo;
    }

    int count(int left, int right, int value) {
        int[] p = where.get(value);
        if (p == null) return 0;                                    // value never occurs
        return firstAtLeast(p, right + 1) - firstAtLeast(p, left); // closed [left, right]
    }
}

The constructor takes O(n) time and stores n positions in total. Each question costs O(log n) and allocates nothing. The result stays an int because a count never exceeds the array length. The midpoint formula lo + (hi - lo) / 2 avoids overflow when lo + hi would pass the int range.

When to use it

Choose A Plan From The Call Contract

Read The Contract Before Choosing

Move work into the constructor when many questions follow it on data that stays fixed. Compare the one-time cost against the saving per question times the number of questions. State the arrival rule too. If every question is known before the first answer, sorting the questions is a legal plan. If an answer is due immediately, the plan must keep a structure that answers any question alone.

The Invariant Of A Summary

The invariant of a summary is that it contains enough information for every allowed question without returning to the original input. The position index keeps that invariant for frequency questions, because the count of a value in any range is the difference of two ranks. A summary that fails the invariant needs a second step, such as verification of a suspect.

Where The Shortcut Misleads

A false friend of this design is the assumption that a cheap candidate is also a correct answer. A majority vote over a range always returns a survivor, even when no value reaches the required count. Skipping the verification step returns a plausible but wrong value. A second false friend is offline sorting under an online contract. Sorted questions give a fast algorithm only when the API reveals all questions in advance.

Exercises

Exercises

Basic

Range Frequency Queries

LeetCode 2080

Problem Specification. Design a class RangeFreqQuery. Its constructor receives an integer array arr. The method query(left, right, value) returns how many indices i with left <= i <= right satisfy arr[i] == value. Both endpoints are inclusive, so the range is closed. The array never changes after construction, so there are zero updates and many queries. The queries arrive one at a time and are not known in advance, so the design must answer each from stored state.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • n is arr.length with 1 <= n <= 10^5.
  • Values satisfy 1 <= arr[i] <= 10^4, and value has the same range.
  • Indices satisfy 0 <= left <= right < n.
  • Calls are one constructor call and up to 10^5 queries, with no updates.
  • Return value is an int count, and the array is not modified.

Example 1. Input arr = [3,1,3,2,3,1,3] and query(1, 5, 3), output 2, because value 3 sits at indices 2 and 4 in that range.

Example 2. Input the same array and query(0, 6, 9), output 0, because 9 never occurs.

Prerequisites. Binary search on a sorted array and the lower-bound idea from this lesson.

What This Exercise Changes. The constructor now stores a summary of the whole array, so each query stops reading the array.

Hint

If the positions of each value form a sorted list, how many list entries lie between left and right?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The constructor packs each pair of value and position into one long key, value * 2^20 + position, and sorts all keys. Equal values then form one block ordered by position, so the block of value v is the position index of v. A closed query [left, right] becomes the half-open key range from v * 2^20 + left up to but excluding v * 2^20 + right + 1. The count is the difference of two lower-bound searches over the whole key array. The shift of 20 bits exceeds the largest right + 1, which is at most 100,000, so one value's keys never overlap the next value's keys. The multiplication happens in long because the shift constant is a long, and the harness asserts that the largest key exceeds the int range. The harness checks both examples and thousands of random arrays against a direct scan.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(n log n) for the constructor sort and O(log n) per query, because each query runs two binary searches over n keys.
  • Space is O(n) for the key array, and each query allocates nothing.
import java.util.Arrays;
import java.util.Random;

public final class RangeFrequencyQueries {
    /** Shift that separates the value part from the position part inside one sorted key. */
    private static final long SHIFT = 1L << 20;
    /** Sorted keys value * SHIFT + position; all positions of one value are adjacent and ascending. */
    private final long[] keys;

    /**
     * Builds the index once from the array.
     * Time: O(n log n) to sort n keys. Space: O(n) for the key array.
     * Invariant: keys is sorted, so equal values form one block ordered by position.
     */
    RangeFrequencyQueries(int[] arr) {
        // One key per array slot, so the index has exactly n entries.
        keys = new long[arr.length];
        // Pack each (value, position) pair; the multiplication runs in long because SHIFT is long.
        for (int i = 0; i < arr.length; i++) keys[i] = arr[i] * SHIFT + i;
        // Sorting groups equal values together and orders their positions ascending.
        Arrays.sort(keys);
    }

    /**
     * Returns the index of the first key that is at least target (keys.length when none is).
     * Time: O(log n). Space: O(1).
     * Invariant: every key before lo is below target and every key from hi on is at least target.
     */
    private int lowerBound(long target) {
        // The search range is the half-open interval [lo, hi).
        int lo = 0, hi = keys.length;
        // Each pass halves the range, which gives the logarithmic cost.
        while (lo < hi) {
            // The midpoint form avoids int overflow for large lengths.
            int mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2;
            // A key below target proves the answer lies right of mid.
            if (keys[mid] < target) lo = mid + 1;
            // Otherwise mid is a candidate and the answer lies at mid or left of it.
            else hi = mid;
        }
        // lo equals hi, the first index whose key is not below target.
        return lo;
    }

    /**
     * Counts positions p with left <= p <= right (closed) where arr[p] == value.
     * Time: O(log n). Space: O(1).
     * Invariant: the answer is the number of keys in the half-open key range [value, left) to [value, right + 1).
     */
    int query(int left, int right, int value) {
        // The closed lower end becomes the first key of the block that can count.
        long from = value * SHIFT + left;
        // The closed upper end right becomes the half-open end right + 1.
        long to = value * SHIFT + right + 1;
        // Two searches give the block boundaries; their difference is the count.
        return lowerBound(to) - lowerBound(from);
    }

    static int brute(int[] a, int l, int r, int v) {
        // Oracle: scan the closed range directly.
        int c = 0;
        for (int i = l; i <= r; i++) if (a[i] == v) c++;
        return c;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1 from the lesson text.
        RangeFrequencyQueries s = new RangeFrequencyQueries(new int[] {3, 1, 3, 2, 3, 1, 3});
        if (s.query(1, 5, 3) != 2) throw new AssertionError("example 1");
        // Example 2: a value that never occurs gives 0.
        if (s.query(0, 6, 9) != 0) throw new AssertionError("example 2");
        // The packed key of the largest value exceeds the int range, so long arithmetic is required.
        if (10_000 * SHIFT <= Integer.MAX_VALUE) throw new AssertionError("long range");
        // Random inputs compared with the scan oracle for every query.
        Random rnd = new Random(7);
        for (int t = 0; t < 3000; t++) {
            int n = 1 + rnd.nextInt(30);
            int[] a = new int[n];
            for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) a[i] = 1 + rnd.nextInt(5);
            RangeFrequencyQueries q = new RangeFrequencyQueries(a);
            for (int k = 0; k < 20; k++) {
                int l = rnd.nextInt(n), r = l + rnd.nextInt(n - l), v = 1 + rnd.nextInt(6);
                if (q.query(l, r, v) != brute(a, l, r, v)) throw new AssertionError("random mismatch");
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Variation

Majority Element In Subarray

LeetCode 1157

Problem Specification. Design a class MajorityChecker. Its constructor receives an integer array arr. The method query(left, right, threshold) returns a value that occurs at least threshold times among indices left through right inclusive, or -1 when no such value exists. The contract guarantees 2 * threshold > right - left + 1, so at most one value can qualify. The array never changes. The queries arrive one at a time and are not known in advance.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • n is arr.length with 1 <= n <= 2 * 10^4.
  • Values satisfy 1 <= arr[i] <= 2 * 10^4.
  • Indices satisfy 0 <= left <= right < n.
  • Threshold satisfies 2 * threshold > right - left + 1 and threshold <= right - left + 1.
  • Calls are one constructor call and up to 10^4 queries, with no updates.

Example 1. Input arr = [1,1,2,2,1,1] and query(0, 5, 4), output 1, because value 1 occurs four times.

Example 2. Input the same array and query(0, 3, 3), output -1, because values 1 and 2 each occur twice.

Prerequisites. The range frequency exercise above and a segment tree built over array leaves.

What This Exercise Changes. Counting by position lists alone is not enough, because the value to count is unknown. A merge-friendly survivor supplies the one value to verify.

Hint

A range can merge two survivors from a majority vote in constant time. What must you check before you trust the survivor?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. A segment tree stores at each node a survivor pair of a candidate and a count, merged so that equal candidates add and different candidates cancel. If a strict majority of the node range exists, it is the survivor, which is the invariant. A query merges the survivors of the O(log n) nodes that cover the range and obtains one suspect. The suspect is only a suspect, so the code counts it exactly with two binary searches on its sorted position list and returns it only when the count reaches threshold. The contract 2 * threshold > length means any qualifying value is a strict majority, so a failed verification proves that no value qualifies. The harness checks three examples and random arrays with thresholds drawn from the allowed range.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(n) for the build and O(log n) per query, because the walk touches O(log n) nodes and verification runs two binary searches.
  • Space is O(n) for the tree and the position lists.
import java.util.Arrays;
import java.util.HashMap;
import java.util.Map;
import java.util.Random;

public final class MajorityChecker {
    /** Number of leaves, a power of two that is at least n. */
    private final int size;
    /** Segment tree node candidate (Boyer-Moore survivor) and its surviving count. */
    private final int[] cand, cnt;
    /** Ascending positions of each value. */
    private final Map<Integer, int[]> where = new HashMap<>();

    /**
     * Builds the candidate tree and the position lists.
     * Time: O(n). Space: O(n).
     * Invariant: each node holds a survivor pair; if a strict majority of the node range exists, it is the survivor.
     */
    MajorityChecker(int[] arr) {
        // Round the leaf count up to a power of two so the bottom-up layout works.
        int s = 1;
        while (s < arr.length) s <<= 1;
        size = s;
        cand = new int[2 * s];
        cnt = new int[2 * s];
        // Each leaf is its own survivor with count one; padding leaves keep count zero.
        for (int i = 0; i < arr.length; i++) { cand[s + i] = arr[i]; cnt[s + i] = 1; }
        // Each parent merges its two children, which costs O(1) per node.
        for (int v = s - 1; v >= 1; v--) merge(v, cand[2 * v], cnt[2 * v], cand[2 * v + 1], cnt[2 * v + 1]);
        // Count occurrences of every value to size the position lists exactly.
        Map<Integer, Integer> freq = new HashMap<>();
        for (int x : arr) freq.merge(x, 1, Integer::sum);
        // Allocate each list, then fill it in index order so it stays ascending.
        for (Map.Entry<Integer, Integer> e : freq.entrySet()) where.put(e.getKey(), new int[e.getValue()]);
        Map<Integer, Integer> fill = new HashMap<>();
        for (int i = 0; i < arr.length; i++) {
            int k = fill.merge(arr[i], 1, Integer::sum) - 1;
            where.get(arr[i])[k] = i;
        }
    }

    /** Writes the survivor of two survivor pairs into node v. Equal candidates add, different ones cancel. */
    private void merge(int v, int a, int ac, int b, int bc) {
        // Equal candidates add their counts.
        if (a == b) { cand[v] = a; cnt[v] = ac + bc; }
        // Different candidates cancel one for one, and the larger count keeps its candidate.
        else if (ac >= bc) { cand[v] = a; cnt[v] = ac - bc; }
        else { cand[v] = b; cnt[v] = bc - ac; }
    }

    /** Counts entries of a sorted list that fall in the closed range [l, r]. Time: O(log n). Space: O(1). */
    private static int countIn(int[] p, int l, int r) {
        // Entries below l and entries above r are both excluded by two lower bounds.
        return lower(p, r + 1) - lower(p, l);
    }

    /** First index in p whose entry is at least x. Invariant: entries before lo are below x. */
    private static int lower(int[] p, int x) {
        // Half-open search range [lo, hi).
        int lo = 0, hi = p.length;
        while (lo < hi) {
            int mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2;
            if (p[mid] < x) lo = mid + 1; else hi = mid;
        }
        return lo;
    }

    /**
     * Returns a value occurring at least threshold times in positions left..right (closed), or -1.
     * Contract: 2 * threshold > right - left + 1.
     * Time: O(log n) for the tree walk plus O(log n) for verification. Space: O(1).
     * Invariant: the survivor of the range is the only value that can reach a strict majority.
     */
    int query(int left, int right, int threshold) {
        // Accumulated survivor of the walked nodes.
        int ac = 0, acc = 0;
        // Bottom-up walk over the half-open leaf range [l, r).
        int l = left + size, r = right + size + 1;
        while (l < r) {
            // A right child at the left border is taken whole, then the border moves right.
            if ((l & 1) == 1) { int[] m = join(ac, acc, cand[l], cnt[l]); ac = m[0]; acc = m[1]; l++; }
            // A left child at the right border is taken whole after the border moves left.
            if ((r & 1) == 1) { --r; int[] m = join(ac, acc, cand[r], cnt[r]); ac = m[0]; acc = m[1]; }
            l >>= 1; r >>= 1;
        }
        // Survivor count zero means no value can be a strict majority.
        if (acc == 0) return -1;
        int[] p = where.get(ac);
        // Verification: the survivor is only a suspect until its real count reaches the threshold.
        return p != null && countIn(p, left, right) >= threshold ? ac : -1;
    }

    private static int[] join(int a, int ac, int b, int bc) {
        if (a == b) return new int[] {a, ac + bc};
        return ac >= bc ? new int[] {a, ac - bc} : new int[] {b, bc - ac};
    }

    static int brute(int[] a, int l, int r, int t) {
        // Oracle: count every value in the range with a map.
        Map<Integer, Integer> m = new HashMap<>();
        for (int i = l; i <= r; i++) m.merge(a[i], 1, Integer::sum);
        for (Map.Entry<Integer, Integer> e : m.entrySet()) if (e.getValue() >= t) return e.getKey();
        return -1;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Examples from the exercise text.
        MajorityChecker c = new MajorityChecker(new int[] {1, 1, 2, 2, 1, 1});
        if (c.query(0, 5, 4) != 1) throw new AssertionError("example 1");
        if (c.query(0, 3, 3) != -1) throw new AssertionError("example 2");
        if (c.query(2, 3, 2) != 2) throw new AssertionError("example 3");
        // Random inputs, thresholds restricted to the contract 2 * t > length.
        Random rnd = new Random(11);
        for (int t = 0; t < 4000; t++) {
            int n = 1 + rnd.nextInt(25);
            int[] a = new int[n];
            int range = 1 + rnd.nextInt(4);
            for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) a[i] = 1 + rnd.nextInt(range);
            MajorityChecker q = new MajorityChecker(a);
            for (int k = 0; k < 20; k++) {
                int l = rnd.nextInt(n), r = l + rnd.nextInt(n - l), len = r - l + 1;
                int th = len / 2 + 1 + rnd.nextInt(len - len / 2);
                if (q.query(l, r, th) != brute(a, l, r, th)) throw new AssertionError("random mismatch " + Arrays.toString(a));
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Edge Cases

No Majority Exists

Author exercise

Problem Specification. Design a class StrictMajority. Its constructor receives an integer array arr. The method query(left, right) returns the value that occurs strictly more than half the times in indices left through right inclusive. Formally, the count satisfies 2 * count > right - left + 1. When no value meets that rule, including an exact tie, it returns -1. The array never changes, and queries arrive one at a time. A cheap summary may suggest a value that does not qualify, so the method must reject it.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • n is arr.length with 1 <= n <= 10^5.
  • Values satisfy 0 <= arr[i] < 2^15, so -1 never collides with a stored value.
  • Indices satisfy 0 <= left <= right < n.
  • Calls are one constructor call and up to 10^5 queries, with no updates.
  • Return value is an int, and the array is not modified.

Example 1. Input arr = [1,2,3] and query(0, 2), output -1. The bit-vote suggests 3, but 3 occurs once.

Example 2. Input arr = [5,5,6,6] and query(0, 3), output -1 because the tie is not more than half. Input query(0, 2) on the same array gives output 5.

Prerequisites. The subarray majority exercise above.

What This Exercise Changes. The contract no longer promises a majority, so verification becomes the decisive step and not a formality.

Hint

For each bit, count the entries that have it set. What value do the majority bits spell, and how do you test that value exactly?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. For each bit position the constructor stores prefix counts of entries that have that bit set. For a range, a bit joins the candidate when more than half of the entries carry it. If a strict majority value exists, every one of its set bits appears in more than half the range. Every bit it lacks appears in at most half, so the candidate equals that value. When no majority exists, the candidate is some other number that may even be absent from the array. The query counts the candidate exactly through its position list and requires 2 * count > length. That rule rejects the input [1, 2, 3], where the candidate is 3 with count one. It also rejects an exact tie. The harness asserts the plausible candidate, the rejection, the tie, the Arrays.binarySearch insertion code used by the helper, and random arrays against a counting oracle.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(n * 15) for the build and O(15 + log n) per query, because one query reads 15 prefix pairs and runs two binary searches.
  • Space is O(n * 15) for the prefix tables plus O(n) for the position lists.
import java.util.Arrays;
import java.util.HashMap;
import java.util.Map;
import java.util.Random;

public final class NoMajorityExists {
    private static final int BITS = 15;
    /** ones[b][i] counts entries among the first i that have bit b set. */
    private final int[][] ones;
    /** Ascending positions of each value. */
    private final Map<Integer, int[]> where = new HashMap<>();

    /**
     * Builds bit prefix counts and position lists.
     * Time: O(n * BITS). Space: O(n * BITS).
     * Invariant: ones[b][i] equals the number of set bits b among arr[0..i-1].
     */
    NoMajorityExists(int[] arr) {
        ones = new int[BITS][arr.length + 1];
        // One prefix pass per bit; each cell extends the previous prefix by one entry.
        for (int b = 0; b < BITS; b++)
            for (int i = 0; i < arr.length; i++) ones[b][i + 1] = ones[b][i] + ((arr[i] >> b) & 1);
        // Collect the positions of each value in index order.
        Map<Integer, Integer> freq = new HashMap<>();
        for (int x : arr) freq.merge(x, 1, Integer::sum);
        for (Map.Entry<Integer, Integer> e : freq.entrySet()) where.put(e.getKey(), new int[e.getValue()]);
        Map<Integer, Integer> fill = new HashMap<>();
        for (int i = 0; i < arr.length; i++) where.get(arr[i])[fill.merge(arr[i], 1, Integer::sum) - 1] = i;
    }

    /**
     * Builds the only value that could be a strict majority of positions left..right (closed).
     * Time: O(BITS). Space: O(1).
     * Invariant: if a strict majority value exists, each of its bits is set in more than half the range.
     */
    int candidate(int left, int right) {
        // Length of the closed range, kept as an int because it is at most n.
        int len = right - left + 1;
        int c = 0;
        for (int b = 0; b < BITS; b++) {
            // Number of entries with bit b set inside the range, from two prefix reads.
            int set = ones[b][right + 1] - ones[b][left];
            // The bit joins the candidate only when a strict majority of entries carry it.
            if (2 * set > len) c |= 1 << b;
        }
        return c;
    }

    /**
     * Returns the value occurring strictly more than half the range, or -1 when none does.
     * Time: O(BITS + log n). Space: O(1).
     * Invariant: the candidate is verified by its real count, so a plausible but wrong value never escapes.
     */
    int query(int left, int right) {
        int c = candidate(left, right);
        int[] p = where.get(c);
        // A candidate that never occurs in the array has no position list.
        if (p == null) return -1;
        // Real count of c inside the closed range, from two lower bounds.
        int count = insertion(p, right + 1) - insertion(p, left);
        // Strict majority means twice the count exceeds the length.
        return 2 * count > right - left + 1 ? c : -1;
    }

    /** Index of the first entry at least x; binarySearch gives a negative insertion code when absent. */
    private static int insertion(int[] p, int x) {
        int i = Arrays.binarySearch(p, x);
        // Positions are distinct, so a hit is the unique first entry and a miss decodes to -(i) - 1.
        return i >= 0 ? i : -i - 1;
    }

    static int brute(int[] a, int l, int r) {
        Map<Integer, Integer> m = new HashMap<>();
        for (int i = l; i <= r; i++) m.merge(a[i], 1, Integer::sum);
        for (Map.Entry<Integer, Integer> e : m.entrySet()) if (2 * e.getValue() > r - l + 1) return e.getKey();
        return -1;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Plausible but insufficient candidate: bits of 1, 2, 3 produce 3, which occurs once in three slots.
        NoMajorityExists s = new NoMajorityExists(new int[] {1, 2, 3});
        if (s.candidate(0, 2) != 3) throw new AssertionError("candidate");
        if (s.query(0, 2) != -1) throw new AssertionError("rejected");
        // An exact half is not more than half.
        NoMajorityExists t = new NoMajorityExists(new int[] {5, 5, 6, 6});
        if (t.query(0, 3) != -1) throw new AssertionError("tie");
        // A real majority passes verification.
        if (t.query(0, 2) != 5) throw new AssertionError("majority");
        // Arrays.binarySearch returns -(insertion point) - 1 for a missing key.
        if (Arrays.binarySearch(new int[] {2, 4}, 3) != -2) throw new AssertionError("binarySearch code");
        // Random inputs against the counting oracle.
        Random rnd = new Random(5);
        for (int k = 0; k < 4000; k++) {
            int n = 1 + rnd.nextInt(25);
            int[] a = new int[n];
            int range = 1 + rnd.nextInt(5);
            for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) a[i] = rnd.nextInt(range + 1) * 3;
            NoMajorityExists q = new NoMajorityExists(a);
            for (int j = 0; j < 20; j++) {
                int l = rnd.nextInt(n), r = l + rnd.nextInt(n - l);
                if (q.query(l, r) != brute(a, l, r)) throw new AssertionError("random mismatch " + Arrays.toString(a));
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Pattern Recognition

Edge Length Limited Paths II

LeetCode 1724

Problem Specification. Design a class LimitedPaths. Its constructor receives n vertices numbered 0 to n - 1 and an undirected edge list, where each edge is {u, v, weight}. The method query(p, q, limit) returns true when some path from p to q uses only edges with weight strictly less than limit, and false otherwise. Parallel edges and self-loops may appear. The graph never changes. Queries arrive one at a time and are not known in advance, so sorting the queries by limit is not available. Contrast this with the offline task, where all queries are known and a union-find processes them in sorted order.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • n satisfies 2 <= n <= 10^4, and the edge count satisfies 0 <= E <= 10^5.
  • Weights and limits are int values with 1 <= weight, limit <= 10^9.
  • Vertices satisfy 0 <= p, q < n, and p may equal q.
  • Calls are one constructor call and up to 10^4 queries, with no updates.
  • Return value is a boolean, and no path sum is computed.

Example 1. Input four vertices, edges {0,1,2}, {1,2,4}, {0,2,9} and query(0, 2, 5), output true through the two light edges.

Example 2. Input the same graph and query(0, 2, 4), output false because the edge of weight 4 is not strictly less than 4. Input query(0, 3, 100) gives output false because vertex 3 has no edges.

Prerequisites. Union-find, Kruskal's minimum spanning tree and binary lifting.

What This Exercise Changes. The summary changes from a sorted order of queries to a minimum spanning forest, because the API hides future queries.

Hint

What is the smallest possible heaviest edge over all paths between two vertices, and which tree stores it?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The offline task sorts queries by limit, unions every edge strictly below the limit and checks whether p and q share a root. That plan needs every query before the first answer, so an online API cannot use it. The online plan builds a minimum spanning forest with Kruskal's algorithm. The invariant is that the heaviest edge on the forest path between two vertices is the smallest possible heaviest edge over all paths between them. Binary lifting tables store the ancestor and the heaviest edge for each jump of 2^k steps. A query answers false when the roots differ and otherwise lifts both endpoints to their meeting point while tracking the heaviest edge. The answer is true exactly when that edge is strictly below limit. The solution also contains the offline method, and the harness compares online answers, offline answers and a rebuild-from-scratch oracle on random graphs with parallel edges and self-loops.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(E log E) for sorting edges plus O(n log n) for the tables, and O(log n) per query because each lift uses at most log n jumps.
  • Space is O(n log n) for the two lifting tables, plus O(E) for the sorted copy of the edge list during construction.
import java.util.ArrayDeque;
import java.util.Arrays;
import java.util.Comparator;
import java.util.Random;

public final class EdgeLimitedPathsOnline {
    private final int[][] up, mx;
    private final int[] depth, root;
    private final int log;

    private static int find(int[] d, int x) {
        // Path halving keeps the trees shallow; each step points x at its grandparent.
        while (d[x] != x) { d[x] = d[d[x]]; x = d[x]; }
        return x;
    }

    /**
     * Builds the minimum spanning forest, then binary lifting tables of ancestors and heaviest edges.
     * Time: O(E log E + n log n). Space: O(n log n).
     * Invariant: p and q are joined by edges all below limit exactly when the forest path between them has maximum edge below limit.
     */
    EdgeLimitedPathsOnline(int n, int[][] edges) {
        // Kruskal order: ascending weight, compared without subtraction.
        int[][] sorted = edges.clone();
        Arrays.sort(sorted, Comparator.comparingInt(e -> e[2]));
        int[] dsu = new int[n];
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) dsu[i] = i;
        // Forest adjacency as linked arrays: head per vertex, next/to/w per directed half-edge.
        int[] head = new int[n], nxt = new int[2 * n], to = new int[2 * n], w = new int[2 * n];
        Arrays.fill(head, -1);
        int h = 0;
        for (int[] e : sorted) {
            int a = find(dsu, e[0]), b = find(dsu, e[1]);
            // An edge between two different components joins the forest; otherwise a lighter path already exists.
            if (a != b) {
                dsu[a] = b;
                to[h] = e[1]; w[h] = e[2]; nxt[h] = head[e[0]]; head[e[0]] = h++;
                to[h] = e[0]; w[h] = e[2]; nxt[h] = head[e[1]]; head[e[1]] = h++;
            }
        }
        // Smallest table height with 2^log >= n, at least 1.
        int lg = 1;
        while ((1 << lg) < n) lg++;
        log = lg;
        up = new int[lg][n];
        mx = new int[lg][n];
        depth = new int[n];
        root = new int[n];
        boolean[] seen = new boolean[n];
        // Breadth-first walk from each unseen vertex roots one tree per component.
        for (int s = 0; s < n; s++) {
            if (seen[s]) continue;
            ArrayDeque<Integer> dq = new ArrayDeque<>();
            dq.add(s); seen[s] = true; up[0][s] = s; mx[0][s] = 0; root[s] = s;
            while (!dq.isEmpty()) {
                int u = dq.poll();
                for (int e = head[u]; e != -1; e = nxt[e]) {
                    int v = to[e];
                    if (seen[v]) continue;
                    seen[v] = true; root[v] = s; depth[v] = depth[u] + 1;
                    up[0][v] = u; mx[0][v] = w[e];
                    dq.add(v);
                }
            }
        }
        // Level k jumps 2^k parents and keeps the heaviest edge on that jump.
        for (int k = 1; k < lg; k++)
            for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) {
                int mid = up[k - 1][v];
                up[k][v] = up[k - 1][mid];
                mx[k][v] = Math.max(mx[k - 1][v], mx[k - 1][mid]);
            }
    }

    /**
     * Returns true when some path from p to q uses only edges with weight strictly below limit.
     * Time: O(log n) per call. Space: O(1).
     * Invariant: best is the heaviest edge seen on the climb so far.
     */
    boolean query(int p, int q, int limit) {
        // Different trees mean no path exists at all.
        if (root[p] != root[q]) return false;
        int best = 0;
        int u = p, v = q;
        // Lift the deeper endpoint to the depth of the other one.
        if (depth[u] < depth[v]) { int t = u; u = v; v = t; }
        int diff = depth[u] - depth[v];
        for (int k = 0; k < log; k++)
            if (((diff >> k) & 1) == 1) { best = Math.max(best, mx[k][u]); u = up[k][u]; }
        if (u != v) {
            // Lift both endpoints while their ancestors differ, so they stop just below the meeting vertex.
            for (int k = log - 1; k >= 0; k--)
                if (up[k][u] != up[k][v]) {
                    best = Math.max(best, Math.max(mx[k][u], mx[k][v]));
                    u = up[k][u]; v = up[k][v];
                }
            // One last edge on each side reaches the meeting vertex.
            best = Math.max(best, Math.max(mx[0][u], mx[0][v]));
        }
        // The minimum possible heaviest edge over all p-q paths is best; the strict bound compares against limit.
        return best < limit;
    }

    /** Offline contrast: sorts the queries by limit and grows a union-find; needs every query in advance. */
    static boolean[] offline(int n, int[][] edges, int[][] queries) {
        int[][] es = edges.clone();
        Arrays.sort(es, Comparator.comparingInt(e -> e[2]));
        Integer[] order = new Integer[queries.length];
        for (int i = 0; i < order.length; i++) order[i] = i;
        Arrays.sort(order, Comparator.comparingInt(i -> queries[i][2]));
        int[] d = new int[n];
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) d[i] = i;
        boolean[] ans = new boolean[queries.length];
        int ptr = 0;
        for (int qi : order) {
            // Union exactly the edges strictly below this query's limit.
            while (ptr < es.length && es[ptr][2] < queries[qi][2]) {
                d[find(d, es[ptr][0])] = find(d, es[ptr][1]);
                ptr++;
            }
            ans[qi] = find(d, queries[qi][0]) == find(d, queries[qi][1]);
        }
        return ans;
    }

    static boolean brute(int n, int[][] edges, int p, int q, int limit) {
        // Oracle: rebuild a union-find from only the edges below limit.
        int[] d = new int[n];
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) d[i] = i;
        for (int[] e : edges) if (e[2] < limit) d[find(d, e[0])] = find(d, e[1]);
        return find(d, p) == find(d, q);
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example: path 0-1 (weight 2), 1-2 (weight 4), plus a heavy shortcut 0-2 (weight 9).
        int[][] ex = {{0, 1, 2}, {1, 2, 4}, {0, 2, 9}};
        EdgeLimitedPathsOnline s = new EdgeLimitedPathsOnline(4, ex);
        if (!s.query(0, 2, 5)) throw new AssertionError("example 1");
        if (s.query(0, 2, 4)) throw new AssertionError("strict bound");
        if (s.query(0, 3, 100)) throw new AssertionError("isolated vertex");
        // Random graphs, with duplicate edges and self-loops allowed, against the oracle and the offline method.
        Random rnd = new Random(3);
        for (int t = 0; t < 1500; t++) {
            int n = 1 + rnd.nextInt(9), m = rnd.nextInt(15);
            int[][] edges = new int[m][];
            for (int i = 0; i < m; i++) edges[i] = new int[] {rnd.nextInt(n), rnd.nextInt(n), 1 + rnd.nextInt(8)};
            EdgeLimitedPathsOnline g = new EdgeLimitedPathsOnline(n, edges);
            int qn = 15;
            int[][] qs = new int[qn][];
            for (int i = 0; i < qn; i++) qs[i] = new int[] {rnd.nextInt(n), rnd.nextInt(n), 1 + rnd.nextInt(10)};
            boolean[] off = offline(n, edges, qs);
            for (int i = 0; i < qn; i++) {
                boolean want = brute(n, edges, qs[i][0], qs[i][1], qs[i][2]);
                if (g.query(qs[i][0], qs[i][1], qs[i][2]) != want) throw new AssertionError("online mismatch");
                if (off[i] != want) throw new AssertionError("offline mismatch");
            }
        }
    }
}verified

Dynamic Graph Queries

ProblemSimple approachBottleneckKey ideaStateWalkthroughCodeWhen to use itExercises
Problem

Why Quoted Delivery Costs Go Stale

A logistics service quotes the cheapest delivery cost between two depots. Dispatchers also add new routes during the day, and a route can open a shortcut that makes an earlier quote too high. If the service caches quotes, a customer sees a price that no longer matches the network. If the service recomputes every quote from nothing, a busy morning of lookups slows the whole system.

Both problems come from one object that owns two jobs. It accepts new edges, and it answers shortest-path questions about the edges accepted so far. This lesson asks how such an object keeps every answer correct after each new edge without paying the full price on every call. The answer depends on how many edges arrive compared with how many questions arrive.

Simple approach

Running Dijkstra On Every Query

The direct design stores the edges in adjacency lists and runs Dijkstra from the source on every question. An added edge only appends to a list, so it costs O(1) and leaves no stale state. A query always sees every accepted edge, because it reads the lists at that moment.

static long shortestPath(java.util.List<long[]>[] adj, int from, int to) {
    long[] dist = new long[adj.length];
    java.util.Arrays.fill(dist, Long.MAX_VALUE);          // Long.MAX_VALUE marks unreached nodes
    dist[from] = 0;
    java.util.PriorityQueue<long[]> pq =
        new java.util.PriorityQueue<>((x, y) -> Long.compare(x[0], y[0])); // compare, never subtract
    pq.add(new long[] {0, from});
    while (!pq.isEmpty()) {
        long[] top = pq.poll();
        int u = (int) top[1];
        if (top[0] > dist[u]) continue;                   // skip an outdated queue entry
        for (long[] e : adj[u]) {                         // e = {neighbor, cost}
            int v = (int) e[0];
            if (dist[u] + e[1] < dist[v]) {
                dist[v] = dist[u] + e[1];
                pq.add(new long[] {dist[v], v});
            }
        }
    }
    return dist[to] == Long.MAX_VALUE ? -1 : dist[to];
}

This is correct for any mix of calls. Each query pays for one full search.

Bottleneck

Paying A Full Search Per Question

Predict first: A network has 100 nodes and about 9,900 edges, and the service answers 100,000 queries. How many edge examinations does the repeated search perform, roughly?

About one billion. Each query examines every edge reachable from the source, so 100,000 queries times about 10,000 edges gives 10^9 examinations, and the heap adds a logarithmic factor on top.

One search costs O((V + E) log V) for V nodes and E edges. A workload with Q queries therefore costs O(Q * (V + E) log V). On a dense graph, E is close to V squared, so each query costs about V squared times a logarithm, even when the query repeats a pair that the service already answered a moment ago.

The repeated work is the bottleneck. Between two queries only a few edges change, yet the search forgets everything it learned. Rebuilding a table of all pairs from scratch after every edge is no better. Floyd-Warshall costs O(V^3), so one rebuild per added edge costs O(V^3) per edge and wastes the old table.

Key idea

Keep All Pairs And Repair On Insert

Store Every Pair Once

Keep one distance matrix dist where dist[i][j] holds the cheapest known cost from i to j. Fill it once with Floyd-Warshall in O(V^3). A query then reads one cell in O(1). The matrix holds the invariant that each cell equals the true shortest cost over all edges accepted so far.

Repair With One Edge

A new edge a -> b with cost w can improve a pair (i, j) only by using that edge exactly once. The cheapest such route is dist[i][a] + w + dist[b][j]. This update is incremental relaxation: replace dist[i][j] by the smaller of its old value and that sum, for every pair. The loop touches V squared cells, so an insertion costs O(V^2) and the invariant holds again. One pass suffices because a shortest path never needs to cross the same new edge twice when costs are not negative.

Compare By The Update-To-Query Ratio

The update-to-query ratio decides which design wins. Dijkstra per query costs nothing on insert and a full search per question. The matrix costs O(V^3) once, O(V^2) per insert and O(1) per query. With few queries, the search wins because it never builds the table. With many queries, the matrix wins. Whether the calls are known in advance also matters: when all inserts precede all queries, one Floyd-Warshall rebuild replaces every repair.

State

Name The State And The Workload

Four quantities control the design, and each needs a fixed meaning before any comparison.

  • dist[i][j] is a long holding the cheapest known cost from i to j, or INF when no path exists.
  • INF is Long.MAX_VALUE / 4, so the sum of two INF values and one cost never overflows.
  • a, b, w are the source, target and non-negative cost of the inserted edge.
  • U and Q are the numbers of inserts and queries, and they fix the update-to-query ratio.

A query returns -1 when dist[from][to] is still INF. The diagonal dist[i][i] starts at 0.

Walkthrough

Follow Edge Insertions Through Queries

The first trace uses four nodes with edges 0 to 1 at cost 5, 1 to 2 at cost 4 and 2 to 3 at cost 1. The cells are the nodes, and each step shows the distances from node 0 to every node, with INF for unreachable nodes. The first query from 0 to 3 reads the table and returns 10. Inserting edge 0 to 2 at cost 6 gives route 0, 2, 3 with cost 6 + 1 = 7, so the repair lowers dist[0][2] from 9 to 6 and dist[0][3] from 10 to 7. Each step reports how many pairs the repair changed in the whole table, and how many of them lie in the displayed row 0. The next query returns 7. Inserting edge 0 to 3 at cost 9 offers a route costlier than 7, so the repair changes no cell.

The second trace uses three nodes and a single edge 0 to 1 at cost 3. A query from 0 to 2 returns -1 because no path exists. Inserting edge 1 to 2 at cost 2 creates the path with cost 5. A second edge between the same pair at cost 7 is a duplicate that costs more, so nothing changes. An edge 0 to 2 at cost 5 ties the stored value, and the strict comparison leaves it alone. A final edge 0 to 2 at cost 4 improves the pair.

Code

Build The Table And Repair It

final class GraphIndex {
    private static final long INF = Long.MAX_VALUE / 4;
    private final int n;
    private final long[][] dist;

    GraphIndex(int n, int[][] edges) {
        this.n = n;
        dist = new long[n][n];
        for (long[] row : dist) java.util.Arrays.fill(row, INF);   // no path known yet
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) dist[i][i] = 0;                // a node reaches itself for free
        for (int[] e : edges) dist[e[0]][e[1]] = Math.min(dist[e[0]][e[1]], e[2]);
        for (int k = 0; k < n; k++)                                // allow k as an intermediate node
            for (int i = 0; i < n; i++)
                for (int j = 0; j < n; j++)
                    dist[i][j] = Math.min(dist[i][j], dist[i][k] + dist[k][j]);
    }

    void addEdge(int a, int b, long w) {
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++)                                // every source that reaches a
            for (int j = 0; j < n; j++)                            // every target reachable from b
                dist[i][j] = Math.min(dist[i][j], dist[i][a] + w + dist[b][j]);
    }

    long query(int from, int to) {
        return dist[from][to] >= INF ? -1 : dist[from][to];
    }
}

The constructor costs O(n^3) and each addEdge costs O(n^2). The array uses long, and INF leaves room for two additions without overflow. The result of an unreachable query is -1 and the stored INF never leaves the class.

When to use it

Choose A Design From The Workload

State The Invariant First

Every returned distance must reflect all accepted edges, so the invariant belongs to the object and not to one method. The constructor, the insert and the query all preserve it. A design is acceptable only if each public call leaves the stored table consistent with the accepted edges.

Pick By Counting Calls

Use repeated search when queries are rare, when the graph is sparse or when the node count is large. The table needs V squared longs, which is too much memory for a large graph. Use the table with incremental repair when both inserts and queries are frequent and V is small, as in a limit of a few hundred nodes. Use one rebuild when every insert is known before the first query.

Watch The False Friend

Re-running Floyd-Warshall after each insert looks safe and is a false friend, because it recomputes what the old table already knows and costs O(V^3) per insert. A second false friend is repair with removals. The repair formula handles only edges that get cheaper or appear, so deleting an edge or raising a cost forces a rebuild. Negative cycles also break the single-crossing argument, because a route that crosses the new edge repeatedly keeps getting cheaper.

Exercises

Exercises

Basic

Static Shortest-Path Calculator

Author exercise

Problem Specification. A directed graph has n nodes numbered 0 to n - 1 and a list of weighted edges. Each edge is a triple {from, to, cost}. Build a calculator once from the edge list. Then answer queries distance(a, b), which returns the minimum total cost of a directed path from a to b, or -1 when no path exists. The graph never changes after construction. The workload has zero updates and many queries, and all edges are known in advance.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • n is an int with 1 <= n <= 100.
  • Edges number at most n * (n - 1), with 0 <= cost <= 10^6, and repeated pairs are allowed.
  • Queries number at most 10^5, and a may equal b, which returns 0.
  • Return value is a long.

Example 1. Input n = 4, edges {0,1,5}, {1,2,4}, {2,3,1} and query distance(0, 3), output 10.

Example 2. Input the same graph and query distance(3, 0), output -1.

Prerequisites. Dijkstra and Floyd-Warshall from the shortest-path chapter.

What This Exercise Changes. Nothing mutates, so the calculator may spend O(n^3) once and answer from a table.

Hint

With many queries and no updates, which one-time table makes each query O(1)?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The graph never changes and queries dominate, so the calculator pays once for a table of all pairs. The constructor sets every cell to INF, sets the diagonal to 0, keeps the cheapest cost for repeated pairs and runs Floyd-Warshall. After the pass for intermediate node k, each cell holds the cheapest path that uses only intermediates from 0 to k. This invariant makes the final table exact.

A query reads one cell and maps INF to -1. Dijkstra from the source is the alternative and gives the same answers at O((V + E) log V) per query. The harness compares both with a Bellman-Ford oracle on random graphs, and INF = Long.MAX_VALUE / 4 keeps sums of costs safe from overflow.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(n^3) for construction and O(1) per query, because the triple loop touches n^3 cells and a query reads one.
  • Space is O(n^2), because the table stores one long per ordered pair.
import java.util.*;

public final class StaticCalculator {
    private static final long INF = Long.MAX_VALUE / 4; // large, yet two INF values plus a cost still fit in a long
    private final long[][] dist;

    /**
     * Builds the all-pairs table once from the edge list.
     * Time: O(n^3), because Floyd-Warshall visits every (k, i, j) triple.
     * Space: O(n^2), because the table holds one long per ordered pair.
     * Invariant: after round k, dist[i][j] is the cheapest cost using intermediates 0..k only.
     */
    StaticCalculator(int n, int[][] edges) {
        // Allocate the table; this is the O(n^2) memory of the design.
        dist = new long[n][n];
        // Mark every pair unreachable first.
        for (long[] row : dist) Arrays.fill(row, INF);
        // A node reaches itself at cost 0.
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) dist[i][i] = 0;
        // Keep only the cheapest cost for repeated pairs.
        for (int[] e : edges) dist[e[0]][e[1]] = Math.min(dist[e[0]][e[1]], e[2]);
        // Round k allows node k as an intermediate stop.
        for (int k = 0; k < n; k++)
            // Source of the candidate route.
            for (int i = 0; i < n; i++)
                // Target of the candidate route; the n^3 total comes from this nesting.
                for (int j = 0; j < n; j++)
                    // Take the cheaper of the old route and the route through k.
                    dist[i][j] = Math.min(dist[i][j], dist[i][k] + dist[k][j]);
    }

    /**
     * Reads one table cell.
     * Time: O(1). Space: O(1).
     * Invariant: the table is exact, so the stored value is the answer.
     */
    long distance(int a, int b) {
        // INF means no path, which the contract reports as -1.
        return dist[a][b] >= INF ? -1 : dist[a][b];
    }

    /**
     * Alternative design: one Dijkstra search per query over adjacency lists.
     * Time: O((V + E) log V) per call. Space: O(V + E).
     * Invariant: a node leaves the queue with its final distance because costs are non-negative.
     */
    static long dijkstra(int n, int[][] edges, int from, int to) {
        // Build adjacency lists of {target, cost}.
        List<long[]>[] adj = new List[n];
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) adj[i] = new ArrayList<>();
        for (int[] e : edges) adj[e[0]].add(new long[] {e[1], e[2]});
        // Distances start unreached.
        long[] d = new long[n];
        Arrays.fill(d, INF);
        d[from] = 0;
        // Heap ordered with Long.compare, never subtraction, so large values cannot overflow.
        PriorityQueue<long[]> pq = new PriorityQueue<>((x, y) -> Long.compare(x[0], y[0]));
        pq.add(new long[] {0, from});
        // Each pop settles the cheapest unsettled node.
        while (!pq.isEmpty()) {
            long[] top = pq.poll();
            int u = (int) top[1];
            // Skip an entry that a cheaper one already replaced.
            if (top[0] > d[u]) continue;
            // Try to improve each neighbor through u.
            for (long[] e : adj[u]) {
                int v = (int) e[0];
                // A strictly cheaper route updates the neighbor and queues it.
                if (d[u] + e[1] < d[v]) { d[v] = d[u] + e[1]; pq.add(new long[] {d[v], v}); }
            }
        }
        // Report -1 for unreachable targets.
        return d[to] >= INF ? -1 : d[to];
    }

    /** Brute-force oracle: Bellman-Ford with n rounds over the raw edge list. */
    static long oracle(int n, int[][] edges, int from, int to) {
        // Start with only the source known.
        long[] d = new long[n];
        Arrays.fill(d, INF);
        d[from] = 0;
        // A shortest path has at most n - 1 edges, so n rounds suffice.
        for (int round = 0; round < n; round++)
            // Relax every edge once per round.
            for (int[] e : edges) if (d[e[0]] < INF && d[e[0]] + e[2] < d[e[1]]) d[e[1]] = d[e[0]] + e[2];
        // Same -1 convention as the calculator.
        return d[to] >= INF ? -1 : d[to];
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Checks Example 1 and Example 2 from the problem statement.
        int[][] ex = {{0, 1, 5}, {1, 2, 4}, {2, 3, 1}};
        StaticCalculator c = new StaticCalculator(4, ex);
        if (c.distance(0, 3) != 10) throw new AssertionError("example 1");
        if (c.distance(3, 0) != -1) throw new AssertionError("example 2");
        // Checks a == b returns 0 and that sums near the cost limit stay exact in long.
        if (c.distance(2, 2) != 0) throw new AssertionError("self distance");
        int[][] big = {{0, 1, 1_000_000}, {1, 2, 1_000_000}};
        if (new StaticCalculator(3, big).distance(0, 2) != 2_000_000L) throw new AssertionError("long sum");
        // Checks Floyd-Warshall, Dijkstra and the oracle against each other on random graphs with repeated pairs.
        Random rnd = new Random(39);
        for (int t = 0; t < 400; t++) {
            int n = 1 + rnd.nextInt(7);
            int m = rnd.nextInt(2 * n + 3);
            int[][] edges = new int[m][];
            for (int i = 0; i < m; i++) edges[i] = new int[] {rnd.nextInt(n), rnd.nextInt(n), rnd.nextInt(20)};
            StaticCalculator s = new StaticCalculator(n, edges);
            for (int a = 0; a < n; a++)
                for (int b = 0; b < n; b++) {
                    long want = oracle(n, edges, a, b);
                    if (s.distance(a, b) != want) throw new AssertionError("floyd " + a + "->" + b);
                    if (dijkstra(n, edges, a, b) != want) throw new AssertionError("dijkstra " + a + "->" + b);
                }
        }
    }
}verified
Variation

Graph With Shortest Path Calculator

LeetCode 2642

Problem Specification. Design a graph object that is built from n and an initial edge list. It supports addEdge(a, b, w), which inserts a directed edge, and shortestPath(a, b), which returns the minimum path cost over all edges inserted so far, or -1 when none exists. Calls arrive one at a time, in unknown order, so the object cannot see the future. The update-to-query ratio is about one to one.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • n is an int with 1 <= n <= 100, and every edge cost is an int with 1 <= w <= 10^6.
  • addEdge is never called for a pair that already has an edge.
  • Calls number at most 100 of each kind.
  • Return value is a long.

Example 1. Input n = 4, edges {0,1,5}, {1,2,4}, {2,3,1}, then shortestPath(0, 3), addEdge(0, 2, 6), shortestPath(0, 3), output 10 and then 7.

Example 2. Input the same graph after addEdge(0, 2, 6), then addEdge(0, 3, 9) and shortestPath(0, 3), output 7.

Prerequisites. The static calculator exercise above.

What This Exercise Changes. Edges now arrive between queries, so the table must be repaired on insert and not only built once.

Hint

Which cells can an edge a -> b improve, and by what sum?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. Edges arrive between queries, so the table must stay exact after each insert. The design keeps the all-pairs table. A new edge a -> b with cost w improves a pair (i, j) only through the route i to a, the new edge, then b to j, because costs are positive and a shortest path never crosses the same edge twice. Taking the minimum of the old cell and that sum, for every pair, restores the invariant that every cell equals the true shortest cost over all accepted edges.

With about as many inserts as queries and at most 100 nodes, an insert at O(n^2) and a query at O(1) beat a search per query. The alternative DijkstraGraph appends to adjacency lists and searches on demand. The harness runs random interleaved calls on both and compares each answer with a Bellman-Ford oracle over the accepted edge list.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(n^3) to construct, O(n^2) per addEdge and O(1) per shortestPath, because an insert scans each pair once and a query reads one cell.
  • Space is O(n^2), because the table keeps one long per ordered pair.
import java.util.*;

public final class AddEdgeCalculator {
    private static final long INF = Long.MAX_VALUE / 4; // safe against overflow when two INF values are added
    private final int n;
    private final long[][] dist;

    /**
     * Creates the object and fills the table with Floyd-Warshall.
     * Time: O(n^3). Space: O(n^2).
     * Invariant: dist[i][j] is the true shortest cost over the edges accepted so far.
     */
    AddEdgeCalculator(int n, int[][] edges) {
        // Remember the node count for later inserts.
        this.n = n;
        dist = new long[n][n];
        // Start with no known path.
        for (long[] row : dist) Arrays.fill(row, INF);
        // Zero cost from a node to itself.
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) dist[i][i] = 0;
        // Load the initial edges.
        for (int[] e : edges) dist[e[0]][e[1]] = Math.min(dist[e[0]][e[1]], e[2]);
        // Intermediate node k, then source i, then target j: the n^3 cost.
        for (int k = 0; k < n; k++)
            for (int i = 0; i < n; i++)
                for (int j = 0; j < n; j++)
                    // Keep the cheaper of the direct cell and the route through k.
                    dist[i][j] = Math.min(dist[i][j], dist[i][k] + dist[k][j]);
    }

    /**
     * Inserts a directed edge and repairs the table.
     * Time: O(n^2), because every pair is examined once. Space: O(1).
     * Invariant: after the call, every cell is exact for the enlarged edge set.
     */
    void addEdge(int a, int b, long w) {
        // Every possible source of an improved route.
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++)
            // Every possible target; the pair count n^2 is the cost of one insert.
            for (int j = 0; j < n; j++)
                // The new edge appears once in the route: i to a, the edge, b to j.
                dist[i][j] = Math.min(dist[i][j], dist[i][a] + w + dist[b][j]);
    }

    /**
     * Answers one query from the table.
     * Time: O(1). Space: O(1).
     */
    long shortestPath(int from, int to) {
        // INF means unreachable, which maps to -1.
        return dist[from][to] >= INF ? -1 : dist[from][to];
    }

    /** Alternative: adjacency lists with Dijkstra per query; insert is O(1), query is O((V + E) log V). */
    static final class DijkstraGraph {
        private final int n;
        private final List<long[]>[] adj;

        @SuppressWarnings("unchecked")
        DijkstraGraph(int n, int[][] edges) {
            // One list per node.
            this.n = n;
            adj = new List[n];
            for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) adj[i] = new ArrayList<>();
            // Load the initial edges.
            for (int[] e : edges) addEdge(e[0], e[1], e[2]);
        }

        void addEdge(int a, int b, long w) {
            // Appending is O(1) and leaves no stale state.
            adj[a].add(new long[] {b, w});
        }

        long shortestPath(int from, int to) {
            // Fresh distances per query: this is the repeated work.
            long[] d = new long[n];
            Arrays.fill(d, INF);
            d[from] = 0;
            // Compare with Long.compare to avoid subtraction overflow.
            PriorityQueue<long[]> pq = new PriorityQueue<>((x, y) -> Long.compare(x[0], y[0]));
            pq.add(new long[] {0, from});
            // Settle nodes in order of cost.
            while (!pq.isEmpty()) {
                long[] top = pq.poll();
                int u = (int) top[1];
                // Skip outdated entries.
                if (top[0] > d[u]) continue;
                // Relax each outgoing edge.
                for (long[] e : adj[u]) {
                    int v = (int) e[0];
                    if (d[u] + e[1] < d[v]) { d[v] = d[u] + e[1]; pq.add(new long[] {d[v], v}); }
                }
            }
            // -1 when the target stays unreached.
            return d[to] >= INF ? -1 : d[to];
        }
    }

    /** Brute-force oracle: Bellman-Ford over the list of accepted edges. */
    static long oracle(int n, List<int[]> edges, int from, int to) {
        long[] d = new long[n];
        Arrays.fill(d, INF);
        d[from] = 0;
        // n rounds are enough for any simple shortest path.
        for (int r = 0; r < n; r++)
            for (int[] e : edges) if (d[e[0]] < INF && d[e[0]] + e[2] < d[e[1]]) d[e[1]] = d[e[0]] + e[2];
        return d[to] >= INF ? -1 : d[to];
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Checks the worked example: 10, then 7 after the shortcut, then 7 after a costlier edge.
        int[][] ex = {{0, 1, 5}, {1, 2, 4}, {2, 3, 1}};
        AddEdgeCalculator g = new AddEdgeCalculator(4, ex);
        if (g.shortestPath(0, 3) != 10) throw new AssertionError("before");
        g.addEdge(0, 2, 6);
        if (g.shortestPath(0, 3) != 7) throw new AssertionError("after shortcut");
        g.addEdge(0, 3, 9);
