        if (g.shortestPath(0, 3) != 7) throw new AssertionError("costlier edge");
        // Checks the maximum cost chain sums in long: 99 edges of 10^6 sum to 99,000,000 exactly.
        int[][] chain = new int[99][];
        for (int i = 0; i < 99; i++) chain[i] = new int[] {i, i + 1, 1_000_000};
        if (new AddEdgeCalculator(100, chain).shortestPath(0, 99) != 99_000_000L) throw new AssertionError("chain");
        // Checks interleaved random calls on both designs against the oracle (no duplicate pairs, as stated).
        Random rnd = new Random(2642);
        for (int t = 0; t < 300; t++) {
            int n = 2 + rnd.nextInt(6);
            boolean[][] used = new boolean[n][n];
            List<int[]> all = new ArrayList<>();
            for (int i = 0; i < n * 2; i++) {
                int a = rnd.nextInt(n), b = rnd.nextInt(n);
                if (a == b || used[a][b]) continue;
                used[a][b] = true;
                all.add(new int[] {a, b, 1 + rnd.nextInt(20)});
            }
            AddEdgeCalculator f = new AddEdgeCalculator(n, all.toArray(new int[0][]));
            DijkstraGraph dj = new DijkstraGraph(n, all.toArray(new int[0][]));
            for (int step = 0; step < 25; step++) {
                if (rnd.nextBoolean()) {
                    int a = rnd.nextInt(n), b = rnd.nextInt(n);
                    if (a == b || used[a][b]) continue;
                    used[a][b] = true;
                    int w = 1 + rnd.nextInt(20);
                    all.add(new int[] {a, b, w});
                    f.addEdge(a, b, w);
                    dj.addEdge(a, b, w);
                } else {
                    int a = rnd.nextInt(n), b = rnd.nextInt(n);
                    long want = oracle(n, all, a, b);
                    if (f.shortestPath(a, b) != want) throw new AssertionError("table " + a + "->" + b);
                    if (dj.shortestPath(a, b) != want) throw new AssertionError("dijkstra " + a + "->" + b);
                }
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Edge Cases

Duplicate And Dominated Edges

Author exercise

Problem Specification. Extend the graph object so that addEdge(a, b, w) may repeat an existing pair, add a self-loop a == b or add an edge that no shortest path uses. The method returns true exactly when at least one pair distance becomes strictly smaller, and false otherwise. Queries return the minimum path cost as before, or -1. The object must keep only the minimum useful cost, so a dominated edge never raises any distance. Calls arrive online, and the update-to-query ratio is about two to one.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • n is an int with 1 <= n <= 60, and every cost w is an int with 0 <= w <= 10^6.
  • Pairs may repeat, and zero-cost edges are allowed.
  • Calls number at most 200 of each kind.
  • Return value is boolean for addEdge and long for queries.

Example 1. Input n = 3, no edges, then addEdge(0, 1, 3), addEdge(0, 1, 7), addEdge(0, 1, 3), output true, false, false.

Example 2. Continuing from the final state of Example 1, where edge 0 to 1 costs 3, input addEdge(1, 2, 2), shortestPath(0, 2), addEdge(1, 1, 0), addEdge(0, 2, 4), output true, 5, false, true.

Prerequisites. The add-edge calculator exercise above.

What This Exercise Changes. The insert must detect that it changes nothing, and equal or larger costs must leave every cell untouched.

Hint

If the new edge does not beat the current dist[a][b], can any other pair improve through it?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. Repeated pairs, self-loops and unused edges all share one property: they do not beat the stored value. A new edge a -> b with cost w can improve some cell only if w < dist[a][b]. If w >= dist[a][b], every route through the new edge costs at least as much as the same route through the stored shortest path from a to b, so no cell changes and the method returns false without scanning. A self-loop has dist[a][a] = 0 and a non-negative cost, so it falls into the same case.

Otherwise the repair scans every pair, counts strict decreases and returns whether the count is positive. Strict comparison makes ties report false. The harness compares the returned flag with a full Bellman-Ford recomputation before and after each insert, and it compares the whole table.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(1) for a dominated edge and O(n^2) for an improving edge, because the early check skips the scan and the repair visits every pair.
  • Space is O(n^2), because the table keeps one long per ordered pair and the object stores no edge list.
import java.util.*;

public final class DominatedEdges {
    private static final long INF = Long.MAX_VALUE / 4; // overflow-safe sentinel for unreachable pairs
    private final int n;
    private final long[][] dist;

    /**
     * Creates an empty graph on n nodes.
     * Time: O(n^2). Space: O(n^2).
     * Invariant: dist[i][j] is the cheapest cost over every accepted edge, and dist[i][i] stays 0.
     */
    DominatedEdges(int n) {
        // Allocate the table.
        this.n = n;
        dist = new long[n][n];
        // No edges yet, so every off-diagonal pair is unreachable.
        for (long[] row : dist) Arrays.fill(row, INF);
        // The diagonal is free.
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) dist[i][i] = 0;
    }

    /**
     * Inserts an edge and reports whether any pair distance became strictly smaller.
     * Time: O(1) when dominated, O(n^2) when improving. Space: O(1).
     * Invariant: equal or larger costs leave every cell untouched.
     */
    boolean addEdge(int a, int b, long w) {
        // A dominated edge (duplicate, self-loop or costlier) cannot help any route, so skip the scan.
        if (w >= dist[a][b]) return false;
        // Count cells that strictly decrease.
        int changed = 0;
        // Snapshot the two rows used by the formula, because the loop below overwrites cells of row a and column b.
        long[] toA = new long[n];
        long[] fromB = dist[b].clone();
        // Copy dist[i][a] for every source i.
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) toA[i] = dist[i][a];
        // Every source of an improved route.
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++)
            // Every target; this is the O(n^2) cost of an improving insert.
            for (int j = 0; j < n; j++) {
                // Route i to a, the new edge, then b to j.
                long cand = toA[i] + w + fromB[j];
                // Strict comparison keeps ties as unchanged.
                if (cand < dist[i][j]) { dist[i][j] = cand; changed++; }
            }
        // True exactly when at least one cell shrank.
        return changed > 0;
    }

    /**
     * Reads one cell.
     * Time: O(1). Space: O(1).
     */
    long shortestPath(int from, int to) {
        // Unreachable pairs report -1.
        return dist[from][to] >= INF ? -1 : dist[from][to];
    }

    /** Brute-force oracle: Bellman-Ford distances from every source over the raw edge list. */
    static long[][] oracleTable(int n, List<int[]> edges) {
        long[][] t = new long[n][n];
        // One search per source.
        for (int s = 0; s < n; s++) {
            Arrays.fill(t[s], INF);
            t[s][s] = 0;
            for (int r = 0; r < n; r++)
                for (int[] e : edges) if (t[s][e[0]] < INF && t[s][e[0]] + e[2] < t[s][e[1]]) t[s][e[1]] = t[s][e[0]] + e[2];
        }
        return t;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Checks Example 1: improve, worse duplicate, equal duplicate.
        DominatedEdges g = new DominatedEdges(3);
        if (!g.addEdge(0, 1, 3)) throw new AssertionError("first edge");
        if (g.addEdge(0, 1, 7)) throw new AssertionError("worse duplicate");
        if (g.addEdge(0, 1, 3)) throw new AssertionError("equal duplicate");
        // Checks Example 2: path 0 to 2, a zero-cost self-loop, then a strictly better direct edge.
        g.addEdge(1, 2, 2);
        if (g.shortestPath(0, 2) != 5) throw new AssertionError("path");
        if (g.addEdge(1, 1, 0)) throw new AssertionError("self-loop");
        if (!g.addEdge(0, 2, 4)) throw new AssertionError("better direct edge");
        // Checks a zero-cost edge is accepted and shortens distances.
        DominatedEdges z = new DominatedEdges(2);
        if (!z.addEdge(0, 1, 0) || z.shortestPath(0, 1) != 0) throw new AssertionError("zero cost");
        // Checks every insert against full recomputation: flag, and the whole table.
        Random rnd = new Random(77);
        for (int t = 0; t < 400; t++) {
            int n = 1 + rnd.nextInt(6);
            DominatedEdges d = new DominatedEdges(n);
            List<int[]> all = new ArrayList<>();
            for (int step = 0; step < 20; step++) {
                long[][] before = oracleTable(n, all);
                int a = rnd.nextInt(n), b = rnd.nextInt(n), w = rnd.nextInt(15);
                boolean got = d.addEdge(a, b, w);
                all.add(new int[] {a, b, w});
                long[][] after = oracleTable(n, all);
                // The expected flag is whether any oracle cell strictly decreased.
                boolean want = false;
                for (int i = 0; i < n; i++)
                    for (int j = 0; j < n; j++) {
                        if (after[i][j] < before[i][j]) want = true;
                        long have = d.shortestPath(i, j);
                        if (have != (after[i][j] >= INF ? -1 : after[i][j])) throw new AssertionError("table " + i + "->" + j);
                    }
                if (got != want) throw new AssertionError("flag at step " + step);
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Pattern Recognition

Update-Frequency Tradeoff

Author exercise

Problem Specification. A shortest-path service has n nodes, U edge insertions and Q queries. A flag removals is true when some call removes an edge or raises a cost. A flag batched is true when every update precedes the first query. A value memory is the number of long cells the service may store. Return one strategy name by applying the first matching rule in this order. The table needs n * n cells, so REPEATED_SEARCH when n * n > memory. The table build costs about n^3 and each repeated search costs about n^2 on a dense graph, so REPEATED_SEARCH also when Q < n. Next, REBUILD_ONCE when batched is true or U is 0, since one build then serves every query. Next, REBUILD_EACH when removals is true, because repair handles only cheaper edges. Otherwise REPAIR_TABLE.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • n is an int with 1 <= n <= 300.
  • U and Q are int values with 0 <= U, Q <= 10^5.
  • memory is a long with 0 <= memory <= 10^9.
  • Return value is one of the four names.

Example 1. Input n = 100, U = 10, Q = 5, removals = false, batched = false, memory = 10^6, output REPEATED_SEARCH.

Example 2. Input n = 100, U = 1000, Q = 100000, removals = false, batched = false, memory = 10^6, output REPAIR_TABLE.

Prerequisites. The three exercises above.

What This Exercise Changes. The choice is now a structure and not a number, so memory, removals, batching and the update-to-query ratio each select a different strategy.

Hint

Which fact rules out the table entirely, and which fact makes repair unsafe?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The task is a chain of tests, each tied to a fact from the lesson. The table needs n * n cells, so a memory limit below that rules it out. On a dense graph one search costs about n^2 and the build costs about n^3, so fewer than n queries never repay the build. When every update precedes the first query, or no update exists, one build serves all queries. Repair handles only edges that get cheaper or appear, so a removal forces a rebuild after each change. Everything else is the insert-only, online case that the incremental repair serves.

The first matching rule wins, so the order of the tests is part of the answer. The harness compares the picker with a second implementation that scores each rule in a table. It then backs the two key claims with real code. A repair after a cost increase gives a wrong distance that a rebuild corrects, and an insert-only stream of repairs matches a full rebuild. Counted relaxations confirm n^3 for the build and n^2 for one repair.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(1), because the method evaluates at most five comparisons.
  • Space is O(1), because it stores no collection.
import java.util.*;

public final class UpdateFrequency {
    /**
     * Picks a strategy name from the workload facts.
     * Time: O(1). Space: O(1).
     * Invariant: the first rule that matches decides, in the order memory, query count, batching, removals.
     */
    static String choose(int n, int u, int q, boolean removals, boolean batched, long memory) {
        // The table needs n * n cells; computing in long avoids overflow for any n.
        if ((long) n * n > memory) return "REPEATED_SEARCH";
        // Fewer than n queries never repay the n^3 build against n^2 per search.
        if (q < n) return "REPEATED_SEARCH";
        // One build serves all queries when updates come first or never come.
        if (batched || u == 0) return "REBUILD_ONCE";
        // Repair handles only cheaper or new edges, so removals need a rebuild per change.
        if (removals) return "REBUILD_EACH";
        // Insert-only and online: repair each insert in n^2.
        return "REPAIR_TABLE";
    }

    /** Second implementation: lists the rules as data and scans them in order. */
    static String oracle(int n, int u, int q, boolean removals, boolean batched, long memory) {
        String[] name = {"REPEATED_SEARCH", "REPEATED_SEARCH", "REBUILD_ONCE", "REBUILD_EACH", "REPAIR_TABLE"};
        boolean[] fires = {(long) n * n > memory, q < n, batched || u == 0, removals, true};
        for (int i = 0; i < fires.length; i++) if (fires[i]) return name[i];
        throw new AssertionError("unreachable");
    }

    static long relaxations; // counts inner-loop executions of the real code below

    /** Real Floyd-Warshall that counts its inner updates, to back the n^3 claim. */
    static long[][] floyd(int n, long[][] d) {
        for (int k = 0; k < n; k++) for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) for (int j = 0; j < n; j++) {
            relaxations++;
            d[i][j] = Math.min(d[i][j], d[i][k] + d[k][j]);
        }
        return d;
    }

    /** Real incremental repair that counts its inner updates, to back the n^2 claim. */
    static void repair(int n, long[][] d, int a, int b, long w) {
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) for (int j = 0; j < n; j++) {
            relaxations++;
            d[i][j] = Math.min(d[i][j], d[i][a] + w + d[b][j]);
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Checks Example 1 and Example 2.
        if (!choose(100, 10, 5, false, false, 1_000_000).equals("REPEATED_SEARCH")) throw new AssertionError("example 1");
        if (!choose(100, 1000, 100000, false, false, 1_000_000).equals("REPAIR_TABLE")) throw new AssertionError("example 2");
        // Checks each remaining name once.
        if (!choose(100, 1000, 100000, false, true, 1_000_000).equals("REBUILD_ONCE")) throw new AssertionError("batched");
        if (!choose(100, 1000, 100000, true, false, 1_000_000).equals("REBUILD_EACH")) throw new AssertionError("removals");
        if (!choose(300, 1000, 100000, false, false, 89_999).equals("REPEATED_SEARCH")) throw new AssertionError("memory");
        // Checks the boundary: memory exactly n * n allows the table.
        if (!choose(300, 1000, 100000, false, false, 90_000).equals("REPAIR_TABLE")) throw new AssertionError("memory edge");
        // Checks the picker against the rule-table oracle on random workloads.
        Random rnd = new Random(5);
        for (int t = 0; t < 20000; t++) {
            int n = 1 + rnd.nextInt(300), u = rnd.nextInt(3) == 0 ? 0 : rnd.nextInt(100001), q = rnd.nextInt(100001);
            long memory = rnd.nextInt(3) == 0 ? (long) n * n + rnd.nextInt(3) - 1 : rnd.nextInt(1_000_000_001);
            boolean removals = rnd.nextBoolean(), batched = rnd.nextBoolean();
            if (!choose(n, u, q, removals, batched, Math.max(0, memory)).equals(oracle(n, u, q, removals, batched, Math.max(0, memory))))
                throw new AssertionError("rule mismatch");
        }
        // Checks the counted work: n^3 for the build and n^2 per insert.
        int n = 9;
        long[][] d = new long[n][n];
        for (long[] row : d) Arrays.fill(row, Long.MAX_VALUE / 4);
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) d[i][i] = 0;
        relaxations = 0;
        floyd(n, d);
        if (relaxations != (long) n * n * n) throw new AssertionError("n^3 build");
        relaxations = 0;
        repair(n, d, 1, 2, 4);
        if (relaxations != (long) n * n) throw new AssertionError("n^2 repair");
        // Checks that repair equals a full rebuild on an insert-only stream.
        for (int t = 0; t < 200; t++) {
            int k = 2 + rnd.nextInt(6);
            long[][] inc = new long[k][k], all = new long[k][k];
            for (int i = 0; i < k; i++) { Arrays.fill(inc[i], Long.MAX_VALUE / 4); Arrays.fill(all[i], Long.MAX_VALUE / 4); inc[i][i] = 0; all[i][i] = 0; }
            for (int s = 0; s < 8; s++) {
                int a = rnd.nextInt(k), b = rnd.nextInt(k), w = rnd.nextInt(10);
                repair(k, inc, a, b, w);
                inc[a][b] = Math.min(inc[a][b], w);
                all[a][b] = Math.min(all[a][b], w);
                long[][] copy = new long[k][];
                for (int i = 0; i < k; i++) copy[i] = all[i].clone();
                floyd(k, copy);
                if (!Arrays.deepEquals(copy, inc)) throw new AssertionError("repair differs from rebuild");
                all = copy;
            }
        }
        // Checks that repair after a cost increase is wrong: edge 0->1 costs 1, then rises to 5.
        long[][] g = new long[2][2];
        for (long[] row : g) Arrays.fill(row, Long.MAX_VALUE / 4);
        g[0][0] = g[1][1] = 0;
        repair(2, g, 0, 1, 1);
        // The repair formula takes a minimum, so a raised cost of 5 cannot raise the stored 1.
        repair(2, g, 0, 1, 5);
        if (g[0][1] != 1) throw new AssertionError("stale value expected");
        long[][] fresh = new long[2][2];
        for (long[] row : fresh) Arrays.fill(row, Long.MAX_VALUE / 4);
        fresh[0][0] = fresh[1][1] = 0;
        fresh[0][1] = 5;
        floyd(2, fresh);
        if (fresh[0][1] != 5 || g[0][1] == fresh[0][1]) throw new AssertionError("rebuild must differ");
    }
}verified

Unlocked Combinations

A backup server records which byte ranges of a 2 TB file have arrived and must report the missing ranges while chunks keep coming. A separate directory service keeps an org chart and answers who sits 25 levels above an employee, thousands of times per run, while reporting lines change. The first service needs sorted ranges and coverage counts over huge offsets. The second needs indexes that survive updates. The two closing lessons pair those techniques.

These two lessons combine techniques from lessons 1 to 6 of this chapter.

Pairings Taught In This Chapter

Combination Representative problem
Sorted Search + Coverage Counting Gap-Tolerant Stream Summary and Dynamic Coverage API
Ancestor and Path Indexes + Call Workload LeetCode 1483 Kth Ancestor of a Tree Node and LeetCode 1724 Limit Queries on a Growing Graph

The lesson Ordered Interval State requires an explicit endpoint rule and a choice between sorted runs and a tree over the coordinate domain. The lesson Preprocessed Query APIs requires the update-to-query ratio before it keeps any index. Fenwick and segment trees stay in chapter 31.

Ordered Interval State

ProblemWhat Each Technique AddsSimple approachBottleneckKey ideaStateWalkthroughCodeWhen to use itExercises
Problem

Why The Resume Request Is So Slow

A backup client uploads a 2 TB file in chunks over eight parallel connections. Chunks arrive out of order, and a few of them fail the checksum and must be thrown away. When the network drops, the client asks the server which byte ranges of the file it still lacks, so that it can resend only those. A developer stores every chunk the server accepted as a pair of offsets in a list. To answer the question, the code sorts the whole list and sweeps it from left to right.

With 100 chunks the answer is instant. With a 64 KB chunk size the file has about 33.5 million chunks, and the status page asks the same question every few seconds while uploads continue. Each answer sorts millions of pairs again, although only a handful of byte ranges are really missing. The status request takes longer than the interval between two requests, and the server spends its time answering questions about work it already did.

The list grows with the number of chunks ever received, and the answer depends only on the few ranges that remain. This lesson asks what the server should store so that adding a chunk, discarding a corrupt chunk and reporting the received bytes each cost far less than a pass over all chunks. It also asks what changes when the offsets are so large that no array can index them.

What Each Technique Adds

What The Earlier Lessons Contribute

Rules For Stored Runs

The lesson Interval Unions supplied the canonical shape of the stored state. Ranges are half-open, stored in sorted order, disjoint, and never touching, so one run of covered integers has exactly one entry. It also supplied the floor lookup that finds the one stored range which can contain or touch a new range, and the rule that an add merges on equality while a removal does not.

A Descent That Records Predecessors

The lesson Ordered Structures supplied a descent that records the predecessor, which is the last stored key before a limit. One such search serves insertion, removal and lookup alike, and the comparison is strict for insert and erase. That lesson also showed what a layered skip list costs, which lets this lesson compare a sorted array with a skip list honestly.

Ranges That Nodes Own

The lesson Mutable Range Queries supplied the Fenwick tree, where each entry owns a range of positions and an update touches only the entries whose owned range contains the changed position. This lesson reuses the owned-range idea for coordinates up to 10^18. It adds a segment tree, in which each node owns a range that splits at its midpoint, and a change updates the nodes along root-to-leaf paths.

The Choice This Lesson Adds

The new work is the choice between two states. A sorted run array stays small while the covered set has few runs, and it cannot answer a count over a sub-range without walking runs. A tree that creates nodes only where calls touch answers both questions in logarithmic time, and it pays for that with memory per call.

Simple approach

Keeping Every Chunk In A List

The direct design appends each accepted range to a list and recomputes the answer from the whole list. The method that reports the received bytes copies the list, sorts the copy by start offset, and sweeps it while it merges overlapping ranges and adds up their sizes. Ranges are half-open, so the range from l up to but excluding r holds r - l bytes.

final class NaiveCoverage {
    private final java.util.ArrayList<long[]> chunks = new java.util.ArrayList<>(); // {start, end}

    void add(long l, long r) { chunks.add(new long[] {l, r}); }

    long received() {
        java.util.ArrayList<long[]> copy = new java.util.ArrayList<>(chunks);
        copy.sort((a, b) -> Long.compare(a[0], b[0]));           // sorts every chunk ever added
        long total = 0, reach = Long.MIN_VALUE;                  // reach is the largest end seen so far
        for (long[] c : copy) {
            long from = Math.max(c[0], reach);                   // skip bytes that an earlier chunk already counted
            if (c[1] > from) total += c[1] - from;
            reach = Math.max(reach, c[1]);
        }
        return total;
    }
}

With add(0, 10), add(20, 30) and add(5, 25), the call received() returns 30, because the three chunks merge into one range from 0 to 30. The class is correct on every small test, and it has no way to discard a corrupt chunk without finding the right entries in the list and splitting them.

Predict first: A file has 33.5 million chunks and the status page calls received() once per second while chunks keep arriving. About how many comparisons does one call spend on sorting, and how many distinct byte ranges does the answer actually depend on if 99 percent of the file has arrived in order?

One sort of 33.5 million pairs needs about 33.5 million times 25, which is roughly 840 million comparisons per call. If the chunks arrived mostly in order, the merged state is a few long runs, perhaps a few dozen. The answer therefore depends on tens of ranges, while the work depends on millions of entries. Memory also grows with every chunk, because the list keeps ranges that a merge would have absorbed long ago.

Bottleneck

Cost That Follows Calls, Not Runs

Let n be the number of calls so far and k the number of maximal runs of received bytes. One call to received costs O(n log n) time, and the list costs O(n) memory. The useful state has only k entries. In a healthy upload, k stays small while n reaches millions.

The repeated work has a clear shape. Each call re-derives the same merged runs from the same chunks, and every new chunk changes at most a few runs. A stored list of merged runs would avoid this, but a plain list does not search. To add a range, the code must still scan from the front to find the runs that touch it, which costs O(k) per call. For a fragmented state with 10^5 runs, that scan compares up to 10^5 entries one by one, where a binary search needs about 17 probes. A scan of that size is too slow for a call that happens thousands of times per second.

A second weakness appears when positions are huge. A sorted run list stays small while the number of runs is small, but a question such as "how many bytes in offsets 10^17 through 10^17 + 10^9 are received?" must visit every run in that window. A good design needs three things. Neighbours of a range must be found in O(log k). Updates must adjust a stored total without a rescan. A choice must exist for the workloads where counting over a sub-range matters more than keeping memory proportional to k.

Key idea

Search Runs And Keep The Total

Find Neighbours With An Explicit Search

Store the merged runs in two sorted arrays, one for starts and one for ends. Both arrays increase together, because runs are disjoint and do not touch. The routine predecessor search is a binary search over the starts that returns the first index whose start is above a given offset. The runs just left of that index are the predecessors, and the caller walks left from it over the runs that reach the new range. The code names this search firstStartAbove. One search and a short walk replace the scan from the front.

An explicit search is worth writing when no ordered map fits. A sorted array answers the search in O(log k) with no per-entry object, and an insertion shifts the tail with one System.arraycopy. That shift costs O(k) per update, but it is a straight memory copy with no comparison. At 10^4 runs one shift moves at most 160 KB across the two arrays. At 10^5 runs it moves 1.6 MB, and a few thousand updates per second then move gigabytes per second. The lesson accepts the shift only because k stays near tens of thousands. A skip list from the lesson Ordered Structures removes the shift at the price of pointers and random levels, and a TreeMap hides the same search behind a library class. All three give the same answers, and the choice is a cost question.

Keep The Covered Length Beside The Runs

The covered length is the number of integers inside the stored runs, kept in a long. Every change to the runs also changes this number by the exact difference. An add removes the lengths of the runs it absorbs and then adds the length of the fused run. A removal subtracts the lengths of every overlapping run and adds back the surviving left and right pieces. The total is read in constant time, and a test can check it against the sum of the stored run sizes after every call.

Grow A Tree Only Where Calls Touch

The run arrays cannot count the integers inside a sub-range faster than they can visit the runs there. A dynamic segment tree answers that count in O(log D) for a coordinate domain of size D. The root owns the whole domain. A node creates its two children only when a call needs to descend below it, so memory follows the touched paths and not the domain size. A node stores how many integers it covers, and a node is full when that number equals the size of its range.

A full node needs no children. When an add covers a node completely, the node becomes full and drops its subtree, and that drop is the merge of every smaller run that the add swallowed. A node can also become full through its children, for instance after add(0, 8) and then add(8, 16), and it then keeps them. The invariant says that a node is full exactly when its count equals its range size, that a call never descends into a full node, and that every node with children has a count equal to the sum of its two children. Dropped nodes leave their array slots behind, because the arrays never reclaim slots.

Choose The State From The Workload

Three facts decide the state. The domain size says whether any array over coordinates is possible. The operation set says whether a count over a sub-range is needed. The expected number of runs says whether a sorted array stays small. The sorted run state keeps memory proportional to the runs and answers the total in O(1). The tree answers every count in O(log D) and uses up to O(log D) nodes per call. When the domain is at most 64 integers, neither is needed: a single long used as a bitmask holds one bit per integer. An add sets a window of bits, a removal clears it, and Long.bitCount counts the covered bits in O(1).

State

Fields Of The Two States

The sorted run state stores four fields. The arguments of each call are offsets l and r that describe the half-open range from l up to but excluding r.

  • starts is a long array of run starts in increasing order, and only the first size slots are in use.
  • ends is a long array of run ends, where ends[i] is greater than starts[i] and less than starts[i + 1].
  • size is the number of stored runs.
  • covered is the covered length as a long, equal to the sum of ends[i] - starts[i].

The tree state stores a root and a domain size, and each node stores two child references and a count. A node with no children is either empty or full. In the traces below, the cells are the integers 0 to 15, the pointer l marks the start of the call's range, and the pointer r marks the excluded end, so r can equal the number of cells.

The invariant for the run arrays is that after every call the runs are sorted, disjoint and never touch, and covered equals the sum of their lengths. The invariant for the tree is that every node's count equals the number of covered integers in its range.

Walkthrough

Tracing Runs And Tree Nodes

Sorted Runs With Explicit Search

Each call first runs the predecessor search to find the index of the first run that starts above the right edge of the range. It then walks left from that index over the runs that reach the range. The first three adds build one run: the third add touches the runs on both sides of its range, so the two stored runs and the new range fuse into the single run from 2 up to but excluding 12. The removal from 4 to 10 cuts that run into two pieces, and the covered length falls from 10 to 4. The add at 14 creates a separate run. The last removal clears a stretch that spans a whole run and part of two others, and only the pieces outside it remain.

Tree Nodes Appear And Disappear

The domain is the sixteen integers 0 to 15. The first add creates nodes only on the paths to the touched leaves, and the add from 4 to 8 fills the node that owns exactly those integers. The add from 2 to 6 fills the node that owns 2 and 3, discards its two leaf children, and stops at the node for 4 to 8 because that node is already full. The add from 8 to 12 fills one more node. The final add covers the root completely, so the root becomes full and every other node disappears. The covered count reads 16 from the root alone, and the tree is back to one node.

Code

Run Arrays And A Dynamic Tree

The Sorted Run Arrays

The class RunStore keeps the starts and ends in two arrays and finds neighbours with its own binary search. The private method splice replaces a block of runs by at most two pieces with one array copy, so add and remove share the shifting code. The walk to the left over absorbed runs is paid for by the removal of those runs, so its cost does not add to the search.

final class RunStore {
    private long[] starts = new long[4];
    private long[] ends = new long[4];
    private int size;
    private long covered;

    // Predecessor search: returns the first index whose start is greater than x, or size when none is.
    private int firstStartAbove(long x) {
        int lo = 0, hi = size;
        while (lo < hi) {
            int mid = (lo + hi) >>> 1;
            if (starts[mid] > x) hi = mid; else lo = mid + 1;
        }
        return lo;
    }

    // Replaces runs i..j-1 by the given pieces and keeps both arrays sorted.
    private void splice(int i, int j, long[] ps, long[] pe, int count) {
        int newSize = size - (j - i) + count;
        if (newSize > starts.length) {
            starts = java.util.Arrays.copyOf(starts, newSize * 2);
            ends = java.util.Arrays.copyOf(ends, newSize * 2);
        }
        System.arraycopy(starts, j, starts, i + count, size - j);
        System.arraycopy(ends, j, ends, i + count, size - j);
        for (int k = 0; k < count; k++) { starts[i + k] = ps[k]; ends[i + k] = pe[k]; }
        size = newSize;
    }

    void add(long l, long r) {
        if (l >= r) return;                        // an empty range changes nothing
        int j = firstStartAbove(r);                // runs before j start at or before r
        int i = j;
        while (i > 0 && ends[i - 1] >= l) i--;     // walk left while the run reaches l
        long s = l, e = r;
        for (int k = i; k < j; k++) covered -= ends[k] - starts[k];
        if (i < j) { s = Math.min(s, starts[i]); e = Math.max(e, ends[j - 1]); }
        splice(i, j, new long[] {s}, new long[] {e}, 1);
        covered += e - s;
    }

    void remove(long l, long r) {
        if (l >= r) return;                        // an empty cut changes nothing
        int j = firstStartAbove(r - 1);            // runs before j start before r
        int i = j;
        while (i > 0 && ends[i - 1] > l) i--;      // walk left while the run reaches past l
        if (i == j) return;
        long[] ps = new long[2], pe = new long[2];
        int count = 0;
        if (starts[i] < l) { ps[count] = starts[i]; pe[count++] = l; }
        if (ends[j - 1] > r) { ps[count] = r; pe[count++] = ends[j - 1]; }
        for (int k = i; k < j; k++) covered -= ends[k] - starts[k];
        splice(i, j, ps, pe, count);
        for (int k = 0; k < count; k++) covered += pe[k] - ps[k];
    }

    long covered() { return covered; }
}

In add, the call firstStartAbove(r) gives j, so every run before j starts at or before r and every run from j on starts too far right to touch. The loop then lowers i while ends[i - 1] >= l. A run touches or overlaps the new range exactly when its end reaches l, so the test uses >= and the equal case fuses touching runs. The block i through j - 1 is empty when i == j, and the code reads starts[i] and ends[j - 1] only when i < j. The fused run takes the smaller start and the larger end of the new range and that block.

In remove, two tests differ. The search uses r - 1, so j counts the runs that start below r, and a run that starts exactly at r stays. The loop uses ends[i - 1] > l, because a run that only touches the cut shares no integer with it. Only the first run of the block can leave a left piece, when starts[i] < l, and only the last can leave a right piece, when ends[j - 1] > r. Both pieces are non-empty by those tests, so the guard at the top is the only place that rejects empty input.

The Dynamic Segment Tree

The class DynamicCover stores nodes in three parallel arrays and uses index 0 for the root. A child index of 0 means no child, which is safe because the root is never a child. The method add ends early on a full node, and it drops the children of a node that the new range covers completely. The methods remove and countIn are explained after the listing.

final class DynamicCover {
    private int[] left = new int[16], right = new int[16];
    private long[] count = new long[16];
    private int nodes = 1;                          // slot 0 is the root
    private final long limit;

    DynamicCover(long limit) { this.limit = limit; }

    private int create() {
        if (nodes == left.length) {                  // grow all three arrays together
            left = java.util.Arrays.copyOf(left, nodes * 2);
            right = java.util.Arrays.copyOf(right, nodes * 2);
            count = java.util.Arrays.copyOf(count, nodes * 2);
        }
        return nodes++;
    }

    void add(long l, long r) { add(0, 0, limit, l, r); }   // half-open [l, r) inside [0, limit)

    private void add(int node, long lo, long hi, long l, long r) {
        if (count[node] == hi - lo) return;                     // full already
        if (l <= lo && hi <= r) {                               // the range swallows the node
            count[node] = hi - lo;
            left[node] = 0;
            right[node] = 0;                                    // children are abandoned, which merges their runs; their slots are not reclaimed
            return;
        }
        long mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2;
        if (left[node] == 0) { int a = create(); int b = create(); left[node] = a; right[node] = b; }
        if (l < mid) add(left[node], lo, mid, l, r);
        if (r > mid) add(right[node], mid, hi, l, r);
        count[node] = count[left[node]] + count[right[node]];   // recompute from the children
    }

    void remove(long l, long r) { if (l < r) remove(0, 0, limit, l, r); }

    private void remove(int node, long lo, long hi, long l, long r) {
        if (count[node] == 0) return;                           // nothing covered below
        if (l <= lo && hi <= r) {                               // the cut swallows the node
            count[node] = 0;
            left[node] = 0;
            right[node] = 0;
            return;
        }
        long mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2;
        if (left[node] == 0) {                                  // a full node without children must split before a partial cut
            int a = create(); int b = create(); left[node] = a; right[node] = b;
            count[a] = mid - lo; count[b] = hi - mid;           // both halves inherit the full state
        }
        if (l < mid) remove(left[node], lo, mid, l, r);
        if (r > mid) remove(right[node], mid, hi, l, r);
        count[node] = count[left[node]] + count[right[node]];
    }

    // Covered integers inside [l, r), found from stored counts without visiting runs.
    long countIn(long l, long r) { return l < r ? countIn(0, 0, limit, l, r) : 0; }

    private long countIn(int node, long lo, long hi, long l, long r) {
        if (count[node] == 0 || r <= lo || hi <= l) return 0;   // empty node or disjoint range
        if (l <= lo && hi <= r) return count[node];             // the query swallows the node
        if (left[node] == 0) return Math.min(hi, r) - Math.max(lo, l); // a childless non-empty node is full
        long mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2;
        return countIn(left[node], lo, mid, l, r) + countIn(right[node], mid, hi, l, r);
    }

    long covered() { return count[0]; }
}

The method countIn visits at most two partial nodes per level and reads the stored count of every node inside the range, so it costs O(log D). The method remove mirrors add, but a full node without children first splits into two full halves, so a partial cut can leave one half covered.

When to use it

When Ordered State Fits

Recognizing The Pattern

Use ordered interval state when public methods add or remove ranges over a large coordinate line and ask for the covered total or for neighbours of a point. Check three points before you code. Write the endpoint convention once, as half-open ranges. Estimate the number of runs, because it decides whether a sorted array shift is cheap. Ask whether any question counts the covered integers inside a sub-range, because that question moves the design toward a tree. The invariant to protect is that stored runs stay sorted, disjoint and non-touching, and that the stored total equals the sum of their lengths after every call.

False Friends

A boolean array over the coordinates looks like the simplest state, and it is a false friend when the domain is 10^9 or larger, because memory follows the domain and not the number of runs. A HashSet of covered integers has the same problem, and a range add of a million integers inserts a million entries. A third false friend is a sorted list of added ranges that is never merged. It answers search questions quickly, but its size follows the number of calls, and the covered total needs a full sweep.

When The Method Does Not Apply

If every range is a single point and the questions are about rank or order statistics, an order-statistic tree or a Fenwick tree over compressed coordinates fits better. If the ranges carry weights that overwrite each other, as in a paint operation that records the latest colour, a run state needs a value per run, and a lazy tree from the lesson Deferred Transforms fits better. When the whole sequence of calls is known in advance, coordinate compression and a static array can replace a dynamic tree, and the memory per call disappears.

Exercises

Exercises

Basic

Gap-Tolerant Stream Summary

LeetCode 352

Problem Specification. Design a class GapSummary with a constructor that receives an int called gap. The method addNum(v) records the integer v as seen, and recording a value twice has no further effect. The method getIntervals() groups the seen values in sorted order. Two neighbouring seen values a < b belong to the same group exactly when b - a <= gap + 1. The method returns one closed pair [smallest, largest] per group, sorted by smallest value. The pair covers the missing values inside the group without marking them as seen. Search the stored groups with a hand-written binary search, not with TreeMap.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • v is an int with 0 <= v <= 2^31 - 1, and gap is an int with 0 <= gap <= 10^9.
  • Calls number at most 2 * 10^5, and they arrive online, so the sequence is not known in advance.
  • Update/query ratio is about 20 to 1.
  • Return value is a new int[][], empty for an empty stream.
  • Arithmetic must not overflow near 2^31 - 1.

Example 1. With gap = 1, input addNum(1), addNum(4), addNum(9), addNum(6), then getIntervals(), output [[1,1],[4,6],[9,9]]. After addNum(2) the output is [[1,6],[9,9]], and after addNum(7) the output is [[1,9]].

Example 2. With gap = 0, input addNum(5), addNum(5), addNum(3), addNum(4), addNum(4), addNum(9), then getIntervals(), output [[3,5],[9,9]]. A gap of 0 gives the original problem.

Prerequisites. The sorted run arrays and the predecessor search from this lesson, where the last group starting at or before a value sits just left of the returned index. This exercise changes the contract of LeetCode 352: runs also merge across a small gap of missing values.

What This Exercise Changes. The merge test compares a distance with gap + 1 and not an equality with 1, so one new value can bridge two groups that were far apart before.

Hint

A value inside a group changes nothing. Which two groups can a value outside every group be close enough to, and what happens when it is close to both?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The class keeps each group of seen values as a closed pair of smallest and largest value, in two parallel arrays sorted by smallest value. The invariant is that groups are disjoint and that the distance from one group's largest value to the next group's smallest value exceeds gap + 1, so each group is maximal.

A new value v first runs a hand-written predecessor search that returns the last group starting at or before v, which is the group just left of the first start above v. If that group's largest value reaches v, the value lies inside the group, and nothing changes because neither the smallest nor the largest value moves. Otherwise v is compared with two neighbours only: the found group on its left and the next group on its right. When both are within gap + 1, the value bridges them, and the left group takes over the right group's largest value. When only one is close enough, that group stretches to include v. When neither is close, v opens a new group. Distances are computed in long, so values near 2^31 - 1 and gaps up to 10^9 cannot overflow.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(log n) for the search plus an O(n) array shift when a group is inserted or two groups fuse, where n is the number of groups. The shift is a single block copy, and getIntervals costs O(n).
  • Space is O(n) for the two arrays, because the number of groups never exceeds the number of distinct values.
import java.util.*;

public final class GapStream {
    /** Keeps seen values as clusters [lo, hi] (closed) in two parallel sorted arrays. */
    static final class GapSummary {
        // Invariant: clusters are sorted, disjoint, and the distance from one cluster's hi to the next lo exceeds step.
        private final long step;                 // two seen values merge when they differ by at most step
        private long[] lo = new long[4];         // lo[i] is the smallest seen value of cluster i
        private long[] hi = new long[4];         // hi[i] is the largest seen value of cluster i
        private int n;                           // number of clusters in use

        GapSummary(int gap) {
            // A gap of g missing values between neighbours is tolerated, so the largest merged distance is g + 1.
            if (gap < 0) throw new IllegalArgumentException("gap must be at least 0");
            this.step = (long) gap + 1;
        }

        /**
         * Predecessor search: the index of the last cluster whose lo is at most v, or -1 when none exists.
         * Time: O(log n) because each probe halves the candidate range.
         * Space: O(1).
         */
        private int lastStartAtMost(long v) {
            int a = 0, b = n;                                // the answer lies in positions a - 1 .. b - 1
            while (a < b) {                                  // each pass halves b - a
                int m = (a + b) >>> 1;                       // unsigned shift avoids int overflow of a + b
                if (lo[m] <= v) a = m + 1;                   // cluster m starts at or before v, so look right of it
                else b = m;                                  // cluster m starts after v, so look left of it
            }
            return a - 1;                                    // a is the first cluster that starts after v
        }

        /** Opens a slot at position i by shifting the tail right. Time: O(n) copy, Space: amortized O(1). */
        private void openSlot(int i) {
            if (n == lo.length) {                            // grow by doubling so growth costs O(1) amortized
                lo = Arrays.copyOf(lo, n * 2);
                hi = Arrays.copyOf(hi, n * 2);
            }
            System.arraycopy(lo, i, lo, i + 1, n - i);       // shift starts right by one
            System.arraycopy(hi, i, hi, i + 1, n - i);       // shift ends right by one
            n++;                                             // the slot count grows by one
        }

        /**
         * Records v. Time: O(log n) search plus an O(n) array shift in the worst case. Space: O(1) extra.
         * Invariant: after the call every pair of neighbouring clusters is more than step apart.
         */
        void addNum(int v) {
            int p = lastStartAtMost(v);                      // cluster p is the only one that can contain v or lie just left of it
            if (p >= 0 && hi[p] >= v) return;                // v lies inside cluster p, so min and max do not change
            boolean joinLeft = p >= 0 && v - hi[p] <= step;  // cluster p ends close enough to v
            boolean joinRight = p + 1 < n && lo[p + 1] - v <= step; // the next cluster starts close enough to v
            if (joinLeft && joinRight) {                     // v bridges two clusters into one
                hi[p] = hi[p + 1];                           // the left cluster now ends where the right one ended
                System.arraycopy(lo, p + 2, lo, p + 1, n - p - 2); // close the hole left by the right cluster
                System.arraycopy(hi, p + 2, hi, p + 1, n - p - 2);
                n--;                                         // one cluster fewer
            } else if (joinLeft) {
                hi[p] = v;                                   // v extends the left cluster to the right
            } else if (joinRight) {
                lo[p + 1] = v;                               // v extends the right cluster to the left
            } else {
                openSlot(p + 1);                             // v starts a new cluster after cluster p
                lo[p + 1] = v;
                hi[p + 1] = v;
            }
        }

        /** Returns the clusters as closed pairs. Time: O(n). Space: O(n) for the returned array. */
        int[][] getIntervals() {
            int[][] out = new int[n][];                      // a fresh array, so callers may modify it
            for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) out[i] = new int[] {(int) lo[i], (int) hi[i]}; // values came from ints
            return out;
        }
    }

    /** Oracle: keeps every seen value and groups the sorted values on demand. */
    static int[][] oracle(TreeSet<Integer> seen, int gap) {
        List<int[]> out = new ArrayList<>();
        for (int v : seen) {                                 // sorted ascending
            if (!out.isEmpty() && (long) v - out.get(out.size() - 1)[1] <= (long) gap + 1) out.get(out.size() - 1)[1] = v;
            else out.add(new int[] {v, v});
        }
        return out.toArray(new int[0][]);
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: gap 1 bridges one missing value but not two.
        GapSummary a = new GapSummary(1);
        for (int v : new int[] {1, 4, 9, 6}) a.addNum(v);
        if (!Arrays.deepEquals(a.getIntervals(), new int[][] {{1, 1}, {4, 6}, {9, 9}})) throw new AssertionError("example 1a");
        a.addNum(2);
        if (!Arrays.deepEquals(a.getIntervals(), new int[][] {{1, 6}, {9, 9}})) throw new AssertionError("example 1b");
        a.addNum(7);
        if (!Arrays.deepEquals(a.getIntervals(), new int[][] {{1, 9}})) throw new AssertionError("example 1c");
        // Example 2: gap 0 matches the original problem, and repeated or inside values change nothing.
        GapSummary b = new GapSummary(0);
        for (int v : new int[] {5, 5, 3, 4, 4, 9}) b.addNum(v);
        if (!Arrays.deepEquals(b.getIntervals(), new int[][] {{3, 5}, {9, 9}})) throw new AssertionError("example 2");
        // Empty stream returns an empty array, and int extremes do not overflow.
        if (new GapSummary(2).getIntervals().length != 0) throw new AssertionError("empty");
        GapSummary c = new GapSummary(5);
        c.addNum(Integer.MAX_VALUE); c.addNum(0); c.addNum(Integer.MAX_VALUE - 3);
        if (!Arrays.deepEquals(c.getIntervals(), new int[][] {{0, 0}, {Integer.MAX_VALUE - 3, Integer.MAX_VALUE}})) throw new AssertionError("extremes");
        // Random streams against the oracle, with small value ranges so merges and bridges are frequent.
        Random rnd = new Random(3901);
        for (int trial = 0; trial < 6000; trial++) {
            int gap = rnd.nextInt(4), range = 5 + rnd.nextInt(60);
            GapSummary s = new GapSummary(gap);
            TreeSet<Integer> seen = new TreeSet<>();
            for (int step = 0; step < 40; step++) {
                int v = rnd.nextInt(range);
                s.addNum(v);
                seen.add(v);
                if (!Arrays.deepEquals(s.getIntervals(), oracle(seen, gap))) throw new AssertionError("trial " + trial);
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Variation

Range Module With Next Gap

LeetCode 715

Problem Specification. Design a class RangeGaps over the integers with half-open ranges [l, r). The method addRange(l, r) tracks every integer in the range, and removeRange(l, r) untracks every integer in the range. The method nextUncovered(x) returns the smallest untracked integer that is at least x. The method trackedCount() returns how many integers are tracked. There is no yes or no query over a range. Keep the tracked integers as sorted runs that never touch, and keep trackedCount in a stored field.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • l and r are long values with 0 <= l < r <= 10^12, and x is a long with 0 <= x <= 10^12.
  • Calls number at most 10^5, and they arrive online.
  • Update/query ratio is about 1 to 1.
  • Return values are long, and the two update methods return nothing.

Example 1. Input addRange(10,20), addRange(30,40), removeRange(14,33), then nextUncovered(10), nextUncovered(12), nextUncovered(33), nextUncovered(35) and trackedCount(), output 14, 14, 40, 40 and 11.

Example 2. Input addRange(5,8), addRange(8,12), addRange(1,5), then nextUncovered(1), nextUncovered(11), nextUncovered(0) and trackedCount(), output 12, 12, 0 and 11.

Prerequisites. The gap-tolerant exercise above and the half-open removal rules from the lesson Interval Unions. This exercise changes the operation set of LeetCode 715: the query asks for the first untracked integer.

What This Exercise Changes. The query returns the end of one run, so the non-touching property of the runs is now what makes the answer correct and not only compact.

Hint

If the run that contains x ends at e, why is e always untracked? Which property of the stored runs gives that guarantee?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. Tracked integers live in a list of half-open runs sorted by start, with a stored count. The invariant is that runs are disjoint and never touch, so the end of every run is an untracked integer, and the count equals the sum of run lengths.

One lower-bound routine serves both ends of the run, because starts and ends both increase. For an add, the first run whose end reaches l and the first run whose start is beyond r bound the block of runs that overlap or touch the new range. That block collapses into one run that spans the smallest start and the largest end, and the count changes by the new length minus the absorbed lengths. For a removal, the bounds use strict overlap, so a run that only touches the cut stays untouched. Only the first and last overlapping runs can leave a surviving piece, one on each side of the cut. The query nextUncovered(x) finds the last run starting at or before x. If that run ends beyond x, its end is the answer, and the non-touching invariant guarantees that the end is untracked. Otherwise x itself is the answer.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(log n) per search plus an O(n) list shift on update, where n is the number of runs. Each absorbed run is removed once, so the work of the loop over absorbed runs is charged to their creation.
  • Space is O(n) for the run list, independent of the coordinate range.
import java.util.*;
import java.util.function.ToLongFunction;

public final class GapModule {
    /** Tracks half-open runs [start, end) in an ArrayList sorted by start. */
    static final class RangeGaps {
        // Invariant: runs are sorted, disjoint and never touch, so every run end is an untracked integer.
        private final ArrayList<long[]> runs = new ArrayList<>();   // each entry is {start, end}
        private long tracked;                                       // number of tracked integers, always the sum of end - start

        private static final ToLongFunction<long[]> START = r -> r[0];
        private static final ToLongFunction<long[]> END = r -> r[1];

        /**
         * Lower bound: the first index whose key is at least x, or runs.size() when none is.
         * Starts and ends are both increasing, so one search routine serves both.
         * Time: O(log n). Space: O(1).
         */
        private int firstAtLeast(ToLongFunction<long[]> key, long x) {
            int a = 0, b = runs.size();                             // the answer lies in a .. b
            while (a < b) {                                         // each pass halves the range
                int m = (a + b) >>> 1;                              // midpoint without overflow
                if (key.applyAsLong(runs.get(m)) < x) a = m + 1;    // key too small, so the answer is right of m
                else b = m;                                         // key large enough, so m may be the answer
            }
            return a;
        }

        /**
         * Tracks [l, r). Time: O(log n) search plus O(n) list shift. Space: O(1) extra.
         * Invariant: runs touching or overlapping [l, r) collapse into one run.
         */
        void addRange(long l, long r) {
            int i = firstAtLeast(END, l);                           // first run whose end reaches l, so it overlaps or touches
            int j = firstAtLeast(START, r + 1);                     // first run that starts after r, so runs i..j-1 touch [l, r)
            long s = l, e = r;                                      // the merged run begins as the new range
            if (i < j) {                                            // at least one stored run is absorbed
                s = Math.min(s, runs.get(i)[0]);                    // the leftmost absorbed run may start earlier
                e = Math.max(e, runs.get(j - 1)[1]);                // the rightmost absorbed run may end later
                for (int k = i; k < j; k++) tracked -= runs.get(k)[1] - runs.get(k)[0]; // take back absorbed lengths
            }
            runs.subList(i, j).clear();                             // delete the absorbed runs in one shift
            runs.add(i, new long[] {s, e});                         // insert the merged run at the same position
            tracked += e - s;                                       // add the merged length once
        }

        /**
         * Untracks [l, r). Time: O(log n) search plus O(n) list shift. Space: O(1) extra.
         * Invariant: only the first and last overlapping runs can leave a surviving piece.
         */
        void removeRange(long l, long r) {
            int i = firstAtLeast(END, l + 1);                       // first run whose end is beyond l, so it overlaps [l, r)
            int j = firstAtLeast(START, r);                         // first run that starts at or after r, so runs i..j-1 overlap
            if (i >= j) return;                                     // nothing overlaps
            long[] first = runs.get(i), last = runs.get(j - 1);     // only these two can survive in part
            List<long[]> keep = new ArrayList<>(2);
            if (first[0] < l) keep.add(new long[] {first[0], l});   // left piece survives before the cut
            if (last[1] > r) keep.add(new long[] {r, last[1]});     // right piece survives after the cut
            for (int k = i; k < j; k++) tracked -= runs.get(k)[1] - runs.get(k)[0]; // remove every overlapping length
            for (long[] p : keep) tracked += p[1] - p[0];           // add back the surviving pieces
            runs.subList(i, j).clear();                             // drop the overlapping runs
            runs.addAll(i, keep);                                   // reinsert the pieces in order
        }

        /**
         * Smallest untracked integer that is at least x. Time: O(log n). Space: O(1).
         * Invariant: the run containing x ends at an untracked integer because runs never touch.
         */
        long nextUncovered(long x) {
            int p = firstAtLeast(START, x + 1) - 1;                 // last run that starts at or before x
            return p >= 0 && runs.get(p)[1] > x ? runs.get(p)[1] : x; // inside a run jump to its end, else x itself
        }

        /** Number of tracked integers. Time: O(1). Space: O(1). */
        long trackedCount() { return tracked; }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1.
        RangeGaps a = new RangeGaps();
        a.addRange(10, 20); a.addRange(30, 40); a.removeRange(14, 33);
        long[] r1 = {a.nextUncovered(10), a.nextUncovered(12), a.nextUncovered(33), a.nextUncovered(35), a.trackedCount()};
        if (!Arrays.equals(r1, new long[] {14, 14, 40, 40, 11})) throw new AssertionError("example 1 " + Arrays.toString(r1));
        // Example 2.
        RangeGaps b = new RangeGaps();
        b.addRange(5, 8); b.addRange(8, 12); b.addRange(1, 5);
        long[] r2 = {b.nextUncovered(1), b.nextUncovered(11), b.nextUncovered(0), b.trackedCount()};
        if (!Arrays.equals(r2, new long[] {12, 12, 0, 11})) throw new AssertionError("example 2 " + Arrays.toString(r2));
        // Random operations against a boolean array.
        Random rnd = new Random(7155);
        for (int trial = 0; trial < 5000; trial++) {
            RangeGaps m = new RangeGaps();
            boolean[] on = new boolean[70];
            for (int step = 0; step < 50; step++) {
                int l = 1 + rnd.nextInt(55), r = l + 1 + rnd.nextInt(12), kind = rnd.nextInt(3);
                if (kind == 0) { m.addRange(l, r); for (int x = l; x < r; x++) on[x] = true; }
                else if (kind == 1) { m.removeRange(l, r); for (int x = l; x < r; x++) on[x] = false; }
                else {
                    long expect = l;                                // oracle: scan upward to the first untracked integer
                    while (on[(int) expect]) expect++;
                    if (m.nextUncovered(l) != expect) throw new AssertionError("next, trial " + trial);
                }
                int count = 0;
                for (boolean x : on) if (x) count++;
                if (m.trackedCount() != count) throw new AssertionError("count, trial " + trial);
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Edge Cases

Counting Covered Integers On A Huge Line

LeetCode 2276

Problem Specification. Design a class CoverageTree with a constructor that receives a long called limit, so the valid integers are 0 through limit - 1. The method add(left, right) marks every integer from left to right inclusive as covered. The method count() returns how many distinct integers are covered. The method countIn(left, right) returns how many covered integers lie from left to right inclusive. Implement the class with a dynamic segment tree whose nodes exist only along touched paths.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • limit is a long with 1 <= limit <= 10^18.
  • left and right are long values with 0 <= left <= right < limit, so right + 1 never overflows.
  • Calls number at most 10^5, mixed between the three methods, and they arrive online.
  • Update/query ratio is about 1 to 1.
  • Return values are long.

Example 1. With limit = 100, input add(10,14), add(20,24), add(40,44), count(), output 15. After input add(12,41), count() outputs 35, countIn(0,19) outputs 10 and countIn(42,99) outputs 3.

Example 2. With limit = 10^18, input add(0, 10^18 - 1), count(), output 10^18. After input add(5,7), count() still outputs 10^18, and countIn(10^18 - 10, 10^18 - 1) outputs 10.

Prerequisites. The dynamic segment tree from this lesson. This exercise changes LeetCode 2276: the line is far too large for a boolean array, a list of merged runs must visit every run in the window to answer a sub-range count, and the class also counts inside a window.

What This Exercise Changes. One add can swallow many stored runs at once. The tree must discard the swallowed subtrees and keep every ancestor count exact, and the largest coordinate must not overflow when the range becomes half-open.

Hint

What should a node do when a new range covers it entirely, and what happens to the counts of the smaller runs that were stored below it?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The class uses a dynamic segment tree whose root owns the half-open range from 0 up to limit. Each node stores how many integers of its range are covered. The invariant is that a node is full exactly when its count equals its range size, that an add never descends into a full node, and that every node with children has a count equal to the sum of its two children. A node that becomes full through its children keeps them, and dropped nodes are not reclaimed from any array.

An add converts the closed pair into the half-open range from left to right + 1, which cannot overflow because right is below limit. A node that is already full returns immediately. A node inside the new range becomes full and drops both children, and that drop is the multi-interval merge: every smaller covered run below the node is swallowed in constant time, and the count stays exact because the node's own count is set to its size. A node that only overlaps creates its two children on first use, recurses into the halves that the range reaches, and recomputes its count from them. The midpoint is lo + (hi - lo) / 2, which stays correct for ranges up to 10^18. A window count adds the stored count of every node inside the window and, for a full node that only overlaps, the size of the overlap.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(log limit) per call, because at each level at most two nodes are partial and every other visited node stops at once.
  • Space is O(log limit) new nodes per add and O(calls * log limit) in total, and swallowed subtrees return their nodes to the garbage collector.
import java.util.*;

public final class HugeLine {
    /** Counts covered integers on [0, limit) with a dynamic segment tree that only grows along touched paths. */
    static final class CoverageTree {
        /** A node owns a half-open coordinate range and stores how many of its integers are covered. */
        private static final class Node {
            Node left, right;                    // both null for an untouched or fully covered node, both set otherwise
            long covered;                        // covered integers inside the owned range
        }

        private final long limit;                // coordinates are 0 .. limit - 1
        private final Node root = new Node();
        private int nodes = 1;                   // nodes ever created, kept to show the memory bound

        CoverageTree(long limit) {
            if (limit < 1 || limit > 1_000_000_000_000_000_000L) throw new IllegalArgumentException("limit");
            this.limit = limit;
        }

        /**
         * Covers the closed range [left, right]. Time: O(log limit), because every level visits at most two partial nodes.
         * Space: O(log limit) new nodes per call, and fully covered nodes drop their subtrees.
         * Invariant: a node is full exactly when covered equals its size, and a full node is never descended into.
         */
        void add(long left, long right) {
            if (left < 0 || right >= limit || left > right) throw new IllegalArgumentException("range");
            add(root, 0, limit, left, right + 1);   // convert the closed pair to the half-open range [left, right + 1)
        }

        private void add(Node node, long lo, long hi, long l, long r) {
            long size = hi - lo;                                  // size of the range this node owns
            if (node.covered == size) return;                     // already full, so nothing below can change
            if (l <= lo && hi <= r) {                             // the new range swallows this whole node
                node.covered = size;                              // the node becomes full
                node.left = null;                                 // drop every smaller run that the merge swallowed
                node.right = null;
                return;
            }
            long mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2;                        // split point, written to avoid overflow
            if (node.left == null) {                              // first visit below this node
                node.left = new Node();
                node.right = new Node();
                nodes += 2;
            }
            if (l < mid) add(node.left, lo, mid, l, r);           // the range reaches the left half
            if (r > mid) add(node.right, mid, hi, l, r);          // the range reaches the right half
            node.covered = node.left.covered + node.right.covered; // recompute from the children, no double counting
        }

        /** Total covered integers. Time: O(1). Space: O(1). */
        long count() { return root.covered; }

        /**
         * Covered integers inside the closed range [left, right]. Time: O(log limit). Space: O(log limit) stack.
         */
        long countIn(long left, long right) {
            if (left < 0 || right >= limit || left > right) throw new IllegalArgumentException("range");
            return countIn(root, 0, limit, left, right + 1);
        }

        private long countIn(Node node, long lo, long hi, long l, long r) {
            if (node.covered == 0 || r <= lo || hi <= l) return 0;     // empty node or disjoint range
            if (l <= lo && hi <= r) return node.covered;               // node lies inside the query, take its stored count
            if (node.left == null) return Math.min(hi, r) - Math.max(lo, l); // full node: every overlapping integer is covered
            long mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2;
            return countIn(node.left, lo, mid, l, r) + countIn(node.right, mid, hi, l, r); // add both halves
        }

        int nodeCount() { return nodes; }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: a later add swallows two stored runs and overlaps a third.
        CoverageTree a = new CoverageTree(100);
        a.add(10, 14); a.add(20, 24); a.add(40, 44);
        long c1 = a.count();
        a.add(12, 41);
        long c2 = a.count(), c3 = a.countIn(0, 19), c4 = a.countIn(42, 99);
        if (c1 != 15 || c2 != 35 || c3 != 10 || c4 != 3) throw new AssertionError("example 1 " + c1 + " " + c2 + " " + c3 + " " + c4);
        // Example 2: the largest coordinate and the largest limit.
        long big = 1_000_000_000_000_000_000L;
        CoverageTree b = new CoverageTree(big);
        b.add(0, big - 1);
        long d1 = b.count();
        b.add(5, 7);
        long d2 = b.count(), d3 = b.countIn(big - 10, big - 1);
        if (d1 != big || d2 != big || d3 != 10) throw new AssertionError("example 2");
        // A single add on the huge line creates only O(log limit) nodes.
        CoverageTree c = new CoverageTree(big);
        c.add(123456789012345678L, 987654321098765432L);
        if (c.nodeCount() > 4 * 61) throw new AssertionError("node bound " + c.nodeCount());
        // Random closed ranges against a boolean array, for several limits including powers of two and odd sizes.
        Random rnd = new Random(2276);
        for (int trial = 0; trial < 5000; trial++) {
            int limit = 1 + rnd.nextInt(70);
            CoverageTree t = new CoverageTree(limit);
            boolean[] on = new boolean[limit];
            for (int step = 0; step < 30; step++) {
                int l = rnd.nextInt(limit), r = l + rnd.nextInt(Math.min(12, limit - l));
                t.add(l, r);
                for (int x = l; x <= r; x++) on[x] = true;
                int ql = rnd.nextInt(limit), qr = ql + rnd.nextInt(limit - ql), total = 0, part = 0;
                for (int x = 0; x < limit; x++) {
                    if (on[x]) total++;
                    if (on[x] && x >= ql && x <= qr) part++;
                }
                if (t.count() != total || t.countIn(ql, qr) != part) throw new AssertionError("trial " + trial);
            }
            if (t.nodeCount() > 1 + 30 * 2 * 2 * 8) throw new AssertionError("nodes");
        }
    }
}verified
Pattern Recognition

Dynamic Coverage API

Author exercise

Problem Specification. Design a class DynamicCoverage with a constructor that receives a long called domain and a boolean called rangeCounts, where valid integers are 0 through domain - 1 and rangeCounts states whether coveredIn will ever be called. The methods add(l, r) and remove(l, r) cover or uncover the half-open range [l, r), and an empty range changes nothing. The method total() returns the covered count, and coveredIn(l, r) returns the covered count inside [l, r). The method backend() returns the name of the stored state. The name is "mask", the 64-bit bitmask from the lesson, when domain <= 64, otherwise "runs" when rangeCounts is false, otherwise "tree". Calling coveredIn on the "runs" state throws UnsupportedOperationException.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • domain is a long with 1 <= domain <= 10^18.
  • l and r are long values with 0 <= l <= r <= domain, and any other range throws IllegalArgumentException.
  • Calls number at most 10^5, and they arrive online, but the constructor flag rangeCounts is fixed when the object is built.
  • Update/query ratio is about 3 to 1.
  • Return values of total and coveredIn are long.

Example 1. With domain = 40 and rangeCounts = true, backend() returns "mask". Input add(3,10), add(8,20), remove(5,6), output total() is 16 and coveredIn(0,8) is 4. With domain = 10^9 and rangeCounts = false, backend() returns "runs". Input add(100,200), add(200,300), remove(150,250), add(1000,1000), output total() is 100, and coveredIn(0,10) throws.

Example 2. With domain = 10^12 and rangeCounts = true, backend() returns "tree". Input add(0, 5 * 10^11), remove(10^11, 2 * 10^11), output total() is 4 * 10^11 and coveredIn(0, 3 * 10^11) is 2 * 10^11.

Prerequisites. The three exercises above and the choice rules from this lesson.

What This Exercise Changes. The state is chosen from the coordinate domain and the query set, so the same public methods run on three different stored forms, and each form needs its own handling of removal.

Hint

Which state needs memory proportional to the domain, which to the number of runs, and which to the number of calls? Which of them can answer coveredIn without visiting runs?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The public class holds one internal state and picks it in the constructor from the two facts the caller knows: the domain size and whether sub-range counts will be asked. A domain of at most 64 integers fits in one long, where a range is a bit window and a count is a population count. Any larger domain with no sub-range counts uses an ordered map of runs, whose memory follows the number of runs and whose total is a stored field. A larger domain with sub-range counts uses the dynamic segment tree, because the map cannot count inside a window without visiting every run there.

Each state keeps its own invariant through removal. The mask clears a window with a bitwise complement. The map first cuts the range out of every overlapping run, keeps the left and right pieces, and only then fuses a new range with touching neighbours when the call is an add. The tree assigns a uniform value to a node that the range covers completely, and when a partial change reaches a uniform node, it splits the node into two children that inherit the uniform value. After the recursion, a node whose count is zero or its full size collapses back to a childless node, which keeps the tree in one canonical shape. The shift by 64 in the mask case is handled separately, because Java reduces a long shift distance modulo 64.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(1) for the mask, O(log n) plus the number of runs cut for the map, and O(log domain) for the tree, per call.
  • Space is O(1) for the mask, O(n) for the map with n runs, and O(calls * log domain) nodes for the tree in the worst case.
import java.util.*;

public final class DynamicCoverageApi {
    /** One state behind the public API; every range is half-open [l, r) with 0 <= l < r <= domain. */
    interface State {
        void set(long l, long r, boolean on);   // covers or uncovers every integer in [l, r)
        long covered(long l, long r);           // covered integers inside [l, r)
        long total();                           // covered integers overall
    }

    /**
     * Domain of at most 64 integers: one bit per integer.
     * Time: O(1) per call. Space: O(1), one long.
     * Invariant: bit x is set exactly when integer x is covered.
     */
    static final class MaskState implements State {
        private long bits;                                       // the whole state fits in one word

        private long window(long l, long r) {                    // bits l .. r - 1
            long len = r - l;                                    // number of bits in the window
            return len == 64 ? -1L : ((1L << len) - 1) << l;     // a shift by 64 would wrap, so the full window is special
        }

        public void set(long l, long r, boolean on) {
            long w = window(l, r);
            bits = on ? bits | w : bits & ~w;                    // set or clear the window with one operation
        }

        public long covered(long l, long r) { return Long.bitCount(bits & window(l, r)); } // popcount of the masked window

        public long total() { return Long.bitCount(bits); }
    }

    /**
     * Ordered map of disjoint, non-touching runs.
     * Time: O(log n) per call plus O(runs removed). Space: O(n) for n runs, independent of the domain size.
     * Invariant: runs are sorted, disjoint and never touch, and total equals the sum of end - start.
     */
    static final class RunState implements State {
        private final TreeMap<Long, Long> runs = new TreeMap<>();   // start -> end, with end exclusive
        private long total;                                         // covered integers, maintained by every write

        public void set(long l, long r, boolean on) {
            // Step 1: cut [l, r) out of the map so every overlapping run keeps only the pieces outside it.
            Map.Entry<Long, Long> e = runs.lowerEntry(r);           // the last run that starts before r
            while (e != null && e.getValue() > l) {                 // that run reaches into [l, r)
                long s = e.getKey(), t = e.getValue();
                runs.remove(s);                                     // take the whole run out
                total -= t - s;                                     // and take back its length
                if (s < l) { runs.put(s, l); total += l - s; }      // the piece left of the cut survives
                if (t > r) { runs.put(r, t); total += t - r; }      // the piece right of the cut survives
                e = runs.lowerEntry(r);                             // the next candidate; surviving pieces no longer qualify
            }
            if (!on) return;                                        // an uncover is finished after the cut
            // Step 2: insert [l, r) and fuse it with runs that touch either end.
            long s = l, t = r;
            Map.Entry<Long, Long> left = runs.floorEntry(l);        // a run starting at or before l
