            if (left != null && left.getValue() == l) {             // it ends exactly at l, so the two touch
                s = left.getKey();
                runs.remove(s);
                total -= l - s;
            }
            Long right = runs.get(r);                               // a run starting exactly at r touches on the right
            if (right != null) {
                t = right;
                runs.remove(r);
                total -= right - r;
            }
            runs.put(s, t);                                         // one fused run replaces up to three
            total += t - s;
        }

        public long covered(long l, long r) {
            throw new UnsupportedOperationException("range counts need the tree state"); // a map walk would cost O(runs)
        }

        public long total() { return total; }
    }

    /**
     * Dynamic segment tree with assign-cover and assign-uncover.
     * Time: O(log domain) per call. Space: O(operations * log domain) nodes in the worst case.
     * Invariant: a node with no children is uniform (all covered or none), and covered is the sum over its range.
     */
    static final class TreeState implements State {
        private static final class Node { Node left, right; long covered; }
        private final long domain;
        private final Node root = new Node();

        TreeState(long domain) { this.domain = domain; }

        public void set(long l, long r, boolean on) { assign(root, 0, domain, l, r, on); }

        private void assign(Node n, long lo, long hi, long l, long r, boolean on) {
            long size = hi - lo;
            if (l <= lo && hi <= r) {                               // the change covers this node completely
                n.covered = on ? size : 0;                          // the node becomes uniform
                n.left = n.right = null;                            // children are no longer needed
                return;
            }
            long mid = lo + size / 2;                               // split point, overflow-safe
            if (n.left == null) {                                   // a uniform node meets a partial change
                if (n.covered == (on ? size : 0)) return;           // it already equals the target, so nothing changes
                n.left = new Node();
                n.right = new Node();
                if (n.covered == size) {                            // push the full state down to both halves
                    n.left.covered = mid - lo;
                    n.right.covered = hi - mid;
                }
            }
            if (l < mid) assign(n.left, lo, mid, l, r, on);        // the range reaches the left half
            if (r > mid) assign(n.right, mid, hi, l, r, on);        // the range reaches the right half
            n.covered = n.left.covered + n.right.covered;          // recompute from the children
            if (n.covered == 0 || n.covered == size) n.left = n.right = null; // collapse back to a uniform node
        }

        public long covered(long l, long r) { return count(root, 0, domain, l, r); }

        private long count(Node n, long lo, long hi, long l, long r) {
            if (n.covered == 0 || r <= lo || hi <= l) return 0;     // empty node or disjoint range
            if (l <= lo && hi <= r) return n.covered;               // node inside the query
            if (n.left == null) return Math.min(hi, r) - Math.max(lo, l); // uniform full node: count the overlap
            long mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2;
            return count(n.left, lo, mid, l, r) + count(n.right, mid, hi, l, r);
        }

        public long total() { return root.covered; }
    }

    /** The public API; it picks one state at construction from the domain size and the query set. */
    static final class DynamicCoverage {
        private final long domain;
        private final State state;
        private final String backend;

        DynamicCoverage(long domain, boolean rangeCounts) {
            if (domain < 1 || domain > 1_000_000_000_000_000_000L) throw new IllegalArgumentException("domain");
            this.domain = domain;
            if (domain <= 64) { state = new MaskState(); backend = "mask"; }              // one word holds every integer
            else if (!rangeCounts) { state = new RunState(); backend = "runs"; }          // no sub-range counts, so runs suffice
            else { state = new TreeState(domain); backend = "tree"; }                     // sub-range counts over a large domain
        }

        String backend() { return backend; }

        private void check(long l, long r) { if (l < 0 || r > domain || l > r) throw new IllegalArgumentException("range"); }

        void add(long l, long r) { check(l, r); if (l < r) state.set(l, r, true); }      // an empty range changes nothing

        void remove(long l, long r) { check(l, r); if (l < r) state.set(l, r, false); }

        long total() { return state.total(); }

        long coveredIn(long l, long r) { check(l, r); return l == r ? 0 : state.covered(l, r); }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1a: a tiny domain uses the mask, and a removal splits a covered stretch.
        DynamicCoverage a = new DynamicCoverage(40, true);
        a.add(3, 10); a.add(8, 20); a.remove(5, 6);
        if (!a.backend().equals("mask") || a.total() != 16 || a.coveredIn(0, 8) != 4) throw new AssertionError("example 1a");
        // Example 1b: no range counts on a large domain uses runs, and a range count is rejected.
        DynamicCoverage b = new DynamicCoverage(1_000_000_000L, false);
        b.add(100, 200); b.add(200, 300); b.remove(150, 250); b.add(1000, 1000);
        boolean threw = false;
        try { b.coveredIn(0, 10); } catch (UnsupportedOperationException ex) { threw = true; }
        if (!b.backend().equals("runs") || b.total() != 100 || !threw) throw new AssertionError("example 1b");
        // Example 2: range counts on a huge domain use the tree.
        long t12 = 1_000_000_000_000L;
        DynamicCoverage c = new DynamicCoverage(t12, true);
        c.add(0, t12 / 2); c.remove(t12 / 10, t12 / 5);
        if (!c.backend().equals("tree") || c.total() != 4 * (t12 / 10) || c.coveredIn(0, 3 * (t12 / 10)) != 2 * (t12 / 10))
            throw new AssertionError("example 2");
        // The boundary of a full 64-integer domain must not wrap the shift.
        DynamicCoverage full = new DynamicCoverage(64, true);
        full.add(0, 64);
        if (full.total() != 64 || full.coveredIn(0, 64) != 64) throw new AssertionError("64-bit window");
        // Random operations against a boolean array for all three states.
        Random rnd = new Random(2603);
        for (int trial = 0; trial < 6000; trial++) {
            int domain = 1 + rnd.nextInt(trial % 3 == 0 ? 64 : 150);
            boolean counts = rnd.nextBoolean();
            DynamicCoverage d = new DynamicCoverage(domain, counts);
            boolean[] on = new boolean[domain];
            for (int step = 0; step < 40; step++) {
                int l = rnd.nextInt(domain + 1), r = l + rnd.nextInt(domain - l + 1);
                boolean add = rnd.nextBoolean();
                if (add) d.add(l, r); else d.remove(l, r);
                for (int x = l; x < r; x++) on[x] = add;
                int total = 0;
                for (boolean x : on) if (x) total++;
                if (d.total() != total) throw new AssertionError("total, trial " + trial);
                if (!d.backend().equals("runs")) {
                    int ql = rnd.nextInt(domain + 1), qr = ql + rnd.nextInt(domain - ql + 1), part = 0;
                    for (int x = ql; x < qr; x++) if (on[x]) part++;
                    if (d.coveredIn(ql, qr) != part) throw new AssertionError("coveredIn, trial " + trial);
                }
            }
        }
    }
}verified

Preprocessed Query APIs

ProblemWhat Each Technique AddsSimple approachBottleneckKey ideaStateWalkthroughCodeWhen to use itExercises
Problem

Why The Org Chart Lookup Crawls

An internal directory service stores a company org chart with 100,000 employees. Each employee record holds one pointer to a direct manager. A reporting tool asks questions such as who sits 25 levels above an employee, and it asks them all day, 100,000 times per run. The first version walks the manager pointer once per level. On the test data the chart is shallow, and the answers return instantly. In a company that grew by promoting one chain of managers, the chart is 50,000 levels deep. A single question then walks tens of thousands of pointers, and a full run of 100,000 questions spends about 5 billion pointer reads on a chart that never changed.

Every answer is correct, so no test fails. The cost comes from repeating the same climb for each question. A second problem appears when the data does change. The same service also stores approval links between employees, each with a cost, and another tool asks whether two employees connect through links that all cost less than a limit. New links arrive all day. If the service stores extra data to speed up either question, a promotion or a new link can make that data wrong. This lesson asks what the constructor should store once, and how the number of constructor calls, updates and queries decides whether that stored data pays for itself.

What Each Technique Adds

What The Earlier Lessons Contribute

The lesson Query Planning supplied the habit of naming the workload before choosing a structure. It asked how many updates there are, how many queries there are and whether all queries are known at the start. It also supplied the sorted list of positions for one value, and the rule that a range has one stated endpoint convention. This lesson reuses the habit and the sorted lists, and it applies them to objects that answer a stream of calls.

The lesson Dynamic Graph Queries supplied the comparison between rebuilding a table after every change and repairing only what a change can affect. It also supplied the counting of operations as a function of the number of updates and queries. Those two ideas explain why an object that stores a derived index must decide when that index is still valid.

The new work is the combination. A derived index is built in the constructor, read by every query and damaged by some updates. The lesson shows how to price the three kinds of calls separately, how to detect the updates that really damage the index, and how to postpone the repair until a query needs it.

Simple approach

Walking Up One Level At A Time

The direct design keeps the parent array and answers each question with a loop. The loop moves to the parent once per requested level, and it stops with -1 when the root has no parent.

final class WalkAncestor {
    private final int[] parent;                 // parent[0] is -1 for the root

    WalkAncestor(int[] parent) { this.parent = parent.clone(); }

    int kth(int node, int k) {
        int cur = node;
        for (int i = 0; i < k && cur != -1; i++) {
            cur = parent[cur];                  // one pointer read per requested level
        }
        return cur;                             // -1 when the chain ends before k levels
    }
}

With parent = {-1, 0, 0, 1}, the call kth(3, 2) returns 0, and kth(3, 3) returns -1 because the root has no parent. The class needs no setup and uses no extra memory. It is correct on every small chart.

Predict first: The chart is one chain of 50000 employees, and the tool asks 100000 questions about the employee at the bottom with k near 49999. How many parent reads does the run perform, and how many different answers can those questions have?

Each question walks about 50000 levels, so the run performs about 5 billion parent reads. The chain has 50000 employees, so at most 50000 different answers exist for a fixed start, and most reads repeat a climb that an earlier question already did. The cost is the product of the number of questions and the depth, and neither factor shrinks as the questions repeat.

Bottleneck

Cost That Multiplies With Depth

Let n be the number of employees, q the number of questions and k the number of levels asked. One call costs O(k) time, and k can reach n - 1. A run therefore costs O(q * n) in the worst case, which is 5 * 10^9 reads for q = 100,000 and n = 50,000. The class stores nothing beyond the input, so the cost never improves, however many questions arrive.

The repeated work has a clear shape. Two questions about nodes on the same chain climb through the same pointers. The answer for 25 levels is the answer for 16 levels followed by 9 levels, and each of those pieces can also be split further. The naive loop recomputes every such piece from the start for every question.

Three requirements follow. A question must cost far less than the number of levels it asks. The extra data must be small enough to keep in memory next to the input. If the chart can change, the object must also say which changes make the stored data wrong, and it must repair that data at a cost that matches how often changes happen. A design that builds fast lookups but rebuilds them on every change can lose to the loop above, which is why the counts of constructor calls, updates and queries must enter the decision.

Key idea

Store Power-Of-Two Jumps And Price Each Call

Jumps By Powers Of Two

Every distance k is a sum of distinct powers of two, which is the binary form of k. If the object knows the ancestor 2^j levels up for every node and every j, a query of k levels needs only one jump for each set bit of k. This storage scheme is called binary lifting.

The table has a row j for each power. Row 0 is the parent array. Row j comes from row j - 1, because 2^j levels are two jumps of 2^(j-1) levels. A node lacking an ancestor that far holds -1, and no jump starts from -1. Building about log n rows of n entries costs O(n log n), and each query costs O(log k). With n = 100000 the table has 17 rows, which is 1.7 million entries, and the run of 100,000 questions reads at most 1.7 million of them.

The Call Mix Decides

The call mix is the three counts of a run: constructor calls, updates and queries. Let C be the cost of building the index, U the cost of repairing it after one update and Q the cost of one query. A run costs about C + u * U + q * Q, where u and q count the updates and the queries. The naive loop has C = 0 and a large Q. The jump table trades a large C for a small Q, so it wins once q repays C.

The same arithmetic shows when an index loses. Suppose every update forces a full rebuild with U = C, and one query follows each update. The run then costs about u * C, and a direct search per query can match that without extra memory. Indexing pays only when damaging updates are rarer than queries, or when repair costs far less than construction.

Limit Queries Use A Spanning Forest

The approval-link question asks whether p and q connect through links that all weigh less than limit. A minimum spanning forest keeps just enough cheap links to join every connected group. Kruskal's method builds it by scanning links from lightest to heaviest and accepting a link only when its endpoints sit in different trees. Between two connected vertices the forest holds one route, and its heaviest link, called the bottleneck, is the lowest ceiling that any route can achieve. A limit query returns true exactly when the bottleneck is smaller than limit.

Consider four vertices with links 0-1 at weight 5, 1-2 at weight 2, 0-2 at weight 9 and 2-3 at weight 7. Kruskal accepts weights 2, 5 and 7, then rejects 9 because vertices 0 and 2 are already connected. The forest route from 0 to 3 is 0-1-2-3, and its bottleneck is 7. So query(0, 3, 8) returns true, while query(0, 3, 7) returns false because 7 is not below 7. The rejected weight 9 can never matter, since a cheaper route between its endpoints exists.

Detect Damage And Rebuild Later

A stale index is an index that no longer matches the data. Not every update makes it stale. An edge added to a graph whose minimum spanning forest already contains a path with all edges at most as heavy leaves the forest unchanged, so the index stays valid. An edge damages the index only when its ends lie in different trees or its weight is below the bottleneck between them. The object checks this with one bottleneck query when the index is fresh, and it sets a stale mark only when the edge can change an answer.

After the mark is set, later updates skip the check, because a single rebuild covers them all. The next query performs that rebuild and clears the mark. One thousand damaging updates followed by one query cost a single rebuild, while harmless updates cost none. This deferral is what turns the construction cost into an amortized cost spread over the queries.

State

Fields Of An Index Behind An Object

An object with a maintained index stores the data it was given, the index built from it and the facts that decide when to repair the index.

  • n is the number of nodes, fixed at construction.
  • rows is the number of table rows, the smallest r with 2^r >= n.
  • up is the table, where up[j][v] is the ancestor of v that is 2^j levels up, or -1.
  • forest is the index of the limit-query structure, a minimum spanning forest with its own jump table.
  • stale is a boolean that is true when an update has made the index wrong.
  • rebuilds counts the repairs performed after construction, which tests use to check the plan.

In the first trace, the pointer node marks the node where a query currently stands, and -1 means the query left the tree. In the second trace, the pointers p and q mark the two nodes named by the current call, and the cells list the four vertices.

The invariant is that after every call, a clear stale mark means the index answers like a fresh build from the stored data, and a set mark means a rebuild before the next answer restores that property.

Walkthrough

Tracing Jumps And Rebuilds

Jumps On A Deep Chain

The tree below has 12 nodes. Node 0 is the root, nodes 0 to 7 form a chain, and nodes 8 to 11 form a second chain that hangs below node 2. The first query asks for the ancestor of node 11 that is 5 levels up. The number 5 is 101 in binary, so bits 0 and 2 are set. Bit 0 moves one level and bit 2 moves four levels, which together reach node 1 in two table reads and not in five pointer reads. The second query asks for the ancestor of node 6 that is 9 levels up. Bit 0 moves the query to node 5. Bit 3 asks for a jump of eight levels from node 5, and node 5 has only five ancestors above it, so the table holds -1 and the answer is -1.

Updates Against A Stale Mark

The second trace uses a graph with four vertices and one initial edge, and the query asks whether a path exists with every edge below a limit. The constructor builds the index and starts with a clear mark. An edge that connects two separate trees sets the mark. An edge that is not lighter than the heaviest forest edge between its ends leaves the mark clear. While the mark is set, the next insertion only stores its edge. The first query after a set mark pays for exactly one rebuild. The final count of rebuilds is 2, because the insertions of weight 7 and weight 2 each set the mark, the insertion of weight 9 leaves it clear, and the insertion of weight 3 arrives while the mark is set, so the rebuild already pending covers it.

Code

A Jump Table And A Lazy Rebuild

The Jump Table

The class LiftTable builds the table from the parent array and answers a query with one read per set bit. A set -1 entry stays -1 all the way up. The constructor cost is the only place where the table is written, so the object never changes after construction.

final class LiftTable {
    private final int[][] up;
    private final int n;

    LiftTable(int[] parent) {
        n = parent.length;
        int rows = 1;
        while ((1 << rows) < n) rows++;                  // enough rows for any distance below n
        up = new int[rows][];
        up[0] = parent.clone();
        for (int j = 1; j < rows; j++) {
            up[j] = new int[n];
            for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) {
                int mid = up[j - 1][v];                  // half of the jump
                up[j][v] = mid == -1 ? -1 : up[j - 1][mid];
            }
        }
    }

    int kth(int node, int k) {
        if (k >= n) return -1;                           // no ancestor that far exists
        int cur = node;
        for (int j = 0; k >> j != 0 && cur != -1; j++) {
            if ((k >> j & 1) == 1) cur = up[j][cur];     // one table read per set bit
        }
        return cur;
    }
}

The Forest Behind Limit Queries

The class BottleneckForest builds the minimum spanning forest with Kruskal's method, roots each tree and fills the same kind of jump table as LiftTable. A second table heavy stores the heaviest edge on each jump. A query lifts the deeper vertex to the depth of the other, then lifts both until their parents agree, and keeps the largest edge it crosses. It returns Integer.MAX_VALUE when the two vertices lie in different trees. The code of both classes follows the next paragraph.

The class LazyIndex wraps a real BottleneckForest. It stores every edge, keeps the forest built from them and sets the stale mark only for an update that can lower a bottleneck. The query rebuilds the forest first when the mark is set. In the directory, addEdge is a new approval link, query is the question about a cost limit, and a rebuild reruns Kruskal's method on all stored links.

final class BottleneckForest {
    private final int[][] up;                            // up[j][v] is the 2^j-th ancestor in the forest, or -1
    private final int[][] heavy;                         // heavy[j][v] is the heaviest edge on that jump
    private final int[] depth;
    private final int rows;

    BottleneckForest(int n, java.util.List<int[]> edges) {
        java.util.List<int[]> sorted = new java.util.ArrayList<>(edges);
        sorted.sort((x, y) -> Integer.compare(x[2], y[2]));   // lightest edge first
        int[] dsu = new int[n];
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) dsu[i] = i;
        java.util.List<java.util.List<int[]>> adj = new java.util.ArrayList<>();
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) adj.add(new java.util.ArrayList<>());
        for (int[] e : sorted) {
            int a = find(dsu, e[0]), b = find(dsu, e[1]);
            if (a == b) continue;                        // the ends are already joined by lighter edges
            dsu[a] = b;
            adj.get(e[0]).add(new int[] {e[1], e[2]});
            adj.get(e[1]).add(new int[] {e[0], e[2]});
        }
        int r = 1;
        while ((1 << r) < n) r++;
        rows = r;
        up = new int[rows][n];
        heavy = new int[rows][n];
        depth = new int[n];
        java.util.Arrays.fill(up[0], -1);
        boolean[] seen = new boolean[n];
        for (int root = 0; root < n; root++) {
            if (seen[root]) continue;
            seen[root] = true;
            java.util.ArrayDeque<Integer> queue = new java.util.ArrayDeque<>();
            queue.add(root);
            while (!queue.isEmpty()) {                   // breadth-first search roots the tree
                int v = queue.poll();
                for (int[] nb : adj.get(v)) {
                    if (seen[nb[0]]) continue;
                    seen[nb[0]] = true;
                    up[0][nb[0]] = v;
                    heavy[0][nb[0]] = nb[1];
                    depth[nb[0]] = depth[v] + 1;
                    queue.add(nb[0]);
                }
            }
        }
        for (int j = 1; j < rows; j++) {
            for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) {
                int mid = up[j - 1][v];
                if (mid == -1) { up[j][v] = -1; continue; }
                up[j][v] = up[j - 1][mid];
                heavy[j][v] = Math.max(heavy[j - 1][v], heavy[j - 1][mid]);
            }
        }
    }

    private static int find(int[] d, int x) {
        while (d[x] != x) { d[x] = d[d[x]]; x = d[x]; }
        return x;
    }

    int bottleneck(int p, int q) {
        if (p == q) return 0;                            // an empty path has no edge
        if (depth[p] < depth[q]) { int t = p; p = q; q = t; }
        int best = 0, diff = depth[p] - depth[q];
        for (int j = 0; j < rows; j++) {
            if ((diff >> j & 1) == 1) { best = Math.max(best, heavy[j][p]); p = up[j][p]; }
        }
        if (p == q) return best;                         // q was an ancestor of p
        for (int j = rows - 1; j >= 0; j--) {
            if (up[j][p] != up[j][q]) {                  // jump only while the two sides stay apart
                best = Math.max(best, Math.max(heavy[j][p], heavy[j][q]));
                p = up[j][p];
                q = up[j][q];
            }
        }
        if (up[0][p] == -1) return Integer.MAX_VALUE;    // different trees have no common parent
        return Math.max(best, Math.max(heavy[0][p], heavy[0][q]));
    }
}

final class LazyIndex {
    private final int n;
    private final java.util.List<int[]> edges = new java.util.ArrayList<>();
    private BottleneckForest forest;
    private boolean stale;
    private int rebuilds;

    LazyIndex(int n, java.util.List<int[]> initial) {
        this.n = n;
        edges.addAll(initial);
        forest = new BottleneckForest(n, edges);         // the constructor build is not counted
    }

    void addEdge(int u, int v, int w) {
        edges.add(new int[] {u, v, w});                  // the data always changes
        if (stale) return;                               // a pending rebuild already covers this edge
        if (damages(u, v, w)) stale = true;              // only a damaging edge marks the index
    }

    boolean query(int p, int q, int limit) {
        if (stale) {                                     // pay the repair once, at the first read
            forest = new BottleneckForest(n, edges);
            stale = false;
            rebuilds++;
        }
        return forest.bottleneck(p, q) < limit;          // a path exists when its heaviest edge is below limit
    }

    int rebuildCount() { return rebuilds; }

    private boolean damages(int u, int v, int w) {
        return w < forest.bottleneck(u, v);              // joins two trees, or beats the heaviest forest edge
    }
}

Cost

  • Constructor costs O(n log n) time for the table of n log n entries.
  • Forest build sorts the E edges and fills two tables, so it costs O(E log E + n log n) time.
  • Query costs O(log k) time and O(1) extra memory.
  • Update costs O(1) when a rebuild is pending, O(log n) for the damage check otherwise, and one full forest build is charged to the next query.
When to use it

When A Maintained Index Pays Off

Recognizing The Pattern

Use a maintained index when the data is read far more often than it changes, and when each read would otherwise repeat the same work. Before coding, write down three numbers and one promise: the number of constructor calls, updates and queries, and whether the calls are known in advance. When all calls are known in advance, an offline plan that sorts or batches the queries can avoid the index altogether. When calls arrive one at a time, the index must answer each from stored state. The invariant to protect is that every answer equals the answer of a fresh build from the stored data.

False Friends

A rebuild on a timer looks like a repair rule, and it is a false friend. Between two ticks the index is stale, so the answers depend on when the timer last ran. A second false friend is a larger index for a smaller workload. A jump table for a chart of 100,000 employees stores 1.7 million entries, while 1,000 queries on a chart of depth 30 walk at most 30,000 pointers, so the naive loop wins.

When The Method Does Not Apply

If every update damages the index and queries are rare, the lazy rebuild runs once per query and costs more than a direct search. The design then drops the index. If the updates can be repaired locally, as in the distance array of the last exercise, a repair that touches only the affected entries beats a rebuild, and the object keeps the index without any stale mark.

Exercises

Exercises

Basic

Kth Ancestor Of A Tree Node

LeetCode 1483

Problem Specification. Design a class TreeAncestor. Its constructor receives the integer n and an array parent of length n, where parent[i] is the parent of node i and parent[0] = -1 marks the root. The method getKthAncestor(node, k) returns the ancestor of node that is k edges above it, or -1 when the path to the root has fewer than k edges. The tree never changes, so the workload has one constructor call, zero updates and many queries. The queries arrive one at a time, so they are not known in advance.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • n satisfies 1 <= n <= 5 * 10^4, and parent labels need not be smaller than the child label.
  • node satisfies 0 <= node < n, and k satisfies 0 <= k <= n.
  • Calls are one constructor call and up to 5 * 10^4 queries.
  • Return value is an int, and k = 0 returns node.

Example 1. Input n = 9, parent = [-1,0,0,1,1,2,3,6,6] and queries (8,2), (8,3), (8,4), (4,1), (4,2), (5,0); output [3, 1, 0, 1, 0, 5].

Example 2. Input n = 5, parent = [-1,0,1,2,3] and queries (4,4), (4,5), (2,1), (0,1); output [0, -1, 1, -1].

Prerequisites. The jump table and the bit-by-bit query from this lesson.

What This Exercise Changes. The constructor stores a table of about log n rows, so each query stops walking and reads one entry per set bit of k.

Hint

Which power-of-two jumps add up to k, and what must a row store for a node that has no ancestor that far away?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The constructor spends O(n log n) time once, because the workload has no updates and many online queries. Row 0 of the table holds the parent of each node, and row j holds the ancestor 2^j steps up. Each row comes from the row below it, because 2^j steps are two blocks of 2^(j-1) steps. A missing ancestor stays -1 all the way up, so a jump from -1 is never attempted.

A query reads the bits of k from the lowest bit to the highest. For each set bit j, the current node moves to up[j][node]. The loop stops early when the node becomes -1. If k has a bit above the last row, or if k is at least n, no node has an ancestor that far away, so the method returns -1 before the loop.

The invariant is that after the bits below j are processed, node is the ancestor of the start at distance equal to the low j bits of k, or -1 when it does not exist. The oracle walks the parent array one step at a time on random trees whose parent labels are not ordered, and it compares every answer.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(n log n) for the constructor and O(log k) per query, because each query inspects at most bits(n) table entries.
  • Space is O(n log n) for the table of n entries in each of about log n rows.
import java.util.Random;

public final class KthAncestorSolution {
    static final class TreeLifting {
        private final int[][] up; // up[j][v] is the ancestor of v that is 2^j steps up, or -1
        private final int n;

        /**
         * Builds the jump table from the parent array.
         * Time: O(n log n). Space: O(n log n).
         * Invariant: row j equals row j-1 applied twice, so it holds the 2^j-th ancestor.
         */
        TreeLifting(int n, int[] parent) {
            this.n = n;
            // Enough rows to cover every distance from 1 to n - 1.
            int rows = 1;
            while ((1 << rows) < n) rows++;
            up = new int[rows][];
            // Row 0 is a copy of the parent array.
            up[0] = parent.clone();
            for (int j = 1; j < rows; j++) {
                up[j] = new int[n];
                for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) {
                    int mid = up[j - 1][v]; // the node 2^(j-1) steps up
                    // A missing midpoint means the whole jump leaves the tree.
                    up[j][v] = mid == -1 ? -1 : up[j - 1][mid];
                }
            }
        }

        /**
         * Returns the k-th ancestor of node, or -1 when the root is closer than k steps.
         * Time: O(log k). Space: O(1).
         * Invariant: after bit j, cur is the ancestor at the distance given by the low j+1 bits of k.
         */
        int kth(int node, int k) {
            // No node has an ancestor n or more steps away.
            if (k >= n) return -1;
            int cur = node;
            // Visit the set bits of k from low to high; stop once the node leaves the tree.
            for (int j = 0; k >> j != 0 && cur != -1; j++) {
                // A set bit means a jump of 2^j steps through one table read.
                if ((k >> j & 1) == 1) cur = up[j][cur];
            }
            return cur;
        }
    }

    /** Brute force: follows the parent pointer k times. */
    static int oracle(int[] parent, int node, int k) {
        int cur = node;
        // Each step moves one level up; -1 absorbs further steps.
        for (int i = 0; i < k && cur != -1; i++) cur = parent[cur];
        return cur;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: a tree with a branch at nodes 3 and 6, including k = 0 and a miss.
        int[] p1 = {-1, 0, 0, 1, 1, 2, 3, 6, 6};
        TreeLifting t1 = new TreeLifting(9, p1);
        int[][] q1 = {{8, 2}, {8, 3}, {8, 4}, {4, 1}, {4, 2}, {5, 0}};
        int[] e1 = {3, 1, 0, 1, 0, 5};
        for (int i = 0; i < q1.length; i++) {
            if (t1.kth(q1[i][0], q1[i][1]) != e1[i]) throw new AssertionError("example 1 query " + i);
        }
        // Example 2: a path where k equals the depth and then exceeds it by one.
        TreeLifting t2 = new TreeLifting(5, new int[] {-1, 0, 1, 2, 3});
        if (t2.kth(4, 4) != 0 || t2.kth(4, 5) != -1 || t2.kth(2, 1) != 1 || t2.kth(0, 1) != -1) {
            throw new AssertionError("example 2");
        }
        // A single node has no table rows beyond row 0 and still answers.
        TreeLifting one = new TreeLifting(1, new int[] {-1});
        if (one.kth(0, 0) != 0 || one.kth(0, 1) != -1) throw new AssertionError("single node");
        // Random trees with unordered labels against the oracle for every node and every k.
        Random rnd = new Random(39);
        for (int trial = 0; trial < 400; trial++) {
            int n = 1 + rnd.nextInt(40);
            int[] order = new int[n];
            for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) order[i] = i;
            // Shuffle every label except the root 0, which stays first.
            for (int i = n - 1; i > 1; i--) {
                int j = 1 + rnd.nextInt(i);
                int tmp = order[i]; order[i] = order[j]; order[j] = tmp;
            }
            int[] parent = new int[n];
            parent[0] = -1;
            // Each later label attaches to an earlier label, so there is no cycle.
            for (int i = 1; i < n; i++) parent[order[i]] = order[rnd.nextInt(i)];
            TreeLifting t = new TreeLifting(n, parent);
            for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) {
                for (int k = 0; k <= n + 2; k++) {
                    if (t.kth(v, k) != oracle(parent, v, k)) throw new AssertionError("trial " + trial);
                }
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Variation

Counting Values In A Range

LeetCode 2080

Problem Specification. Design a class RangeValueCount. Its constructor receives an integer array arr. The method count(left, right, lo, hi) returns the number of indices i with left <= i <= right and lo <= arr[i] <= hi. Both pairs of bounds are inclusive, and lo > hi returns 0. The array never changes, so the workload has zero updates and many queries. The queries arrive one at a time and are not known in advance, so sorting them is not available.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • n is arr.length with 1 <= n <= 10^5.
  • Values are int values from -10^9 to 10^9, with duplicates allowed.
  • Indices satisfy 0 <= left <= right < n, and lo and hi are any int values.
  • Calls are one constructor call and up to 10^5 queries, and the array is not modified.

Example 1. Input arr = [5,1,5,9,2,5,9,1] and queries (0,7,1,5), (2,5,5,9), (3,3,10,20), (0,4,6,8), (1,6,2,2); output [6, 3, 0, 0, 1].

Example 2. Input arr = [-3,-3,0,7,-3] and queries (0,4,-3,-3), (1,3,-5,0), (0,4,1,6), (2,4,-10,10); output [3, 2, 0, 3].

Prerequisites. The Kth Ancestor exercise above and the sorted position lists of the lesson Query Planning. The structure is a merge-sort tree, a segment tree whose every node keeps the values of its index segment in sorted order. This exercise changes the contract of LeetCode 2080: a query counts every value inside a value range and not one fixed value.

What This Exercise Changes. The summary changes from one list of positions per value to a merge-sort tree with one sorted list of values per index segment. A query touches O(log n) segments and runs two binary searches in each, so it costs O(log^2 n) time, and the tree stores n log n entries.

Hint

One position list per value needs one search per distinct value in the range. A merge-sort tree keeps the values of each segment sorted. How many segments cover [left, right], and how many binary searches does one segment need?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. A position list per value answers a single value, but a value range would need one binary search per distinct value inside the range. The structure here keeps the positions together instead. The constructor builds a merge-sort tree over the index range, and each node stores the values of its index segment in sorted order. A query splits [left, right] into O(log n) nodes whose segments lie fully inside the range, and each such node counts values in [lo, hi] with two binary searches.

The invariant is that every node holds exactly the values of its segment, sorted, so the count inside a node is the difference of an upper bound and a lower bound. A merge of the two children builds the node in linear time, so each level of the tree costs O(n). The oracle scans the range for each query on random arrays with duplicates and negative values.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(n log n) for the constructor and O(log^2 n) per query, because a query visits O(log n) nodes and each node costs one binary search.
  • Space is O(n log n), because each of the log n levels stores all n values once.
import java.util.Random;

public final class ValueRangeSolution {
    static final class RangeValueCount {
        private final int[][] tree; // tree[node] holds the sorted values of the node's index segment
        private final int n;

        RangeValueCount(int[] arr) {
            n = arr.length;
            // A recursive layout of 4n nodes is enough for any length.
            tree = new int[4 * n][];
            build(1, 0, n - 1, arr);
        }

        /**
         * Builds the sorted list of one segment from its two children.
         * Time: O(n log n) over the whole tree. Space: O(n log n).
         * Invariant: tree[node] is the sorted multiset of arr[lo..hi].
         */
        private void build(int node, int lo, int hi, int[] arr) {
            // A leaf stores its single value.
            if (lo == hi) { tree[node] = new int[] {arr[lo]}; return; }
            int mid = (lo + hi) >>> 1;
            build(2 * node, lo, mid, arr);
            build(2 * node + 1, mid + 1, hi, arr);
            int[] a = tree[2 * node], b = tree[2 * node + 1], m = new int[a.length + b.length];
            int i = 0, j = 0, k = 0;
            // Standard merge of two sorted lists in linear time.
            while (i < a.length || j < b.length) {
                // Take from a when b is exhausted or a's head is not larger.
                m[k++] = (j == b.length || (i < a.length && a[i] <= b[j])) ? a[i++] : b[j++];
            }
            tree[node] = m;
        }

        /** Returns the count of indices in [left, right] with lo <= value <= hi. Time O(log^2 n). */
        int count(int left, int right, int lo, int hi) {
            // An empty value range matches nothing.
            if (lo > hi) return 0;
            return query(1, 0, n - 1, left, right, lo, hi);
        }

        /**
         * Sums the counts of the nodes that lie fully inside [left, right].
         * Time: O(log^2 n). Space: O(log n) recursion.
         * Invariant: each returned count covers exactly the part of the node segment inside the range.
         */
        private int query(int node, int s, int e, int left, int right, int lo, int hi) {
            // A segment outside the index range contributes nothing.
            if (right < s || e < left) return 0;
            // A segment fully inside is answered by two binary searches on its sorted list.
            if (left <= s && e <= right) return upper(tree[node], hi) - lower(tree[node], lo);
            int mid = (s + e) >>> 1;
            return query(2 * node, s, mid, left, right, lo, hi) + query(2 * node + 1, mid + 1, e, left, right, lo, hi);
        }

        /** First position whose value is at least x. */
        private static int lower(int[] a, int x) {
            int l = 0, r = a.length;
            // The invariant keeps every value before l below x.
            while (l < r) { int m = (l + r) >>> 1; if (a[m] < x) l = m + 1; else r = m; }
            return l;
        }

        /** First position whose value is greater than x. */
        private static int upper(int[] a, int x) {
            int l = 0, r = a.length;
            // The invariant keeps every value before l at most x.
            while (l < r) { int m = (l + r) >>> 1; if (a[m] <= x) l = m + 1; else r = m; }
            return l;
        }
    }

    /** Brute force: scans the index range and tests each value. */
    static int oracle(int[] arr, int left, int right, int lo, int hi) {
        int c = 0;
        // Each entry in the range is tested against both value bounds.
        for (int i = left; i <= right; i++) if (arr[i] >= lo && arr[i] <= hi) c++;
        return c;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: duplicates, a value range spanning several values, and a miss.
        RangeValueCount r1 = new RangeValueCount(new int[] {5, 1, 5, 9, 2, 5, 9, 1});
        int[][] q1 = {{0, 7, 1, 5}, {2, 5, 5, 9}, {3, 3, 10, 20}, {0, 4, 6, 8}, {1, 6, 2, 2}};
        int[] e1 = {6, 3, 0, 0, 1};
        for (int i = 0; i < q1.length; i++) {
            if (r1.count(q1[i][0], q1[i][1], q1[i][2], q1[i][3]) != e1[i]) throw new AssertionError("example 1 query " + i);
        }
        // Example 2: negative values, repeated values and the full int range.
        RangeValueCount r2 = new RangeValueCount(new int[] {-3, -3, 0, 7, -3});
        if (r2.count(0, 4, -3, -3) != 3 || r2.count(1, 3, -5, 0) != 2 || r2.count(0, 4, 1, 6) != 0
                || r2.count(2, 4, -10, 10) != 3) throw new AssertionError("example 2");
        // Extreme bounds and a reversed value range.
        if (r2.count(0, 4, Integer.MIN_VALUE, Integer.MAX_VALUE) != 5 || r2.count(0, 4, 5, 1) != 0) {
            throw new AssertionError("bounds");
        }
        // Random arrays against the oracle on random index and value ranges.
        Random rnd = new Random(392);
        for (int trial = 0; trial < 500; trial++) {
            int n = 1 + rnd.nextInt(30);
            int[] arr = new int[n];
            for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) arr[i] = rnd.nextInt(15) - 7;
            RangeValueCount t = new RangeValueCount(arr);
            for (int q = 0; q < 40; q++) {
                int a = rnd.nextInt(n), b = rnd.nextInt(n), l = Math.min(a, b), r = Math.max(a, b);
                int lo = rnd.nextInt(17) - 8, hi = lo + rnd.nextInt(9) - 1;
                if (t.count(l, r, lo, hi) != oracle(arr, l, r, lo, hi)) throw new AssertionError("trial " + trial);
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Edge Cases

Limit Queries On A Growing Graph

LeetCode 1724

Problem Specification. Design a class GrowingPaths. Its constructor receives n vertices numbered 0 to n - 1 and an initial list of undirected edges {u, v, w}. The method addEdge(u, v, w) inserts one edge. The method query(p, q, limit) returns true when a path from p to q exists that uses only edges with weight strictly less than limit, and false otherwise. The method rebuildCount() returns how many times the index was rebuilt after the constructor. The index rebuilds exactly once inside a query call whenever an addEdge since the previous rebuild, made while the index was not stale, either joined two different trees of the minimum spanning forest or was strictly lighter than the heaviest forest edge between its endpoints. An insertion made while the index is already stale only stores the edge. The workload mixes insertions and queries in any order, and calls arrive one at a time.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • n satisfies 1 <= n <= 10^4, with up to 10^5 edges in total and up to 10^5 calls.
  • Weights and limits are int values from 1 to 10^9, and self-loops and parallel edges may appear.
  • Vertices satisfy 0 <= p, q, u, v < n, and a query with p == q returns true.
  • Constructor build does not count in rebuildCount().

Example 1. Input n = 5, initial edges {0,1,3}, {1,2,6}, then calls query(0,2,7), addEdge(0,2,9), query(0,2,6), addEdge(0,2,4), addEdge(3,4,2), query(0,2,5), query(3,4,3), addEdge(0,1,3), query(0,2,5), query(2,3,100); output: the query results are true, false, true, true, true, false and rebuildCount() is 1.

Example 2. Input n = 4, no initial edges, then calls query(0,1,5), addEdge(0,1,4), addEdge(0,1,4), addEdge(0,1,2), addEdge(1,1,1), query(0,1,3), query(0,1,2), addEdge(2,3,8), addEdge(1,2,1), query(0,3,9), query(0,3,8); output: the query results are false, true, false, true, false and rebuildCount() is 2.

Prerequisites. The Vary exercise above, the minimum spanning forest from the lesson Query Planning and the stale mark from this lesson. This exercise changes the contract of LeetCode 1724: edges can be added after construction, and the answers must always reflect every edge added so far.

What This Exercise Changes. The static forest becomes a forest that is rebuilt on demand. An edge that cannot lower any path bottleneck never triggers a rebuild, and a burst of damaging edges pays for one rebuild.

Hint

Which value on the tree route between two vertices decides every limit query, and which new edges leave that value unchanged?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The index is the minimum spanning forest of the edges seen so far, rooted per tree, with a jump table that stores for each node the ancestor and the maximum edge weight along the jump. A query asks whether the maximum weight on the tree route between two nodes is below the limit, which equals the existence of a path with all edges below the limit. The forest is a minimum bottleneck structure, so the forest path minimizes its heaviest edge over all graph paths.

An inserted edge changes that index only in two cases. The endpoints are in different trees, or the heaviest edge on their current tree path is strictly heavier than the new edge. In every other case the forest stays valid, so the insertion costs one path query and no rebuild. When the edge does change the forest, the index is marked stale. Later insertions then append to the edge list without any check, because one rebuild covers all of them. The rebuild runs inside the next query, at most once per query, and it clears the stale mark.

The invariant is that when the stale mark is clear, the stored forest is a minimum spanning forest of all edges, and when it is set, the edge list still holds every edge so a rebuild restores the first case. The oracle answers each query with a union-find over all edges below the limit. A second oracle model decides staleness by recomputing the total forest weight and the component count with Kruskal, which does not use the jump table.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(E log E) per rebuild, O(log n) per query without a rebuild and O(log n) per insertion that checks the index.
  • Space is O(E + n log n) for the edge list and the jump tables.
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.Arrays;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.Random;

public final class GrowingLimitedPathsSolution {
    static final class GrowingPaths {
        private final int n;
        private final List<int[]> edges = new ArrayList<>(); // {u, v, w}, every edge ever inserted
        private final int rows;
        private int[][] up, heavy; // up[j][v]: 2^j-th ancestor in the forest; heavy[j][v]: heaviest edge on that jump
        private int[] depth, comp;
        private boolean stale;
        private int rebuilds;

        GrowingPaths(int n, int[][] initial) {
            this.n = n;
            int r = 1;
            // Enough rows for the longest possible tree path.
            while ((1 << r) < Math.max(2, n)) r++;
            rows = r;
            for (int[] e : initial) edges.add(e.clone());
            build(); // the constructor build is not counted in rebuilds
        }

        /**
         * Rebuilds the forest and the jump tables from the full edge list.
         * Time: O(E log E + n log n). Space: O(E + n log n).
         * Invariant: afterwards the tables describe a minimum spanning forest of every stored edge.
         */
        private void build() {
            int[][] sorted = edges.toArray(new int[0][]);
            // Kruskal needs the edges by weight.
            Arrays.sort(sorted, (x, y) -> Integer.compare(x[2], y[2]));
            int[] dsu = new int[n];
            for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) dsu[i] = i;
            List<List<int[]>> adj = new ArrayList<>();
            for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) adj.add(new ArrayList<>());
            for (int[] e : sorted) {
                int a = find(dsu, e[0]), b = find(dsu, e[1]);
                // Keep an edge only when it joins two different trees.
                if (a != b) {
                    dsu[a] = b;
                    adj.get(e[0]).add(new int[] {e[1], e[2]});
                    adj.get(e[1]).add(new int[] {e[0], e[2]});
                }
            }
            up = new int[rows][n];
            heavy = new int[rows][n];
            depth = new int[n];
            comp = new int[n];
            Arrays.fill(comp, -1);
            // Root every tree at its smallest unvisited node with an iterative walk.
            for (int root = 0; root < n; root++) {
                if (comp[root] != -1) continue;
                comp[root] = root;
                up[0][root] = root;
                heavy[0][root] = 0;
                List<Integer> stack = new ArrayList<>();
                stack.add(root);
                while (!stack.isEmpty()) {
                    int u = stack.remove(stack.size() - 1);
                    for (int[] nb : adj.get(u)) {
                        if (comp[nb[0]] != -1) continue; // already placed, so this is the parent side
                        comp[nb[0]] = root;
                        depth[nb[0]] = depth[u] + 1;
                        up[0][nb[0]] = u;
                        heavy[0][nb[0]] = nb[1];
                        stack.add(nb[0]);
                    }
                }
            }
            // Each row doubles the jump and keeps the heavier of the two halves.
            for (int j = 1; j < rows; j++) {
                for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) {
                    int mid = up[j - 1][v];
                    up[j][v] = up[j - 1][mid];
                    heavy[j][v] = Math.max(heavy[j - 1][v], heavy[j - 1][mid]);
                }
            }
        }

        private static int find(int[] d, int x) {
            // Path halving keeps the finds short.
            while (d[x] != x) { d[x] = d[d[x]]; x = d[x]; }
            return x;
        }

        /** Heaviest edge on the forest path between two nodes of one tree; 0 when they are equal. */
        private int pathMax(int a, int b) {
            if (depth[a] < depth[b]) { int t = a; a = b; b = t; }
            int best = 0, diff = depth[a] - depth[b];
            // Lift the deeper node by the depth difference, one set bit at a time.
            for (int j = 0; diff >> j != 0; j++) {
                if ((diff >> j & 1) == 1) { best = Math.max(best, heavy[j][a]); a = up[j][a]; }
            }
            if (a == b) return best;
            // Lift both nodes together while their ancestors differ.
            for (int j = rows - 1; j >= 0; j--) {
                if (up[j][a] != up[j][b]) {
                    best = Math.max(best, Math.max(heavy[j][a], heavy[j][b]));
                    a = up[j][a];
                    b = up[j][b];
                }
            }
            // One last step on each side reaches the common parent.
            return Math.max(best, Math.max(heavy[0][a], heavy[0][b]));
        }

        /**
         * Stores an edge and marks the index stale only when the edge changes the forest.
         * Time: O(log n) when the index is fresh, O(1) when it is already stale.
         * Invariant: a clear stale mark means the tables match all stored edges.
         */
        void addEdge(int u, int v, int w) {
            edges.add(new int[] {u, v, w});
            // A stale index is rebuilt anyway, so no check is needed.
            if (stale) return;
            // Different trees, or a heavier path, means the forest must change.
            if (comp[u] != comp[v] || pathMax(u, v) > w) stale = true;
        }

        /** Returns whether a path from p to q uses only edges with weight below limit. */
        boolean query(int p, int q, int limit) {
            // The first query after a forest change pays for one rebuild.
            if (stale) { build(); stale = false; rebuilds++; }
            // Nodes in different trees have no path at all.
            if (comp[p] != comp[q]) return false;
            return pathMax(p, q) < limit;
        }

        int rebuildCount() { return rebuilds; }
    }

    /** Oracle: union-find over all edges below the limit. */
    static boolean oracle(int n, List<int[]> edges, int p, int q, int limit) {
        int[] d = new int[n];
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) d[i] = i;
        // Every light edge joins two sets.
        for (int[] e : edges) if (e[2] < limit) d[find(d, e[0])] = find(d, e[1]);
        return find(d, p) == find(d, q);
    }

    private static int find(int[] d, int x) {
        while (d[x] != x) { d[x] = d[d[x]]; x = d[x]; }
        return x;
    }

    /** Kruskal summary {components, total weight}; it equals the summary before an insertion exactly when the forest stays valid. */
    static long[] summary(int n, List<int[]> edges) {
        int[][] s = edges.toArray(new int[0][]);
        Arrays.sort(s, (x, y) -> Integer.compare(x[2], y[2]));
        int[] d = new int[n];
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) d[i] = i;
        long comps = n, total = 0;
        // Count joins and their weights.
        for (int[] e : s) {
            int a = find(d, e[0]), b = find(d, e[1]);
            if (a != b) { d[a] = b; comps--; total += e[2]; }
        }
        return new long[] {comps, total};
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: a rebuild after a useful edge, and no rebuild for useless edges.
        GrowingPaths g = new GrowingPaths(5, new int[][] {{0, 1, 3}, {1, 2, 6}});
        boolean[] got = new boolean[10];
        got[0] = g.query(0, 2, 7);
        g.addEdge(0, 2, 9);
        got[2] = g.query(0, 2, 6);
        g.addEdge(0, 2, 4);
        g.addEdge(3, 4, 2);
        got[5] = g.query(0, 2, 5);
        got[6] = g.query(3, 4, 3);
        g.addEdge(0, 1, 3);
        got[8] = g.query(0, 2, 5);
        got[9] = g.query(2, 3, 100);
        boolean[] exp = {true, false, false, false, false, true, true, false, true, false};
        for (int i : new int[] {0, 2, 5, 6, 8, 9}) if (got[i] != exp[i]) throw new AssertionError("example 1 call " + i);
        if (g.rebuildCount() != 1) throw new AssertionError("example 1 rebuilds " + g.rebuildCount());
        // Example 2: an empty start, stale insertions that skip the check, and two rebuilds.
        GrowingPaths h = new GrowingPaths(4, new int[0][]);
        if (h.query(0, 1, 5)) throw new AssertionError("example 2 first");
        h.addEdge(0, 1, 4);
        h.addEdge(0, 1, 4);
        h.addEdge(0, 1, 2);
        h.addEdge(1, 1, 1);
        if (!h.query(0, 1, 3) || h.query(0, 1, 2)) throw new AssertionError("example 2 limits");
        h.addEdge(2, 3, 8);
        h.addEdge(1, 2, 1);
        if (!h.query(0, 3, 9) || h.query(0, 3, 8)) throw new AssertionError("example 2 chain");
        if (h.rebuildCount() != 2) throw new AssertionError("example 2 rebuilds " + h.rebuildCount());
        // Random workloads: answers against union-find, rebuilds against the Kruskal summary model.
        Random rnd = new Random(1724);
        for (int trial = 0; trial < 600; trial++) {
            int n = 1 + rnd.nextInt(8);
            List<int[]> all = new ArrayList<>();
            int init = rnd.nextInt(5);
            int[][] first = new int[init][];
            for (int i = 0; i < init; i++) {
                first[i] = new int[] {rnd.nextInt(n), rnd.nextInt(n), 1 + rnd.nextInt(9)};
                all.add(first[i]);
            }
            GrowingPaths t = new GrowingPaths(n, first);
            boolean modelStale = false;
            int modelRebuilds = 0;
            long[] sum = summary(n, all);
            for (int step = 0; step < 30; step++) {
                if (rnd.nextInt(2) == 0) {
                    int[] e = {rnd.nextInt(n), rnd.nextInt(n), 1 + rnd.nextInt(9)};
                    all.add(e);
                    t.addEdge(e[0], e[1], e[2]);
                    long[] now = summary(n, all);
                    // The model marks stale only from a fresh state, exactly as the contract says.
                    if (!modelStale && (now[0] != sum[0] || now[1] != sum[1])) modelStale = true;
                    sum = now;
                } else {
                    int p = rnd.nextInt(n), q = rnd.nextInt(n), limit = 1 + rnd.nextInt(11);
                    // The model rebuilds once per stale query.
                    if (modelStale) { modelStale = false; modelRebuilds++; }
                    if (t.query(p, q, limit) != oracle(n, all, p, q, limit)) throw new AssertionError("trial " + trial);
                    if (t.rebuildCount() != modelRebuilds) throw new AssertionError("rebuild count in trial " + trial);
                }
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Pattern Recognition

Fixed Source Costs After Edge Decreases

LeetCode 2642

Problem Specification. Design a class SourceCosts. Its constructor receives n, a source vertex source and a list of directed edges {from, to, cost} with no parallel edges and no self-loops. The method lower(a, b, newCost) lowers the cost of the existing edge from a to b to newCost when newCost is smaller than its current cost, and returns true exactly when at least one shortest distance from the source gets smaller. The method cost(target) returns the minimum total cost of a directed path from the source to target, or -1 when none exists. The workload has many decreases and many queries in unknown order. Choose between an all-pairs table, a fresh search per query and one maintained distance array, and justify the choice by the update and query counts.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • n satisfies 1 <= n <= 10^3, and the edge count is at most 10^4.
  • Costs are int values from 0 to 10^9, and path totals use long.
  • lower throws IllegalArgumentException when the edge does not exist, and a larger or equal newCost changes nothing.
  • Calls are one constructor call and up to 10^5 calls to lower and cost combined.

Example 1. Input n = 4, source = 0, edges {0,1,6}, {1,2,5}, {2,3,2}, {0,2,20}, then the calls cost(3), lower(0,2,9), cost(3), lower(1,2,5), lower(0,2,30), lower(0,1,1), cost(3), lower(2,3,0), cost(3); output [13, true, 11, false, false, true, 8, true, 6].

Example 2. Input n = 4, source = 0, edges {1,2,5}, {2,3,2}, then the calls cost(1), lower(1,2,1), cost(3); output [-1, false, -1], because node 1 is unreachable.

Prerequisites. The Boundary exercise above and the incremental relaxation from the lesson Dynamic Graph Queries. This exercise keeps the theme of LeetCode 2642 but changes its contract: edges are only lowered and never added, a query asks for the cost from one fixed source, and lower reports whether any distance improved. All queries start at one source, and an update only lowers the cost of an existing edge.

What This Exercise Changes. The index is a single distance array that a decrease repairs locally and a query reads in constant time. No stale mark exists, because the repair cost depends on the nodes that actually improve.

Hint

A decrease of the edge (a, b) can help a path only through b. When does the comparison of dist[a] + newCost with dist[b] already prove that nothing changes? When it does not, lower dist[b] and relax outward from b along its outgoing edges, visiting only the nodes whose distance improves.

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The workload has one fixed source, many cost decreases and queries that ask for one distance, with no future knowledge. An all-pairs table would cost n^2 memory and repair work that no query needs. A fresh search per query would cost O(m log n) for each of the many queries. The cheaper plan keeps one distance array from the source and makes the query a plain array read.

A decrease of edge (a, b) can only help paths that use that edge. If dist[a] + newCost is not below dist[b], no distance changes and the method returns after one comparison. Otherwise dist[b] drops, and the node b starts a Dijkstra-style relaxation that touches only nodes whose distance improves. Costs never rise, so a distance that has dropped never needs to rise again, and each improved node settles through the usual priority-queue rule.

The invariant is that after every call dist[v] is the true shortest cost from the source to v, or infinity. The relaxation works because all improvements stem from b, and every node whose shortest path changes lies behind b on its new path. The oracle runs Bellman-Ford from the source on the current edge costs after each update.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(m log n) in the worst case per decrease, O(1) when no distance changes, O(1) per query and O((n + m) log n) for the constructor.
  • Space is O(n + m) for the adjacency lists and the distance array.
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.Arrays;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.PriorityQueue;
import java.util.Random;

public final class SourceDecreaseSolution {
    static final class SourceCosts {
        private static final long INF = Long.MAX_VALUE / 4;
        private final int n;
        private final List<int[]>[] out; // out[a] holds {b, edgeId}
        private final long[] cost;       // cost[edgeId] is the current cost
        private final int[] from, to;
        private final long[] dist;

        @SuppressWarnings("unchecked")
        SourceCosts(int n, int source, int[][] edges) {
            this.n = n;
            out = new List[n];
            for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) out[i] = new ArrayList<>();
            cost = new long[edges.length];
            from = new int[edges.length];
            to = new int[edges.length];
            // Each edge keeps a stable id so a later decrease finds it by pair.
            for (int i = 0; i < edges.length; i++) {
                from[i] = edges[i][0]; to[i] = edges[i][1]; cost[i] = edges[i][2];
                out[from[i]].add(new int[] {to[i], i});
            }
            dist = new long[n];
            Arrays.fill(dist, INF);
            dist[source] = 0;
            PriorityQueue<long[]> pq = new PriorityQueue<>((x, y) -> Long.compare(x[0], y[0]));
            pq.add(new long[] {0, source});
            relax(pq); // the full search runs once
        }

        /**
         * Runs the queue until no stored distance can improve.
         * Time: O(k log n) for the k edges scanned. Space: O(n) queue entries.
         * Invariant: a popped entry equal to dist[u] has final edges relaxed from it.
         */
        private void relax(PriorityQueue<long[]> pq) {
            // Each pop either is current and relaxes its edges or is stale and is skipped.
            while (!pq.isEmpty()) {
                long[] top = pq.poll();
                int u = (int) top[1];
                if (top[0] > dist[u]) continue; // stale entry of a node that improved again
                for (int[] e : out[u]) {
                    long cand = dist[u] + cost[e[1]];
                    // A shorter route replaces the stored distance and re-enters the queue.
                    if (cand < dist[e[0]]) { dist[e[0]] = cand; pq.add(new long[] {cand, e[0]}); }
                }
            }
        }

        /**
         * Lowers the cost of the existing edge (a, b); returns true when a distance changes.
         * Time: O(1) without change, O(m log n) worst case. Space: O(n).
         * Invariant: dist equals the shortest costs for the current edge costs.
         */
        boolean lower(int a, int b, long newCost) {
            int id = -1;
            // Find the edge by its endpoints in the list of a.
            for (int[] e : out[a]) if (e[0] == b) id = e[1];
            if (id < 0) throw new IllegalArgumentException("no such edge");
            // A cost that is not smaller changes nothing.
            if (newCost >= cost[id]) return false;
            cost[id] = newCost;
            // An unreachable tail or a tail that gives no better route leaves all distances alone.
            if (dist[a] >= INF || dist[a] + newCost >= dist[b]) return false;
            dist[b] = dist[a] + newCost;
            PriorityQueue<long[]> pq = new PriorityQueue<>((x, y) -> Long.compare(x[0], y[0]));
            pq.add(new long[] {dist[b], b});
            relax(pq); // only nodes behind b can improve
            return true;
        }

        /** Returns the shortest cost from the source, or -1 when unreachable. */
        long cost(int target) {
            return dist[target] >= INF ? -1 : dist[target];
        }
    }

    /** Oracle: Bellman-Ford from the source on the current costs. */
    static long[] oracle(int n, int source, int[][] edges, long[] cur) {
        long inf = Long.MAX_VALUE / 4;
        long[] d = new long[n];
        Arrays.fill(d, inf);
        d[source] = 0;
        // n - 1 passes settle every shortest path with non-negative costs.
        for (int pass = 0; pass < n; pass++) {
            for (int i = 0; i < edges.length; i++) {
                if (d[edges[i][0]] < inf && d[edges[i][0]] + cur[i] < d[edges[i][1]]) d[edges[i][1]] = d[edges[i][0]] + cur[i];
            }
        }
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) if (d[i] >= inf) d[i] = -1;
        return d;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: a decrease that reroutes node 3, a no-gain decrease and a zero cost.
        SourceCosts s = new SourceCosts(4, 0, new int[][] {{0, 1, 6}, {1, 2, 5}, {2, 3, 2}, {0, 2, 20}});
        if (s.cost(3) != 13 || s.cost(2) != 11) throw new AssertionError("example 1 start");
        if (!s.lower(0, 2, 9) || s.cost(2) != 9 || s.cost(3) != 11) throw new AssertionError("example 1 step 1");
        if (s.lower(1, 2, 5)) throw new AssertionError("example 1 equal cost");
        if (s.lower(0, 2, 30)) throw new AssertionError("example 1 raise");
        if (!s.lower(0, 1, 1) || s.cost(2) != 6 || s.cost(3) != 8) throw new AssertionError("example 1 step 4");
        if (!s.lower(2, 3, 0) || s.cost(3) != 6) throw new AssertionError("example 1 zero");
        // Example 2: edges behind an unreachable node never change a distance.
        SourceCosts u = new SourceCosts(4, 0, new int[][] {{1, 2, 5}, {2, 3, 2}});
        if (u.cost(1) != -1 || u.lower(1, 2, 1) || u.cost(3) != -1 || u.cost(0) != 0) throw new AssertionError("example 2");
        // Random graphs without parallel edges against Bellman-Ford after every decrease.
        Random rnd = new Random(2642);
        for (int trial = 0; trial < 600; trial++) {
            int n = 2 + rnd.nextInt(7);
            List<int[]> list = new ArrayList<>();
            boolean[][] used = new boolean[n][n];
            for (int i = 0; i < n * 2; i++) {
                int a = rnd.nextInt(n), b = rnd.nextInt(n);
                if (a == b || used[a][b]) continue;
                used[a][b] = true;
                list.add(new int[] {a, b, rnd.nextInt(30)});
            }
            if (list.isEmpty()) continue;
            int[][] edges = list.toArray(new int[0][]);
            long[] cur = new long[edges.length];
            for (int i = 0; i < edges.length; i++) cur[i] = edges[i][2];
            int src = rnd.nextInt(n);
            SourceCosts t = new SourceCosts(n, src, edges);
            for (int step = 0; step < 15; step++) {
                int id = rnd.nextInt(edges.length);
                long nc = rnd.nextInt(30);
                long[] before = oracle(n, src, edges, cur);
                if (nc < cur[id]) cur[id] = nc;
                long[] after = oracle(n, src, edges, cur);
                boolean changed = !Arrays.equals(before, after);
                // The returned flag must equal whether any distance changed.
                if (t.lower(edges[id][0], edges[id][1], nc) != changed) throw new AssertionError("flag in trial " + trial);
                for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) if (t.cost(v) != after[v]) throw new AssertionError("distance in trial " + trial);
            }
        }
    }
}verified

Review

A prefix sum that ignores a correction, two touching ranges that stay split, and a division by a multiplier of zero all pass small tests. The ten questions below cover all eight lessons. Try them after the lessons and again after a few days, and commit to an answer before opening the explanation.
